Anosov flows in dimension 3 from gluing building blocks with quasi-transverse boundary
Neige Paulet

Abstract
. We prove a new result allowing to construct Anosov flows in dimension 3 by gluing building blocks . By a building block, we mean a compact 3-manifold with boundary P P , equipped with a 𝒞 1 {\mathcal{C}}^{1} vector field X X , such that the maximal invariant set ∩ t ∈ ℝ X t ( P ) \cap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}X^{t}(P) is a saddle hyperbolic set, and the boundary ∂ P \partial P is quasi-transverse to X X , i.e. transverse except for a finite number of periodic orbits contained in ∂ P \partial P . Our gluing theorem is a generalization of a recent result of F. Béguin, C. Bonatti, and B. Yu who only considered the case where the block does not contain attractors nor repellers, and the boundary ∂ P {\partial P} is transverse to X X . The quasi-transverse setting is much more natural. Indeed, our result can be seen as a counterpart of a theorem by Barbot and Fenley which roughly states that every 3-dimensional Anosov flow admits a canonical decomposition into building blocks (with quasi-transverse boundary). We will also show a number of applications of our theorem.



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification
37D20, 37D05, 37C10, 57K30, 57R30

Introduction

The definition of Anosov flows appeared in the 1960’s, when D. Anosov studied the qualitative dynamical properties of the geodesic flow on Riemannian manifolds with negative curvature in his famous article
[
Ano67
]
, which became the prototype example of an Anosov flow.
The understanding of Anosov flows, together with their generalizations and their relationships to other fields and structures, has led to many discoveries in dynamical systems, geometry and geometric topology.
As Anosov flows are structurally stable, one can hope to obtain a complete classification of their orbital equivalence classes on a given manifold by a finite number of combinatorial invariants.
Such a classification in dimension 3 (the minimal dimension for an Anosov flow) is still today the motivation of many works.
They revealed remarkable interactions between the dynamics of the flow and the topology of the underlying manifold.
For certain classes of 3-manifolds (torus bundles over the circle, or Seifert manifolds for example), the topology of the manifold almost completely determines the dynamics of the Anosov flow it can carry, up to orbital equivalence.
On the other hand, there are 3-manifolds which carry several non-orbit equivalent Anosov flows.
The main object of this paper is the construction of new examples of Anosov flows in 3-manifolds.

Previous constructions of Anosov flows

The two standard examples of Anosov flows are geodesic flows of a negatively curved manifolds and suspensions of hyperbolic toral automorphisms.
These were the only examples of Anosov flows for decades.
These two flows can also be described as the action of a one-parameter subgroup of a Lie group
G
, acting on a quotient of
G
by a co-compact lattice.
Such a flow is called an
algebraic flow
.
In the 1980’s the first examples of non-algebraic Anosov flows appeared.
These examples show that the problem of classification of Anosov flows in dimension 3 cannot be easily reduced, because of
flexibility
phenomena: there are many Anosov flows which do not have the qualities common to algebraic flows, and there are manifolds carrying many Anosov flows whose dynamics are not equivalent, or even very distant.

•

J. Franks and B. Williams construct in
[
FW80
]
the first non-transitive Anosov flow in dimension 3 by the
Blow up – Excise – Glue
surgery.

•

M. Handel and W.P. Thurston construct in
[
HT80
]
the first non-algebraic transitive Anosov flow by performing a
cut-and-paste
surgery on the geodesic flow of a hyperbolic surface.

•

C. Bonatti and R. Langevin construct in
[
BL94
]
the first example of transitive Anosov flow, transverse to an embedded torus, but not equivalent to a suspension.
The two previous examples are obtained by gluing the boundary components of building blocks that have been
cut out of a (blown-up) Anosov flow
.
The Bonatti-Langevin example is the first Anosov flow constructed from a building block which is not obtained by surgery on an initial standard Anosov flow.
T. Barbot generalizes this construction in
[
Bar98
]
.

•

F. Béguin, C. Bonatti, and B. Yu have developed a general procedure for constructing Anosov flows by gluing “abstract building blocks” in
[
BBY17
]
, in the spirit of the Bonatti-Langevin construction but where the manifold and the dynamics are not explicit.
A
Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block
is a pair
(P,X)
where
P
is a compact manifold with boundary equipped with a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
vector field
X
, transverse to
\partial P
, and such that the maximal invariant set
\cap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}X^{t}(P)
forms a hyperbolic set with one-dimensional strong stable and unstable bundles.
The authors show that under very general conditions, there is a way to glue the boundary components of
P
via a diffeomorphism
\varphi\colon\partial P\to\partial P
which pairs the components of the
exit boundary
(along which the flow exits
P
) with the components of the
entrance boundary
(along which the flow enters
P
) to obtain a closed manifold
P_{\varphi}:=P/\varphi
equipped with a vector field
X_{\varphi}
induced by
X
which is Anosov.

The gluing procedure of Béguin-Bonatti-Yu is a powerful technique to show the flexibility of Anosov flows, and allows to build Anosov dynamics on manifolds with a rich and complicated topology.
They construct for example the first family of manifolds
{\mathcal{M}}_{N}
carrying
N
pairwise non-orbit equivalent Anosov flows for every integer
N
.
However for Anosov flows constructed with this procedure, there always exist embedded tori which are transverse to the flow.
The existence of such tori is not a common property.
For example, the unit tangent bundle of a closed hyperbolic surface contain plenty of incompressible tori, but one can easily prove that none of these is isotopic to a torus transverse to the geodesic flow.

Decomposition of an Anosov flow: the modified JSJ decomposition

The counterpart of the Béguin-Bonatti-Yu gluing procedure is to find a good way to decompose a given Anosov vector field
Z
on a closed orientable 3-manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
along tori into building blocks
(P_{i},X_{i})
.

The Jaco-Shalen-Johannson (JSJ) theorem states that any irreducible connected orientable closed manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
of dimension 3 can be cut along a minimal finite collection, unique up to isotopy, of incompressible tori
{\mathcal{T}}=\{T_{1},\dots,T_{n}\}
into pieces
P_{1},\dots,P_{m}
such that each
P_{i}
is either atoroidal or admits a Seifert fibration.
Any 3-manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
which carries an Anosov vector field
X
is irreducible (since its universal covering is
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
), and thus admits a JSJ-decomposition along incompressible tori.
In
[
BF13
]
, the authors study in detail the “optimal” position of an incompressible torus
T
embedded in an orientable manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
with respect to the Anosov vector field
X
on
{\mathcal{M}}
.
We will say that the torus
T
is
quasi-transverse
to
X
if
T
contains a finite (possibly zero) number of periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}=\{{\mathcal{O}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{O}}_{n}\}
of the flow,
it is transverse to
X
on the complementary of the orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, and the transverse orientation given by the vector field
X
on two adjacent components of
T{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
never coincide.

T. Barbot and S. Fenley show (
[
BF13
, Theorem 6.10]
) that any incompressible torus embedded in a 3-manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
carrying an Anosov vector field
X
is homotopic to a torus quasi-transverse to
X
and
weakly embedded
(embedded outside the periodic orbits of
X
contained in the torus).
This result gives a
modified JSJ decomposition
of an Anosov flow (see
[
BF17
, Section 2.2]
for a precise statement) which is unique up to homotopy along the flow, and only depends on the topology of the underlying manifold.
With the help of this decomposition, S. Fenley and T. Barbot have started the intensive study of Anosov flows on toroidal 3-manifolds (
[
BF13
]
) and their classification in restriction to JSJ Seifert pieces and to some graph manifolds (
[
BF15
,
BF21
,
BF22
]
).
Let us stress that Anosov flows on atoroidal manifolds or in restriction to atoroidal pieces of the JSJ decomposition are still very poorly understood.

In conclusion, up to some technical details, any Anosov flow in a toroidal manifold can be canonically decomposed into blocks
(P_{i},X_{i})
where
P_{i}
is a manifold with boundary, and
X_{i}
is a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
vector field on
P_{i}
which is quasi-transverse to the boundary
\partial P_{i}
.
It is therefore natural to try to generalize the Béguin-Bonatti-Yu construction for such “quasi-transverse building blocks”.
This is the goal of this paper.

The Gluing Theorem

In our setting, a
building block

(P,X)
is a compact 3-manifold
P
with boundary equipped with a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
vector field
X
, such that the maximal invariant set
\Lambda
of the generated flow is (saddle) hyperbolic, and whose boundary
{\partial P}
is
quasi-transverse
to the vector field.
We provide very general sufficient conditions to glue the boundary components of building blocks, so that the resulting manifold is closed and equipped with an Anosov flow induced by the initial vector field.
A key element is the one-dimensional lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
induced by the trace of the stable and unstable manifold of
\Lambda
on the boundary
{\partial P}
, and its image by a
gluing map

\varphi:{\partial P}\to{\partial P}
.
We say that the block is
filled
if
{\mathcal{L}}
is a
filling lamination
(i.e. it extends to a foliation on
{\partial P}
with a technical additive assumption) and that the gluing map is
strongly quasi-transverse
if the pair of laminations
({\mathcal{L}},\varphi_{*}{\mathcal{L}})
extend to a pair of foliation transverse except along the boundary orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
where they coincide.
These assumption (except the technical one) are necessary to obtain an Anosov flow by such gluing procedure.
To do so, we want to perturb the initial vector field and the boundary of the block by isotopy, and to perturb the initial gluing map by isotopy so as to preserve the “drawing” of the transverse intersection of the pair of laminations
({\mathcal{L}},\varphi_{*}{\mathcal{L}})
.
We will say that such perturbation is a
strong isotopy of the triple

(P,X,\varphi)
.

Théorème A

(Gluing Theorem, Theorem
1
)
.

Let
(P,X)
be a filled building block, and
\varphi
be a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map of
(P,X)
.
There exists a triple
(P_{1},X_{1},\varphi_{1})
strongly isotopic to
(P,X,\varphi)
such that

X_{1}
induces an Anosov vector field on the closed 3-manifold
P_{1}/\varphi_{1}
.

This theorem is the analog of the Béguin-Bonatti-Yu gluing theorem (
[
BBY17
, Theorem 1.5]
) in the case where the set
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
of periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
is empty, and extends it to blocks containing attractors or repellers.
Moreover it includes the Franks-Williams surgery, the Handel-Thurston surgeries and their generalizations.
Finally, it allows to consider the most natural building blocks for Anosov flows in dimension 3, in the sense that the quasi-transverse position is the “optimal” position of an incompressible closed surface embedded in an Anosov flow according to the work of T. Barbot and S. Fenley mentioned above.

Transitivity criterion

We show a transitivity criterion of an Anosov vector field
X_{\varphi}
on a closed manifold
P_{\varphi}
obtained by gluing a building block
(P,X)
by a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map
\varphi
.
We associate an oriented graph
G=G(P,X,\varphi)
, analogous to the Smale’s graph in the following way.
The vertices are the basic pieces
\Lambda_{i}
of the maximal hyperbolic invariant set
\Lambda
of
(P,X)
, and there exists a directed edge from
\Lambda_{i}
to
\Lambda_{j}
if and only if
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(\Lambda_{i})
intersects
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(\Lambda_{j})
, or
\varphi({\mathcal{W}}^{u}(\Lambda_{i}))
intersects
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(\Lambda_{j})
.

Proposition B

(Proposition
6.4.2
)
.

If the graph
G(P,X,\varphi)
is strongly connected, then the Anosov vector field
X_{\varphi}
on
P_{\varphi}
is transitive.

Applications of the Gluing Theorem

We show a number of application of Theorem
A
.

Realizing bi-foliation in Anosov flows

As first application, we prove that any type of
quasi-transverse bi-foliation
on a torus can be realized as the trace of the stable and unstable foliation of a transitive Anosov flow on an embedded quasi-transverse torus.
If
(P,X)
is a building block, then the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
of
{\partial P}
is a generalization of a
Morse-Smale type
lamination on a surface, called
quasi-Morse-Smale
.
It contains a finite number of compact leaves, and each half non-compact leaf accumulates on a compact leaf.
Among the compact leaves, we distinguish the compact leaves induced by the periodic orbits of
X
in
{\partial P}
, which we call
marked compact leaves
.
If
({\mathcal{F}}_{1},{\mathcal{F}}_{2})
is a pair of quasi-Morse-Smale foliations on the oriented torus
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
, such that
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
are tangent along their marked leaves and transverse to each other on the complementary of the marked leaves, they are said to be
quasi-transverse
.
Then we can associate to the pair a finite combinatorial data
\sigma=\sigma({\mathcal{F}}_{1},{\mathcal{F}}_{2})
, which encodes the (cyclic) order of the compact leaves of
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
, and the “type” of holonomy of each one.
We say that
\sigma
is a
combinatorial type of the bifoliation
.
We prove

Théorème C

(Theorem
2
)
.

Let
\sigma
be a combinatorial type of a quasi-transverse bifoliation.
There exists a transitive Anosov vector field
Z
on an oriented
3
-manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
and an incompressible torus
T
embedded in
{\mathcal{M}}
, quasi-transverse to
Z
, such that the trace of the stable and unstable foliation
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{u}
on
T
induces a bifoliation
({\mathcal{F}}_{1},{\mathcal{F}}_{2})
on
T
of combinatorial type
\sigma
.

Embedding block in Anosov flows

We prove that any filled building block can be embedded in an Anosov flow.

Théorème D

(Theorem
3
)
.

For any (transitive) orientable filled block
(P,X)
, there exists a (transitive) Anosov vector field
Z
on a closed orientable 3-manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
, such that
(P,X)
is embedded in
({\mathcal{M}},Z)
.
More precisely, there exists a finite collection of incompressible tori
{\mathcal{T}}
embedded in
{\mathcal{M}}
, quasi-transverse to
Z
, such that the closure of one connected component of
{\mathcal{M}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{T}}
is a compact submanifold diffeomorphic to
P
and such that the restriction of
Z
on
P
is orbit equivalent to
X
.

Realizing geometric type in building blocks

We study the dynamical properties of building blocks, giving necessary and sufficient criteria for an abstract geometric type to be realized on a Markov partition in a nice building block.
The
geometric type of a Markov partition
, introduced by C. Bonatti and R. Langevin, is a finite combinatorial data
{\mathscr{T}}
which encodes the geometry of the intersection of the rectangles of a Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}
under the action of the dynamic.
F. Béguin and C. Bonatti show (
[
BB02
]
) that this data characterizes the
germ
of a vector field
X
on a
saddle saturated
hyperbolic compact invariant set
K
.
They show that the germ of
X
along
K
determines a unique (up to orbit equivalence) orientable manifold with boundary
U
, provided with a vector field
Y
transverse to the boundary
\partial U
, which realizes the germ of
X
along
K
, and this manifold is in a sense the “simplest” topologically speaking.
The pair
(U,Y)
is a building block, and one will then speak of
model block
.
Béguin, Bonatti and Veitez construct in
[
BBV99
]
, for a given abstract geometric type
{\mathscr{T}}
, a model block
(U,Y)
whose maximal invariant admits a Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}
of geometric type
{\mathscr{T}}
, which is unique up to orbit equivalence.
We then speak of
the model of the geometric type
{\mathscr{T}}
.
We are interested in the question of the realizability of an abstract geometrical type
{\mathscr{T}}
by a filled building block.
We show the following necessary and sufficient criterion:

Théorème E

(Theorem
4
)
.

Let
{\mathscr{T}}
be an abstract geometric type.
There exists an orientable filled block
(P,X)
, admitting a Markov partition of geometric type
{\mathscr{T}}
if and only if the model
(U,Y)
of
{\mathscr{T}}
satisfies the following conditions;

(1)

\partial U
is a union of tori and spheres, each sphere contains two open disks
D_{i}
and
D_{j}
, disjoint from the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
, bounded by two distinct compact leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
;

(2)

{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
is a pre-foliation on the complementary
\partial U{\smallsetminus}\cup_{i}D_{i}
.

Such a block
(P,X)
is then unique up to isotopy.

The two conditions can be checked using a simple algorithm for attaching the rectangles of the associated Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}
, which allows to compute the boundary of the model
U
and the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
.

Orbit complement as JSJ pieces of Anosov flows

We show that one can realize periodic orbits complement of transitive (pseudo)-Anosov flow as JSJ piece of transitive Anosov flow.
A
pseudo-Anosov flow
is a generalization of an Anosov flow where we allow a finite number of singularities of stable and unstable foliations of
p
-prong type,
p\geq 3
.

Théorème F

(Theorem
5
)
.

Let
\Gamma=\{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\}
be a finite collection of periodic orbits of a transitive pseudo-Anosov vector field
X
on an orientable 3-manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Assume that all the singular orbits of
X
are contained in
\Gamma
and that the complementary
{\mathcal{M}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma
is atoroidal.
Then there exists an orientable 3-manifold
{\mathcal{N}}
carrying a transitive Anosov vector field
Y
such that the JSJ decomposition of
{\mathcal{N}}
is made of two atoroidal pieces
P
and
P^{\prime}
, both homeomorphic to
{\mathcal{M}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma
, and a periodic Seifert piece.
The restriction of
Y
to
P
and
P^{\prime}
is obtained from
X
by a DA
1
1

1

Derived from Anosov
bifurcation on the orbits of
\Gamma
.

A Seifert piece in a 3-manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
carrying an Anosov vector field
X
is said to be
periodic
if there exists a Seifert fibration for which the regular fiber is homotopic to a power of a periodic orbit of the flow of
X
.
Those pieces are well understood from works of Barbot and Fenley (
[
BF15
]
).

As a corollary of this theorem, we obtain sufficient conditions for the complementary of a
hyperbolic knot
in
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
to be realized as an atoroidal JSJ piece of a transitive Anosov flow.
Here is an example of such knots.

Théorème G

(Corollary
10.4.3
)
.

Let
K=\partial(S_{1}\#S_{2}\#\dots\#S_{n})
be a plumbing of
n
copies of the Seifert surface of the figure eight knot.
Then
K
is a hyperbolic fibered knot and the complementary of
K
is an atoroidal JSJ piece of a manifold carrying a transitive Anosov flow.

Gluing pieces of skewed Anosov flows

We show that, under minimal conditions, one can glue pieces cut along a collection of incompressible tori embedded in a skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov flow.
An Anosov flow on a 3-manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
is said to be
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered
if the leaf space of the lifted stable foliations
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{s}
on the universal cover
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
is separated (hence homeomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}
).
It is said to be
skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered
if it is moreover not orbit equivalent to a suspension.
A result of Barbot (
[
Bar95
]
) allows us to cut building blocks out of a a skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov flow along a collection of incompressible tori.
We call such piece a
skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov block
.

Théorème H

(Theorem
6
)
.

Let
(P,X)
and
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
be two skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov blocks and
\varphi\colon{\partial P}\to{\partial P}^{\prime}
a gluing map.
There is a gluing map
\psi
isotopic to
\varphi
among gluing maps such that the vector field
Z
induced by
X
and
X^{\prime}
on
P\cup P^{\prime}/\psi
is Anosov.

Note that this statement does not require any assumption on the action of the gluing map on the boundary laminations.
Any
piece of geodesic flow
or finite covering of geodesic flow is a skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov block.
Hence Theorem
H
generalizes the Handel-Thurston construction and their generalization and gets rid of the
positivity constraint
on the isotopy class of the gluing map.
Note, however, that it allows to use blocks that are much more general than geodesic flow pieces, and that are not
a priori
cut out from the same Anosov flow, inside the very rich family of skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov flows.

Structure of the paper

The paper is organized as follows.
The first part is devoted to the proof of Theorem
A
.
In the preliminary Section
1
, we formally define building blocks, gluing maps, and fundamental properties.
In Section
2
, we show that we can put a triple
(P,X,\varphi)
candidate for Theorem
A
in a “normalized” form.
In Section
3
, we study the hyperbolic properties of the crossing map of the flow from the entrance boundary to the exit boundary of the block.
In Section
4
, we show how to “spread hyperbolicity” by a process of coordinate change on the boundary of
P
.
In Section
5
, we show that we can use this change of coordinates to modify the gluing map
\varphi
in order to create hyperbolicity along the new recurrent orbits of the flow obtained after gluing, and this in a way compatible with the natural hyperbolicity of the initial flow.
In Section
6
, we show that for such a choice of gluing map, the flow induced by the initial flow on the glued manifold is Anosov, which completes the proof of Theorem
A
. We also show the transitivity criterion stated in Proposition
B
.

The second part is devoted to the applications of the Gluing Theorem.
In Section
7
, we show Theorem
C
.
In Section
8
, we show Theorem
D
.
In Section
9
, we show Theorem
E
.
In Section
10
, we show Theorem
F
.
Finally, we show Theorem
H
in Section
11
.

1.
Preliminaries: building blocks and gluing maps

1.1.
Building block

Definition 1.1.1

(Surface quasi-transverse to a vector field)
.

Let
S
be a transversely orientable surface embedded in a 3-dimensional manifold
M
equipped with a vector field
X
.
We say that
S
is
quasi-transverse to
X
if

(1)

S
contains a finite collection
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}=\{{\mathcal{O}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{O}}_{n}\}
of periodic orbits of
X
,

(2)

X
is transverse to
S{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
,

(3)

each orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
is two-sided in
S
and the orbits of
X
cross these two local sides in two opposite directions.
2
2

2

Formally, this means that the transverse orientation induced by the vector field
X
on the two local sides of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
never simultaneously coincide with a global transverse orientation of the surface
S
.

Figure 1
.
A torus quasi-transverse to a vector field
X
containing two periodic orbits

In particular, a surface
S
transverse to a vector field
X
is a quasi-transverse surface with
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}={\varnothing}
.
We denote
X^{t}
the flow generated by
X
.
Recall that a compact set
\Lambda
invariant by the flow
X^{t}
on a manifold
M
is said to be
hyperbolic of index
(1,1)
for
X
if there exists an
X^{t}
-invariant splitting of the tangent space of
M
over
\Lambda
into a sum

TM|_{\Lambda}=E^{ss}\oplus{\mathbb{R}}.X\oplus E^{uu}
of 1-dimensional subbundles,
and constants
\lambda>1
, and
C>0
such that for a Riemannian metric on
M
, we have

•

\forall v\in E^{uu}
,
\forall t\geq 0
,
\|(X^{t})_{*}v\|\geq C\lambda^{t}v\|
;

•

\forall v\in E^{ss}
,
\forall t\leq 0
,
\|(X^{t})_{*}v\|\geq C\lambda^{-t}\|v\|
.

Definition 1.1.2

(Building block)
.

Let
P
be a compact 3-dimensional manifold with boundary, equipped with a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
vector field
X
.
We say that the pair
(P,X)
is a
building block
(or more simply a
block
) if

(1)

the boundary
\partial P
is quasi-transverse to the vector field
X
,

(2)

the maximal invariant set of the flow of

X
in
P
, denoted
\Lambda:=\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}X^{t}(P)
, is an index
(1,1)
hyperbolic set for the flow of
X
.

Remark 1.1.3
.

The manifold
P
is not necessarily connected.
Building blocks should be thought as the basic pieces of a building game, our goal being to build some Anosov flows by gluing a collection of such blocks together.
From a formal point of view, a finite collection of building blocks can always be viewed as a single non-connected building block.

Remark 1.1.4
.

Let
(P,X)
be a building block.
In the case where the collection
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
of periodic orbit contained in
{\partial P}
is empty, the boundary is transverse to the vector field
X
and one recover the definition of a
hyperbolic plug
in the sense of
[
BBY17
, Definition 3.1 and Definition 3.2]
.
We will then say that
(P,X)
is a
Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block
.
In this case the maximal invariant set
\Lambda
is contained in the interior of
P
, which is no longer true if the collection
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
is non-empty.

Definition 1.1.5

(Entrance boundary and exit boundary)
.

Let
(P,X)
be a building block and
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
be the set of periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
.
We call the
entrance boundary
of
(P,X)
, denoted
P^{\mathrm{in}}
, the subset of
{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
where the flow enters
P
, and the
exit boundary
of
(P,X)
, denoted
P^{\mathrm{out}}
, the subset of
{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
where the flow exits
P
.

We have the splitting
{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}=P^{\mathrm{in}}\sqcup P^{\mathrm{out}}
, and it follows from Definition
1.1.1
, Item
3
that the connected components of
P^{\mathrm{in}}
and
P^{\mathrm{out}}
alternate along
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
We refer to Figure
2
and
3
.

Figure 2
.
A building block
(P,X)

Figure 3
.
Boundary of a building block in a neighborhood of a periodic orbit contained in the boundary

1.2.
Boundary lamination

Let
(P,X)
be a building block, and
\Lambda:=\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}X^{t}(P)
the maximal invariant set.
Let
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
and
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
be respectively the stable and the unstable manifolds of
\Lambda
.
The hyperbolicity of index
(1,1)
of
\Lambda
implies that
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
and
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
are 2-dimensional laminations, transverse to each other.
We suppose that the reader is familiar with basic hyperbolic dynamic theory and refer to
[
HPM77
]
for more details.
Let
{\mathcal{O}}
be a periodic orbit of
X
.
The
multipliers
of
{\mathcal{O}}
are the eigenvalues of the first return map on a Poincaré section for
{\mathcal{O}}
.
The orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
has positive multipliers if and only if its (local) invariant manifolds are cylinders.
In that case, the union
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathcal{O}})\cup{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathcal{O}})
of the local invariant manifolds of
{\mathcal{O}}
disconnects a neighborhood of
{\mathcal{O}}
into four open regions, which are called
quadrants
.
We will say that
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
is a
continuation
of
(P,X)
if
\tilde{P}
is a 3-manifold,
\tilde{X}
is a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
vector field on
\tilde{P}
, and there exists an embedding
h\colon P\to\tilde{P}
which maps the vector field
X
to the vector field
\tilde{X}
restricted to
h(P)
, and such that
h(\Lambda)\subset\operatorname{int}\tilde{P}
.
If
{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
is a periodic orbit contained in the boundary
{\partial P}
of a building block
(P,X)
, the previous definitions hold in a continuation of
(P,X)
.

Claim 1.2.1
.

Let
(P,X)
be a building block and
{\mathcal{O}}
a periodic orbit of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
.
The multipliers of
{\mathcal{O}}
are positive and
\partial P
crosses two opposite quadrants of
{\mathcal{O}}
.

Figure 4
.
The boundary
\partial P
crosses two opposite quadrants of
{\mathcal{O}}

Proof of Claim
1.2.1
.

Let
A
be a tubular neighborhood of
{\mathcal{O}}
in
\partial P
.
It follows from Item
3
of Definition
1.1.1
that
A
is an annulus.
Let
p
be a point in
{\mathcal{O}}
and consider
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-coordinates
(x,y,\theta)
in the neighborhood of
p
(in a continuation
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
of the block) in which the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
coincides with
x=y=0
and the stable and unstable manifolds of
{\mathcal{O}}
are respectively straightened on
y=0
and
x=0
.
The tangent plane to
A
at
p
is transverse to at least one of the two planes
x=0
and
y=0
.
Let us assume for concreteness that it is transverse to
x=0
.
Since
A
contains the periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}:x=y=0
, we deduce that in the neighborhood of
p
,
A
is a smooth graph
y=\phi(x,\theta)
, and the two connected components of
A{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}
correspond to
x<0
and
x>0
.
Orbits in a neighborhood of
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}({\mathcal{O}})\cup{\mathcal{W}}^{u}({\mathcal{O}})
enter a neighborhood of
p
near the unstable manifold
y=0
and exit in a neighborhood of
p
near the stable manifold
x=0
.
Thus, where
\phi>0
, these orbits go from the hypograph of
\phi
to the epigraph of
\phi
, and where
\phi<0
, they go from the epigraph to the hypograph of
\phi
.
Now we know that
A
is transverse to the orbits of
\tilde{X}
and that the orbits cross
A
in two different directions on one side and on the other side of
{\mathcal{O}}
by definition of a quasi-transverse surface.
Therefore, up to changing the coordinate
y
by
-y
, we have
\phi(x,\theta)<0
for
x<0
and
\phi(x,\theta)>0
for
x>0
.
In other words,
A
is topologically transverse to the stable manifold of
{\mathcal{O}}
in a neighborhood of
p
, and this is true for any point
p
.
We deduce that
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathcal{O}}){\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}
is composed of two connected components located on both sides of the annulus
A
.
Therefore
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathcal{O}})
is an annulus.
The argument is the same for
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{\mathrm{loc}}({\mathcal{O}})
.
We deduce that the multipliers of
{\mathcal{O}}
are positive and
A
crosses two opposite quadrants (Figure
4
).
∎

Proposition and Definition 1.2.2

(Entrance, Exit, Boundary lamination)
.

(1)

{\mathcal{L}}:=({\mathcal{W}}^{u}\cup{\mathcal{W}}^{s})\cap{\partial P}
is a 1-dimensional lamination on
{\partial P}
, called
boundary lamination of
(P,X)

(2)

{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}:={\mathcal{L}}\cap P^{\mathrm{in}}={\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cap P^{\mathrm{in}}
is a 1-dimensional lamination on
P^{\mathrm{in}}
, called
entrance lamination of
(P,X)

(3)

{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}:={\mathcal{L}}\cap P^{\mathrm{out}}={\mathcal{W}}^{u}\cap P^{\mathrm{out}}
is a 1-dimensional lamination on
P^{\mathrm{out}}
, called
exit lamination of
(P,X)

Remark 1.2.3
.

The lamination
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
does not intersect the exit boundary
P^{\mathrm{out}}{}
because any orbit in
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
converges to
\Lambda
in the future so never exits the block
(P,X)
, and similarly the lamination
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
does not intersect the entrance boundary
P^{\mathrm{in}}{}
because any orbit in
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
converges to
\Lambda
in the past.
The trace
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cap{\partial P}
and the trace
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}\cap{\partial P}
are therefore disjoint on
{\partial P}
except along the periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
where they coincide.
The previous lemma states that the traces
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cap{\partial P}
and
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}\cap{\partial P}
come together along
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
in a (single) lamination
{\mathcal{L}}=({\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cup{\mathcal{W}}^{u})\cap{\partial P}
of dimension 1 on
{\partial P}
.

Proof of Proposition
1.2.2
.

The lamination
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
is tangent to the vector field
X
, and the vector field
X
is transverse to
P^{\mathrm{in}}
, so
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
intersects transversely
P^{\mathrm{in}}
.
Therefore
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}:={\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cap P^{\mathrm{in}}
is a one-dimensional lamination on
P^{\mathrm{in}}
.
Similarly for the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}={\mathcal{W}}^{u}\cap P^{\mathrm{out}}
.

According to Remark
1.2.3
,

({\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cup{\mathcal{W}}^{u})\cap{\partial P}={\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}\cup{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
.
It suffices to show that the union of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
and
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
reconnect along
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}=\partial P^{\mathrm{in}}=\partial P^{\mathrm{out}}
in a lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
on
{\partial P}
.
Let
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
be an extension of
(P,X)
.
Let
p
be a point of
{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, and
{\mathcal{U}}_{p}
a neighborhood of
p
in
\tilde{P}
with coordinates
(x,y,\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
, such that
{\mathcal{O}}=\{x=y=0\}
.
Let
(\partial_{x},\partial_{y},\partial_{\theta})
be the associated vector fields and consider the usual scalar product in these coordinates.
Since
\partial P
is a smooth submanifold, it is locally transversely orientable by a normal vector field which we denote
\vec{n}
in
{\mathcal{U}}_{p}
.
Since
\partial P
contains
{\mathcal{O}}
, we deduce that
\vec{n}(r)
converges to a vector in the normal plane to
\partial_{\theta}
when
r
tends to
p
.
Similarly, the lamination
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
is locally transversely orientable by a normal vector field which we denote
\vec{m}
in
{\mathcal{U}}_{p}
.
Since the leaf passing through
p
contains
{\mathcal{O}}
, we deduce that the vector
\vec{m}(r)
converges to a vector in the plane normal to the vector
\partial_{\theta}
when
r
tends to
p
.
A vector field tangent to the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
is defined by the vector product
\vec{v}=\vec{n}\times\vec{m}
at each intersection point
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cap P^{\mathrm{in}}
.
It is a vector field normal to the plane generated by
\vec{n}
and
\vec{m}
.
We deduce that
\vec{v}(r)
converges to a vector tangent to
{\mathbb{R}}.\partial_{\theta}
for
r\in{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
which tends to
p
, in other words
\vec{v}(r)
converges to a vector tangent at
T_{p}{\mathcal{O}}
.
Doing this at any point
p
of the set
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, we show that the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
on
P^{\mathrm{in}}
reconnect with
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}=\partial P^{\mathrm{in}}
in a lamination on
\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}=P^{\mathrm{in}}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
A similar proof shows that the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
on
P^{\mathrm{out}}
reconnect with
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}=\partial P^{\mathrm{out}}
in a lamination
\overline{P^{\mathrm{out}}}=P^{\mathrm{out}}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
Therefore,
{\mathcal{L}}:=({\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cup{\mathcal{W}}^{u})\cap{\partial P}={\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}\cup{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
is a lamination of dimension 1 in
\partial P=P^{\mathrm{in}}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}\cup P^{\mathrm{out}}
.
∎

Quasi-Morse-Smale laminations

Definition 1.2.4

(Quasi-Morse-Smale lamination)
.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}
be a 1-dimensional lamination on a closed surface
S
.
We say that
{\mathcal{L}}
is a
quasi-Morse-Smale
lamination if it satisfies the following conditions:

(1)

There exists a finite number of compact leaves
\Gamma=\{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{N}\}
;

(2)

Each half non-compact leaf accumulates on a single compact leaf;

(3)

Each compact leaf
\gamma
, given an orientation, has a contracting or expanding holonomy on each side.
3
3

3

If
\gamma
admits two sides in
S
, the holonomy of
\gamma
may be contracting on both sides, expanding on both sides, or contracting on one side and expanding on the other. If it has a single side in
S
, its holonomy is either contracting or expanding
.

The (possibly empty) set of elements of
\Gamma
such that the holonomy is contracting on one side and expanding on the other is denoted
\Gamma_{*}
, and called the set of
marked compact leaves
.
We further ask:

(4)

There exists a splitting
S{\smallsetminus}\Gamma_{*}=S^{\mathrm{in}}\,\sqcup\,S^{\mathrm{out}}
into two disjoint open sets such that each leaf
\gamma_{*}\in\Gamma_{*}
is adjacent to a connected component of
S^{\mathrm{in}}
and to a connected component of
S^{\mathrm{out}}
.
We call it the
(in,out)-splitting of
S
for
{\mathcal{L}}
.

Figure 5
.
An example of quasi-Morse-Smale lamination on the torus

Remark 1.2.5
.

A quasi-Morse-Smale lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
such that the set of marked leaves
\Gamma_{*}
is empty is a
Morse-Smale lamination
in the sense of
[
BBY17
, Definition 3.9]
, which justifies this terminology.

Proposition 1.2.6
.

Let
(P,X)
be a building block.
Then the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
is a quasi-Morse-Smale lamination on
{\partial P}
, and the set
\Gamma_{*}
of marked leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}
coincides with the set
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
of periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
.

In the case where the boundary
\partial P
is transverse to the vector field
X
, i.e. if
(P,X)
is a Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block, this property is known.
More precisely, we have the following

Lemma 1.2.7

(
[
BBY17
, Proposition 3.8]
)
.

Let
(U,Y)
be a building block whose boundary
\partial U
is transverse to the vector field
Y
.
Let
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
be the boundary lamination of
(U,Y)
.
Then it satisfies the following properties:

(1)

there exists a finite number of compact leaves;

(2)

each non-compact half-leaf accumulates on a single compact leaf;

(3)

each compact leaf has an orientation for which its holonomy is contracting (on each side).

In other words, the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
of a Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block
(U,Y)
is a quasi-Morse-Smale lamination for which the set of marked leaves is empty, namely a
Morse-Smale lamination
.
The following lemma reduces the study of the boundary lamination of a building block
(P,X)
to the case where the boundary of the block is transverse to the vector field.
We denote by
\overline{A}
the closure of a set
A
.
Let
(P,X)
be a building block.
Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
be the set of periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
, and
\Lambda_{X}
the maximal invariant set of
(P,X)
,
P^{\mathrm{in}}
the entrance boundary and
P^{\mathrm{out}}
the exit boundary.

Lemma 1.2.8

(From building block to Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block)
.

There exists a Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block
(U,Y)
such that, if
\Lambda_{Y}
denotes the maximal invariant set of
(U,Y)
,
({\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{Y},{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{Y})
the pair of stable and unstable lamination of
\Lambda_{Y}
,
U^{\mathrm{in}}
the entrance boundary,
U^{\mathrm{out}}
the exit boundary, and
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
the boundary lamination, then :

(1)

(embedding)

there exists an embedding
h\colon P\to U
which maps the vector field
X
on the vector field
Y
in
h(P)
and the set
\Lambda_{X}
on
\Lambda_{Y}
,

(2)

(lamination)

there exists a finite collection
D_{*}=\{D_{1},\dots,D_{n}\}
of open disks contained in
\partial U
, disjoint from
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
, bounded by pairwise distinct compact leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
, such that
U^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
is isotopic to
\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}
along the lamination
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{Y}
and
U^{\mathrm{out}}{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
is isotopic to
\overline{P^{\mathrm{out}}}
along the lamination
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{Y}
.

Recall that a
linearizing neighborhood
of a periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
of
X
is a tubular neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}
of
{\mathcal{O}}
on which the flow of
X
is (locally) orbit equivalent to the suspension flow of the diagonal linear map
(x,y)\mapsto(\lambda x,\mu y)
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
with
0<|\lambda|<1
and
|\mu|>1
.
Every (saddle) hyperbolic periodic orbit has a linearizing neighborhood.
A
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-coordinate system
\xi=(x,y,\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
on
{\mathcal{V}}
in which the flow is linear is a
linearizing coordinate system of
{\mathcal{O}}
.

Proof of Lemma
1.2.8
.

Let
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
be a continuation of
(P,X)
.
Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}=\{{\mathcal{O}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{O}}_{n}\}
be the collection of periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
.
For each
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, we can consider (up to local orbit equivalence) a linearizing neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
for the flow of
\tilde{X}
in
\tilde{P}
, provided with a linearizing coordinate system
(x,y,\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
.
The boundary
\partial P
crosses the opposite quadrants
\{x>0,y>0\}
and
\{x<0,y<0\}
(Claim
1.2.1
).
Let
S
be a topological surface, smooth outside a finite number of simple closed curves
c_{1}^{\mathrm{in}},\dots,c_{n}^{\mathrm{in}}
and
c_{1}^{\mathrm{out}},\dots,c_{n}^{\mathrm{out}}
, which coincides with
{\partial P}
outside the
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
neighborhoods, and decomposes into the union
S=S^{\mathrm{in}}\cup(\cup_{i}A_{i})\cup S^{\mathrm{out}}
, where

•

S^{\mathrm{in}}
is a surface with boundary transverse to the vector field
\tilde{X}
and bounded by
c_{1}^{\mathrm{in}},\dots,c_{n}^{\mathrm{in}}
, which coincides with
P^{\mathrm{in}}
outside the union of the neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
;

•

S^{\mathrm{out}}
is a surface with boundary transverse to the vector field
\tilde{X}
and bounded by
c_{1}^{\mathrm{out}},\dots,c_{n}^{\mathrm{out}}
, which coincides with
P^{\mathrm{out}}
outside the union of the neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
;

•

A_{i}
is an annulus tangent to the vector field
\tilde{X}
, contained in the linearizing neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
in the quadrant
\{x>0,y<0\}
, and bounded by the curves
c^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}
and
c^{\mathrm{out}}_{i}
, such that all orbits of
\tilde{X}
on
A_{i}
go from
c^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}
to
c^{\mathrm{out}}_{i}
.

See Figure
6
.

Figure 6
.
Manifold
\check{P}
in a linearizing neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}

The surface
S
cuts in
\tilde{P}
a compact submanifold
\check{P}
with boundary, which contains
P
and coincides with
P
outside the union of the neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.
Let
\check{X}
be the restriction of
\tilde{X}
to
\check{P}
.
The vector field
\check{X}
restricted to
A_{i}
is orbit equivalent to the vertical field
\partial t
on
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}\times I
, where
t
is the coordinate on
I=[0,1]
.
We glue on each annuli
A_{i}\simeq{\mathbb{S}}^{1}\times I
a cylinder
C_{i}={\mathbb{D}}^{2}\times I
provided with the vertical vector field
\partial_{t}
, along
B_{i}=\partial{\mathbb{D}}_{2}\times I\subset\partial C_{i}
.
Let
f_{i}\colon B_{i}=\partial{\mathbb{D}}^{2}\times I\to{\mathbb{S}}^{1}\times I\simeq A_{i}
a diffeomorphism which preserve the coordinate
t\in I
(Figure
7
).

Figure 7
.
Gluing of a cylinder
C_{i}
along the annulus
A_{i}
tangent to the vector field
\check{X}

Define the quotient manifold
U:=\check{P}\cup(\cup_{i}C_{i})/f
where
f
is the product of
f_{i}
.
Each
f_{i}
maps the vertical vector field of
C_{i}
on the vector field
\check{X}
, it follows that
U
is equipped with a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
vector field
Y
for a differentiable structure on
U
, induced by the union of
\check{X}
and the vertical vector field on each
C_{i}
.

Let us show that the pair
(U,Y)
satisfies Lemma
1.2.8
.
The manifold
U
is a compact smooth manifold of dimension 3 with boundary.
The boundary
\partial U
is the quotient
\partial U=S\cup(\cup_{i}\partial C_{i})/f
.
A point of
\partial U
is either in
S{\smallsetminus}\cup_{i}A_{i}
, where
Y
coincides with the vector field
\check{X}
, or in the boundary
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}\times\{0\}
or
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}\times\{1\}
of a cylinder
C_{i}={\mathbb{D}}^{2}\times[0,1]
, where
Y
is the vertical vector field.
It follows that
Y
is transverse to
\partial U
.
It is clear by construction that the manifold
P
is embedded in the manifold
U
via an embedding which maps
X
to
Y
.
Moreover, the maximal invariant set of
Y
in
P
coincides with the maximal invariant set of
X
in
P
via this embedding.
Indeed, an orbit of
Y
in
C_{i}
enters and exits
C_{i}
, hence
U
, in uniformly bounded time, and an orbit of
Y
which intersects
\check{P}
is either an orbit of the flow of
X
contained in
P
, or intersects the boundary of
\check{P}
.
It follows that
(U,Y)
is a building block which satisfies
1
of Lemma
1.2.8
.

It remains to check Item
2
.
The boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
does not intersect the boundary of
U
contained in
\partial C_{i}
because all orbits in
C_{i}
enter and exit
U
.
In
\check{P}
, the orbits of
Y
coincide with the orbits of
\check{X}
.
We denote
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
and
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
the stable and unstable manifolds of the set
\Lambda_{X}
for the flow of
\check{X}
in
\check{P}
.
Then
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
is the union of the saturation of the laminations
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{X}
by the flow of
\check{X}
and the local stable manifolds of the periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
in the linearizing neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.
Similarly
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
is the union of the saturation of the laminations
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{X}
by the flow of
\check{X}
and of the local unstable manifolds of the periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
in the linearizing neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.
Define

\check{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}:={\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cap\partial\check{P}={\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cap S
and
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}:={\mathcal{W}}^{u}\cap\partial\check{P}={\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cap S
.
By definition, the orbits of the points of
\check{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
are the orbits of the flow of
\check{X}=Y|_{\check{P}}
that accumulate on the set
\Lambda_{X}=\Lambda_{Y}
in the future, in other words
\check{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}={\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{Y}
is the entrance lamination of
(U,Y)
.
The local stable manifold of the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
in the neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
intersects
\partial\check{P}
along a compact leaf
\gamma^{s}_{i}
, which bounds a connected component
D_{i}^{\mathrm{in}}
of
U^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{Y}
, where
U^{\mathrm{in}}
is the union of the connected components of
\partial U
along which the vector field
Y
points into
U
.
This component is a disk which contains the entrance boundary
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}\times\{0\}
of the cylinder
C_{i}
(Figure
8
).

Figure 8
.
Disk
D^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}
in
U^{\mathrm{in}}
, disjoint from
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{Y}
and bounded by a compact leaf
\gamma^{s}_{i}

Similarly,
\check{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}={\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{Y}
is the exit lamination of
(U,Y)
, and the local unstable manifold of the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
in the neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
intersects
\partial\check{P}
along a compact leaf
\gamma^{u}_{i}
.
This leaf borders a connected component
D_{i}^{\mathrm{out}}
of
U^{\mathrm{out}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{Y}
, where
U^{\mathrm{out}}
is the union of the connected components of
\partial U
along which the vector field
Y
points inwards.
This component is a disk which contains the exit boundary
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}\times\{1\}
of the cylinder
C_{i}
.
Let
D_{*}=(\cup_{i}D_{i}^{\mathrm{in}})\cup(\cup_{i}D_{i}^{\mathrm{out}})
.
The surfaces
U^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}D
and
\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}
coincide outside the union of neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
, and in each
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
, they are annuli whose interior is transverse to the lamination
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
.
Thus there is an isotopy between
U^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
and
P^{\mathrm{in}}
, supported in the linearizing neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
, which preserves each leaf of the lamination
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
.
The argument is symmetric for the isotopy between
U^{\mathrm{out}}{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
and
\overline{P^{\mathrm{out}}}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
1.2.6
.

Let
(P,X)
be a building block and
(U,Y)
be the building block associated by Lemma
1.2.8
whose boundary
\partial U
is transverse to the vector field
Y
.
Let
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}={\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{X}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}\cup{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{X}
denote the boundary lamination of
(P,X)
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}={\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{Y}\cup{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{Y}
the boundary lamination of
(U,Y)
.
Let
D_{*}=\{D_{1},\dots,D_{n}\}
be the collection of (open) disks in
\partial U
, disjoint from
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
and bordered by compact leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
given by Lemma
1.2.8
, Item
2
.
It follows from this item the existence of a diffeomorphism
H^{s}\colon U^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}D_{*}\to\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}
which maps the restriction of the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{Y}
to the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}^{s}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
We conclude that the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{X}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
satisfies the first three items of Definition
1.2.4
of a quasi-Morse-Smale lamination on
\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}
.
The compact leaves bordering the disks
D_{i}\in D_{*}\cap U^{\mathrm{in}}
correspond via this diffeomorphism to the compact leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{X}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
on the boundary of
\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}
, in other words to the periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
of
X
on
{\partial P}
.
The similar argument for
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{X}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
implies that the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}={\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{X}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{X}
on
{\partial P}=\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}\cup\overline{P^{\mathrm{out}}}
satisfies the first three items of Definition
1.2.4
, and each compact leaf of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{X}
and
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{X}
admits an orientation for which its holonomy is contracting (on both sides).

It remains to show that the holonomy of the oriented elements of
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
is contracting on one side and expanding on the other, i.e.
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
is the set of marked leaves of the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
.
Item
4
of Definition
1.2.4
is then satisfied by definition of a quasi-transverse surface (Definition
1.1.1
, Item
3
) for the decomposition
{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}=P^{\mathrm{in}}\cup P^{\mathrm{out}}
.
Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
be a periodic orbit of
X
on
{\partial P}
.
It is a compact leaf of
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
, adjacent on one side to
P^{\mathrm{in}}
and on the other to
P^{\mathrm{out}}
.
From the previous proof, there exists an orientation of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
such that its holonomy on the side of
P^{\mathrm{in}}
is contracting, and there exists an orientation such that its holonomy on the side of
P^{\mathrm{out}}
is contracting.
Let us show that these two orientations cannot coincide.
Let
l^{s}
be a leaf of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{X}
which accumulates on
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
.
Then the leaf
l^{s}
intersects a local product neighborhood of a point
p\in{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
,
and it follows that
l^{s}
is the transverse intersection of the stable manifold
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{X}(\gamma)
of an orbit
\gamma\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{X}({\mathcal{O}}_{i})
with the surface
P^{\mathrm{in}}
.
As the surfaces
P^{\mathrm{in}}
and
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{X}({\mathcal{O}}_{i})
are both transverse to
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{X}
, we deduce that the holonomy of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{X}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
along
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
is conjugate to the holonomy of the lamination induced by
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{X}
on
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{X}({\mathcal{O}}_{i})
along
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
.
This holonomy is conjugate to the first return map of the flow of
X
on a transversal of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
in
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{X}({\mathcal{O}}_{i})
, and is therefore expanding for the orientation of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
given by the flow.
Similarly, the holonomy of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{X}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
along
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
is conjugate to the holonomy of the lamination induced by
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{X}
on
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{X}({\mathcal{O}}_{i})
along
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
, and this holonomy is contracting for the orientation of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
induced by the orientation of the flow of
X
(Figure
9
).
Finally, each compact leaf
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
of the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
has an expanding holonomy on one side and a contracting one on the other.
We conclude that the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}={\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{X}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{X}
is a quasi-Morse-Smale lamination, which proves Proposition
1.2.6
.
∎

Figure 9
.
Holonomy of an orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
along the boundary lamination on the entrance boundary
P^{\mathrm{in}}

Prefoliation and filling lamination

Definition 1.2.9

(Prefoliation)
.

A 1-dimensional lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
on a surface
S
is said to be a
prefoliation
if there exists a 1-dimensional foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
on
S
which contains
{\mathcal{L}}
as a sublamination, i.e. such that the leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}
are leaves of
{\mathcal{F}}
.

Remark 1.2.10
.

This is a necessary condition on the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
of a block
(P,X)
for the existence of an Anosov vector field
Z
on a closed manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
obtained by gluing the boundary of a block
(P,X)
.
Indeed, the surface
{\partial P}
then projects in
{\mathcal{M}}
onto a surface
S
quasi-transverse to the vector field
Z
and provided with two foliation
(\zeta^{u},\zeta^{s})
of dimension 1, whose union contains the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
as sublamination.
These are the traces on
S
of the unstable and stable foliations
{\mathcal{F}}^{u}
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}
of the Anosov flow generated by
Z
.
It follows that each connected component of
{\partial P}
is a closed connected surface of zero Euler characteristic, hence a torus or a Klein bottle.

Proposition 1.2.11
.

A quasi-Morse-Smale lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
on a closed surface
S
is a prefoliation on
S
if and only if each connected component of
S{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}
is either

(1)

homeomorphic to an annulus or a Moebius strip bounded by compact leaves, either

(2)

homeomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
, and the accessible boundary
4
4

4

those are the points of the boundary which are endpoints of a segment contained in the interior of the component
is the union of two non-compact leaves
l
and
l^{\prime}
which are asymptotic to each other at both ends.

Definition 1.2.12

(Strip, Filling lamination)
.

Let
B
be a connected component of
S{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}
.
We say that
B
is a
strip
if
B
is homeomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and the accessible boundary of
B
is the union of two non-compact leaves
l
and
l^{\prime}
of
{\mathcal{L}}
which are asymptotic to each other at both ends.
If all connected component of
S{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}
are strips, we say that the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
is
filling on
S
.

Proof of Proposition
1.2.11
.

A lamination contains a finite number of compact leaves (Definition
1.2.4
, Item
1
).
The proposition is then a direct consequence of the following fact, shown in
[
BBY17
]
.

Lemma 1.2.13

(
[
BBY17
, Lemme 3.12]
)
.

Let
S
be a closed surface and
{\mathcal{L}}
be a 1-dimensional lamination on
S
which contains a finite number of compact leaves.
Then there exists only a finite number of connected components of
S{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}
which are not strips.

∎

Figure 10
.
A prefoliation
{\mathcal{L}}
on the torus
S={\mathbb{T}}^{2}

The figure shows a type
1
(in brown) and a type
4
(in grey) for a prefoliation
{\mathcal{L}}
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
.
We will have to make the following additional assumption for technical reasons.

Definition 1.2.14

(Filled block)
.

We say that a block
(P,X)
is a
filled block
if the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
is filling on
{\partial P}
.

In other words,
(P,X)
is a filled block if the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
is a prefoliation and no connected component of
{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}
is an annulus or a Moebius strip.
Figure
11
represents an example of a building block whose boundary lamination is filling.

Figure 11
.
A building block
(P,X)
with a torus
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
as boundary and a filling boundary lamination

1.3.
Gluing map

We want to choose a diffeomorphism
\varphi\colon{\partial P}\to{\partial P}
in such a way that the quotient
P/\varphi=P/(x\sim\varphi(x))
is a closed smooth manifold, and the vector field
X
induces a vector field of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
on
P/\varphi
which is Anosov.
We then note
P_{\varphi}:=P/\varphi
and
X_{\varphi}
the vector field induced by
X
on
P_{\varphi}
.

Gluing maps

Definition 1.3.1

(Gluing map of a building block)
.

Let
(P,X)
be a building block,
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
the periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
,
P^{\mathrm{in}}
the entrance boundary and
P^{\mathrm{out}}
the exit boundary of
(P,X)
.
A
gluing map of
(P,X)
is a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-involution
\varphi\colon{\partial P}\to{\partial P}
such that:

(1)

there exists a partition
{\partial P}=\partial_{1}P\,\sqcup\,\partial_{2}P
where
\partial_{1}P
and
\partial_{2}P
are unions of connected components of
{\partial P}
, and such that
\varphi(\partial_{1}P)=\partial_{2}P
,

(2)

\varphi({\mathcal{O}}_{*})={\mathcal{O}}_{*}
and
\varphi
preserves the orientation of the flow on the orbits of
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
,

(3)

\varphi(P^{\mathrm{out}}{})=P^{\mathrm{in}}{}
.

Remark 1.3.2
.

•

One could equivalently define a gluing map
\varphi
of a block
(P,X)
as a diffeomorphism from
\partial_{1}P
to
\partial_{2}P
, for some partition
{\partial P}=\partial_{1}P\sqcup\partial_{2}P
in two collections of connected components, and which satisfies Items
2
and
3
of Definition
1.3.1
.
This definition has the drawback of arbitrarily breaking the symmetry between
\partial_{1}P
and
\partial_{2}P
, so we prefer to see a gluing map of a block as an involution of
{\partial P}
which matches the connected components pairwise.
In the case where the boundary
{\partial P}
is transverse to the vector field
X
, in other words if
(P,X)
is a Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block, we can take
\partial_{1}P=P^{\mathrm{out}}
,
\partial_{2}P=P^{\mathrm{in}}
, and we already have a natural distinction between
\partial_{1}P
and
\partial_{2}P
.
It is then more natural to define a gluing map as a diffeomorphism
\varphi\colon P^{\mathrm{out}}\to P^{\mathrm{in}}
as the authors of
[
BBY17
]
do.

•

If
\varphi
is a gluing map of a block
(P,X)
, there exists a smooth structure on the quotient space
P/\varphi
which makes it a closed smooth manifold denoted
P_{\varphi}
.
If the boundary of
P
is transverse to the vector field
X
, then
X
always induces a vector field of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
on
P_{\varphi}
which is denoted
X_{\varphi}
.
In the case where there are periodic orbits contained in the boundary, this is not always true.
Indeed, it is necessary to be able to identify the dynamics in the neighborhood of two orbits which are paired, in particular the periodic orbits must have the same multipliers.

Definition 1.3.3

(Dynamical gluing map)
.

A
dynamical gluing map
of
(P,X)
is a gluing map which maps the vector field
X
to itself.
Formally, we require that there exists a continuation
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
of
(P,X)
which contains
P
in its interior, and a tubular neighborhood
U
of
{\partial P}
in
\tilde{P}
, such that
\varphi
is the restriction of a diffeomorphism
\tilde{\varphi}\colon U\to U
which preserves the vector field
\tilde{X}
.

Claim 1.3.4
.

If
\varphi
is a dynamical gluing map of
(P,X)
, then there exists a differentiable structure on the quotient manifold
P_{\varphi}=P/\varphi
which makes it a closed smooth manifold equipped with a vector field
X_{\varphi}
of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
induced by the vector field
X
on
P
.

Proof.

This is a consequence of the definition of a dynamical gluing map, and of the natural differentiable structure on
P_{\varphi}
.
∎

Strong (quasi)-transversality

We are led to make additional assumptions about the gluing map concerning the way the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
and the image
\varphi_{*}{\mathcal{L}}
intersects each other.

Definition 1.3.5

(Strongly quasi-transverse laminations)
.

Let
S
be a surface (possibly with boundary) and
({\mathcal{L}}_{1},{\mathcal{L}}_{2})
a pair of laminations on
S
.

•

{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
are said to be
strongly transverse
if they are transverse to each other on
S
and the closure of a connected component of the complementary
S{\smallsetminus}({\mathcal{L}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{L}}_{2})
is a compact rectangle
bounded by two disjoint arcs of leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
and two disjoint arcs of leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
.

Suppose further that
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
are quasi-Morse-Smale laminations.
Denote
\Gamma_{{\mathcal{L}}_{i}}
the compact leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
,
\Gamma_{{\mathcal{L}}_{i},*}
the marked compact leaves, and
S{\smallsetminus}\Gamma_{{\mathcal{L}}_{i},*}=S^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}\cup S^{\mathrm{out}}_{i}
the decomposition of
S{\smallsetminus}\Gamma_{{\mathcal{L}}_{i},*}
corresponding to
{\mathcal{L}}_{i}
, for
i=1,2
.

•

They are said to be
quasi-transverse
if they coincide on the set of marked leaves
\Gamma_{{\mathcal{L}}_{1},*}=\Gamma_{{\mathcal{L}}_{2},*}=:\Gamma_{*}
, are transverse to each other on the complementary
S{\smallsetminus}\Gamma_{*}
, and if
S^{\mathrm{in}}_{1}=S^{\mathrm{out}}_{2}
.

•

They are said to be
strongly quasi-transverse
if they are quasi-transverse and if they are strongly transverse on
S{\smallsetminus}\Gamma_{*}
.

On the left of Figure
12
, the laminations
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
(in full line) and
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
(in dashed line) are strongly quasi-transverse.
On the right, they are quasi-transverse, but not strongly quasi-transverse.
Indeed, some components of the complementary
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}{\smallsetminus}({\mathcal{L}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{L}}_{2})
, for example the one colored in yellow, are not bounded by two disjoint arcs of leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
and two disjoint arcs of leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}
.

Figure 12
.
Pairs of quasi-transverse laminations on the torus
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
.

Definition 1.3.6

(Strongly quasi-transverse gluing map)
.

Let
(P,X)
be a building block and
\varphi
be a gluing map of
(P,X)
.
We say that
\varphi
is a
strongly quasi-transverse gluing map
if
({\mathcal{L}},\varphi_{*}{\mathcal{L}})
is a pair of strongly quasi-transverse laminations on
\partial P
.

Remark 1.3.7
.

The conditions from Definitions
1.2.9
and
1.3.6
ensure that we can complete the pair
({\mathcal{L}},\varphi_{*}{\mathcal{L}})
into a pair of quasi-transverse foliations on
{\partial P}
.
This is a necessary condition for our construction.
Indeed, if a block
(P,X)
has a dynamical gluing map
\varphi
such that the vector field
X_{\varphi}
on
P_{\varphi}:=P/\varphi
is Anosov, then the manifold
P
projects onto the closed manifold
P_{\varphi}
and the image of the boundary is an embedded quasi-transverse surface
S
, equipped with a pair of quasi-transverse foliations
(\xi^{u},\xi^{s})
of dimension 1 whose union contains the projection of the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
as sublamination.
These are the intersections with
S
of the unstable and stable foliation
{\mathcal{F}}^{u}
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}
of the Anosov flow generated by
X_{\varphi}
.
It follows that each connected component of
{\partial P}
is a closed connected surface of Euler characteristic zero, hence a torus or a Klein bottle.

Figure 13
.
Building block
(P,X)
with filling boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
, and a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map
\varphi

1.4.
Strong isotopy

Equivalence of blocks

We define the following equivalence relation on building blocks.

Definition 1.4.1

(Isotopic building blocks)
.

Let
(P_{0},X_{0})
and
(P_{1},X_{1})
be two building blocks.
They are said to be
isotopic
if there exists a family
(P_{t},X_{t})
of building blocks and a 3-manifold
\tilde{P}
such that:

(1)

there exists a family of embeddings
\{H_{t}\colon P_{0}\to\tilde{P}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
, continuous in the
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
topology, such that
H_{0}
is the inclusion and
H_{t}(P)=P_{t}
,

(2)

there exists a family of vector fields
\{\tilde{X}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
on
\tilde{P}
, continuous in the
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
topology, where
\tilde{X}_{t}
is an extension of
X_{t}
.

We say that
\{(P_{t},X_{t})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is an
isotopy
of building blocks.

Remark 1.4.2
.

In
[
BBY17
, Definition 3.28]
, the authors use a simpler notion of a building block isotopy, for which we have a continuous family of vector fields
\{X_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
all carried by the same manifold
P
with boundary.
In this article, we will really need to consider families
\{P_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of manifolds with boundary. More precisely, we will have to consider cases where the position of the boundary of
P_{0}
and the position of the boundary of
P_{1}
in the neighborhood of a periodic orbit is not the same, as on Figure
14
:
\partial P_{1}
is transverse to the stable and unstable manifolds of a periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
contained in
{\partial P}_{0}
, and
{\partial P}_{1}
is topologically transverse but tangent to the stable manifold
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}({\mathcal{O}})
along
{\mathcal{O}}
.
In the case where the collection
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
is empty, two blocks which are isotopic according to Definition
1.4.1
will be orbit equivalent to blocks which are isotopic in the sense of
[
BBY17
]
.

Figure 14
.
Two isotopic but non-orbit equivalent blocks in the neighborhood of a common periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
contained in the boundary

We will see that this isotopy relation is equivalent to a “weakened” orbit equivalence relation.
Let us recall some elementary definitions. An
orbit equivalence
between a vector field
X
on a manifold
M
and a vector field
Y
on a manifold
N
is a homeomorphism
h\colon M\to N
, which maps the oriented orbits of the flow of
X
to the oriented orbits of the flow of
Y
. A vector field
X
on a manifold
M
is said to be
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-structurally stable
if, for any perturbation
Y
of
X
small enough in the
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-topology on
M
,
Y
is orbit equivalent to
X
on
M
.

We define a simple continuation of a building block
(P,X)
, which consists in gluing linearizing neighborhoods on the periodic orbits contained in the boundary
{\partial P}
.
More precisely:

Definition 1.4.3

(Minimal continuation)
.

Let
(P,X)
be a building block and
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
be the collection of periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
.
We say that
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
is a
minimal continuation of
(P,X)
if

(1)

P\subset\tilde{P}
and
X=\tilde{X}|_{P}
;

(2)

{\mathcal{O}}_{*}\subset\operatorname{int}\tilde{P}
;

(3)

there exists a disjoint union
{\mathcal{V}}_{*}
of linearizing neighborhoods of periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
in
\tilde{P}
such that

\tilde{P}{\smallsetminus}P\,\subset\,{\mathcal{V}}_{*}
.

The following proposition shows that the isotopy relation is equivalent to having a common minimal continuation, up to orbit equivalence.

Proposition 1.4.4
.

Let
(P_{0},X_{0})
and
(P_{1},X_{1})
be two building blocks.

(1)

if
(P_{0},X_{0})
and
(P_{1},X_{1})
are isotopic, then there exists an orbit equivalence
(P_{0}^{\prime},X_{0}^{\prime})
of
(P_{0},X_{0})
, such that
(P_{0}^{\prime},X_{0}^{\prime})
and
(P_{1},X_{1})
have a common minimal continuation
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
.

(2)

Conversely, if
(P_{0},X_{0})
and
(P_{1},X_{1})
are contained in the same minimal continuation
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
, then there exists a block isotopy
(P_{t},X_{t})
such that
P_{t}\subset\tilde{P}
,
X_{t}=\tilde{X}|_{P_{t}}
, and
P_{t}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
is obtained by pushing
P_{0}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
along the flow of
\tilde{X}
.

Two blocks contained in the same minimal continuation have the same collection
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
of periodic orbits contained in the boundary, which justifies the previous notation.
We use the following

Lemma 1.4.5
.

Let
(P,X)
be a building block such that the boundary
\partial P
is transverse to the vector field
X
.
Then
(P,X)
is structurally stable, i.e. for any perturbation
Y
of
X
small enough in the
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-topology on
P
,
(P,Y)
is a building block orbit equivalent to
(P,X)
.

Proof.

We use the following result which allows us to embed
P
into a closed manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
with a structurally stable extension of
X
.
A neighborhood
U\subset{\mathcal{M}}
is said to be
filtrating
for a vector field
Z
on
{\mathcal{M}}
if the boundary
\partial U
is transverse to
Z
and the intersection with any orbit of the flow of
Z
is connected.

Claim 1.4.6

(
[
BB02
, Appendix A]
)
.

There exists a closed 3-manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
equipped with a structurally stable vector field
\bar{X}
, such that
P
is embedded in
{\mathcal{M}}
,
\bar{X}
is an extension of
X
, and
P
is a filtrating neighborhood of
\Lambda
for the flow of
\bar{X}
.

A
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-small perturbation
Y
of the vector field
X
extends to a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-small perturbation
\bar{Y}
of the vector field
\bar{X}
on
{\mathcal{M}}
.
By structural stability, there exists an orbit equivalence
h:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{M}}
which maps the oriented orbits of
\bar{X}
to the oriented orbits of
\bar{Y}
, and which is
{\mathcal{C}}^{0}
-close to the identity.
The orbit equivalence
h
maps
P
to a filtrating neighborhood of a locally maximal compact hyperbolic invariant set of index
(1,1)
for the flow of
\bar{Y}
.
Up to choose a small perturbation, and make an isotopy along the orbits of the flow, we can assume that
h(P)=P
and the vector field
\bar{Y}
is transverse to the boundary
\partial P
.
By setting
Y:=\bar{Y}|_{P}
, it follows that
(P,Y)
is a building block orbit equivalent to
(P,X)
.
∎

Remark 1.4.7
.

This result is no longer true if the boundary of
P
is quasi-transverse to the vector field
X
and contains a nonempty set of periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
Indeed, a small perturbation of such a building block is not always a building block because the condition of quasi-transversality of the boundary to the vector field is not stable by small perturbation.
Moreover, the maximal invariant set
\Lambda
intersects the boundary
{\partial P}
along the periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
For this reason one cannot require
a priori
that the orbit equivalence preserves the boundary of
P
.

Proof of Proposition
1.4.4
.

Let us show the first item.
Let
\{(P_{t},X_{t})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be an isotopy of building blocks.
Let
(U_{t},Y_{t})
be the Béguin-Bonatti-Yublock associated to the block
(P_{t},X_{t})
by Lemma
1.2.8
whose boundary is transverse to the vector field, and such that
(P_{t},X_{t})
is embedded in
(U_{t},Y_{t})
.
Each step in the construction of
(U_{t},Y_{t})
in the proof of Lemma
1.2.8
can be done in a continuous way in
t
, i.e. so that
\{(U_{t},Y_{t})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is an isotopy of building blocks.
The blocks
(U_{t},Y_{t})
are structurally stable by Lemma
1.4.5
.
By Definition
1.4.1
, there exists a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-continuous family of embeddings
H_{t}\colon U_{0}\to\tilde{U}
such that
H_{0}
is the inclusion and
H_{t}(U_{0})=U_{t}
.
Let
Y^{\prime}_{t}
be the conjugate of
Y_{t}
by
H_{t}
.
It is a vector field on
U_{0}
arbitrarily
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-close to
Y^{\prime}_{0}=Y_{0}
.
By structural stability of
(U_{0},Y_{0})
,
the vector fields
Y^{\prime}_{t}
and
Y_{0}
are orbit equivalent via a homeomorphism
h^{\prime}_{t}:U_{0}\to U_{0}
with
h^{\prime}_{0}
the identity on
U_{0}
. Conjugating again by
H_{t}
we deduce that the vector fields
Y_{t}
and
Y_{0}
are orbit equivalent via a homeomorphism
h_{t}\colon U_{0}\to U_{t}
.
Let
(P_{t}^{\prime},X_{t}^{\prime})
be the image by
h_{t}
of the block
(P_{t},X_{t})
in
U_{0}
.
Then
X_{t}^{\prime}
coincides with the restriction of
Y_{t}
to
P_{t}^{\prime}
, and the family
\{(P_{t}^{\prime},X_{t}^{\prime})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
is an isotopy of orbit equivalent building blocks.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
be the periodic orbits of
X_{0}
contained in
{\partial P}_{0}
.
Each block
(P_{t}^{\prime},X_{t}^{\prime})
has naturally the same collection
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
of periodic orbits contained in the boundary.
Let
{\mathcal{V}}_{*}
be a disjoint union of small tubular neighborhoods of the orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
in
U_{0}
contained in a union of linearizing neighborhoods of the orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
Then the family
\{{\partial P}^{\prime}_{t}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{V}}_{*}\}_{t}
is an isotopy of compact surfaces uniformly transverse to
\tilde{X}
embedded in
\tilde{P}
.
If we push along the orbits of
\tilde{X}
, we can suppose that
{\partial P}^{\prime}_{t}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{V}}_{*}={\partial P}^{\prime}_{0}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{V}}_{*}={\partial P}_{0}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{V}}_{*}
.
This operation amounts to an orbit equivalence on each block
(P_{t}^{\prime},X_{t}^{\prime})
.
We deduce that (up an orbit equivalence) the blocks
P_{t}^{\prime}
coincide outside
{\mathcal{V}}_{*}
, and consequently the blocks
(P_{t}^{\prime},X_{t}^{\prime})
are all contained in the same minimal continuation
(\tilde{P}_{0},\tilde{X}_{0})
, with
\tilde{P}_{0}=P_{0}\cup{\mathcal{V}}_{*}
, and the vector field is the restriction of the vector field
\tilde{X}
.

Let us show the second item.
Let
{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
be a periodic orbit of
X
on
{\partial P}
and
{\mathcal{V}}
a linearizing neighborhood of
{\mathcal{O}}
in
\tilde{P}
.
According to Claim
1.2.1
, the surfaces
(\partial P_{0}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}})\cap{\mathcal{V}}
and
(\partial P_{1}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}})\cap{\mathcal{V}}
are annuli contained in the same quadrant and which coincide in their boundary
{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
As the flow is trivializable in a quadrant, we deduce the result.
∎

Remark 1.4.8
.

In Item
2
of Proposition
1.4.4
, we cannot have an isotopy along the orbits of the flow of
\tilde{X}
from
P_{t}
to
P_{0}
.
To see this, let us consider again the case of the blocks
(P_{0},X_{0})
and
(P_{1},X_{1})
of Remark
1.4.2
.
The isotopy along the flow between
\partial P_{0}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}
and
\partial P_{1}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}
in this neighborhood does not extend along the periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
: the transit time tends to infinity when approaching the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}
.

Strongly isotopic triples

Definition 1.4.9

(Strongly isotopic triples)
.

Let
(P_{0},X_{0})
and
(P_{1},X_{1})
be two building blocks with gluing maps
\varphi_{0}
and
\varphi_{1}
.
The triples
(P_{0},X_{0},\varphi_{0})
and
(P_{1},X_{1},\varphi_{1})
are said to be
isotopic
if:

(1)

There exists an isotopy
\{(P_{t},X_{t})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of building blocks.

(2)

There exists a continuous family
\{\varphi_{t}\colon{\partial P}_{t}\to{\partial P}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
of gluing maps of
(P_{t},X_{t})
(in the sense of Remark
1.4.10
).

They are said to be
strongly isotopic
if in addition

(3)

There exists a continuous family of homeomorphisms
\{h_{t}\colon{\partial P}_{0}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{0,*}\to{\partial P}_{t}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{t,*}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
, such that:

•

h_{0}=\operatorname{Id}
,

•

h_{t}
maps the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{0,*}
on the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{t,*}
,

•

h_{1}
maps the lamination
(\varphi_{0})_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{0}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{0,*})
on the lamination
(\varphi_{1})_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{1}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{1,*})
.

Remark 1.4.10
.

The gluing maps
\varphi_{t}
do not share the same domain and destination, so we have to specify what is formally meant by a continuous family.
By definition of a building blocks isotopy
(P_{t},X_{t})
(Definition
1.4.1
), there exists a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-continuous family of embeddings
H_{t}\colon P_{0}\to\tilde{P}
in dimension 3, such that
H_{t}(P)=P_{t}
.
Consider
\psi_{t}\colon{\partial P}_{0}\to{\partial P}_{0}
the conjugate of
\varphi_{t}
by
H_{t}
, and ask that the family be continuous in the
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-topology.
The same remark holds for the family of homeomorphisms
h_{t}
, where we require a continuous family in the
{\mathcal{C}}^{0}
-topology.

Remark 1.4.11
.

In more intuitive terms, the strong isotopy of triples (Definition
1.4.9
) means that, in addition to a classical isotopy of building blocks and gluing maps, one requires a
{\mathcal{C}}^{0}
-isotopy of the intersection patterns of the laminations.
This last isotopy is only continuous because we will lose regularity of the gluing maps in the course of the proof by conjugating by orbit equivalences which are only continuous and non-trivial on the boundary of the blocks.

The definition of strongly isotopic triples ensures that the “glued manifolds” are the same, and that the “intersection pattern of the laminations” is the same.
More formally :

Proposition 1.4.12
.

Let
(P_{0},X_{0},\varphi_{0})
and
(P_{1},X_{1},\varphi_{1})
be two strongly isotopic triples. Then

(1)

up to orbit equivalence, the blocks
(P_{0},X_{0})
and
(P_{1},X_{1})
are contained in the same minimal continuation,

(2)

the quotient manifolds
P_{0}/\varphi_{0}
and
P_{1}/\varphi_{1}
are homeomorphic,

(3)

there exists a homeomorphism
h\colon{\partial P}_{0}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*,0}\to{\partial P}_{1}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*,1}
such that

•

h_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{0}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*,0})={\mathcal{L}}_{1}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*,1}
,

•

h_{*}(\varphi_{0})_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{0}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*,0})=(\varphi_{1})_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{1}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*,1})
.

Proof.

The first item is a consequence of Proposition
1.4.4
, Item
1
.
The others follow directly from Definition
1.4.9

∎

Remark 1.4.13
.

In Definition
1.4.9
, one cannot simply ask for the existence of a continuous family of strongly quasi-transverse gluing maps
\varphi_{t}\colon\partial P_{t}\to\partial P_{t}
.
Indeed, if
(P_{0},X_{0})
and
(P_{1},X_{1})
are two blocks contained in the same minimal continuation
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
, we want to identify as equivalent the following gluing maps:

•

\varphi_{0}\colon\partial P_{0}\to\partial P_{0}
, where
{\partial P}_{0}
is identified with
x=y
in linearizing coordinates
({\mathcal{V}}_{i},\xi_{i}=(x,y,{\mathcal{O}}_{i}))
in the neighborhood of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, and
\varphi_{0}
is defined by
\varphi_{0}(x,y,\theta)=(-x,-y,\theta)
in coordinates
\xi_{i}
and
\xi_{j}
;

•

\varphi_{1}:\partial P_{1}\to\partial P_{1}
, where
{\partial P}_{1}
is identified with
y=\sqrt{x}
in linearizing coordinates
({\mathcal{V}}_{i},\xi_{i}=(x,y,\theta))
in the neighborhood of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, and
\varphi_{1}
is defined
\varphi_{1}(x,y,\theta)=(-x,-y,\theta)
in coordinates
\xi_{i}
and
\xi_{j}
.

Now these two gluing diffeomorphisms are not isotopic via a family of strongly quasi-transverse gluing maps
\varphi_{t}\colon{\partial P}_{t}\to{\partial P}_{t}
.
Indeed, let us assume by contradiction that there is such an isotopy.
Let
W^{s}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
and
W^{u}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
be respectively a leaf of the stable and unstable manifold of
\Lambda
, such that if
l^{s}_{0}=W^{s}\cap{\partial P}_{0}
and
l^{u}_{0}=W^{u}\cap{\partial P}_{0}
are the leaves induced on the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}
of
{\partial P}_{0}
,
l^{s}_{0}
and
\varphi_{0}(l^{u}_{0})
accumulate on a same periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
We can without loss of generality suppose that
W^{s}
is defined by the equation
\theta=-\ln(x)(\operatorname{mod}{\mathbb{Z}})
in the coordinates
\xi_{i}
and
W^{u}
is defined by the equation
\theta=\ln(x)(\operatorname{mod}{\mathbb{Z}})
in the corresponding coordinates
\xi_{j}
.
The set
\{p_{n,0}\}\subset{\partial P}_{0}
of intersection points of
l^{s}_{0}\cap\varphi_{0}(l^{u}_{0})
in the neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
