is ordered (the first intersection, then the second one, etc), and we easily check that there are two convergent subsequences to two distinct points of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
.
Let
l^{s}_{t}=W^{s}\cap{\partial P}_{t}
and
l^{u}_{t}=W^{u}\cap{\partial P}_{t}
.
Then, by strong transversality, we can consider the set
\{p_{n,t}\}\subset{\partial P}_{t}
of the intersection points of
l^{s}_{t}\cap\varphi_{t}(l^{u}_{t})
, and by continuity (in
t
) it also contains two convergent subsequences.
However, we can easily check that the gluing map
\varphi_{1}
does not satisfy this property:
the sequence of points
\{p_{n,1}\}\subset{\partial P}_{1}
admits three convergent subsequences at three distinct points of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
(Figure
15
).

Figure 15
.
Two laminations
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{1}^{\prime}
which are not isotopic via a family of laminations strongly quasi-transverse to
{\mathcal{L}}_{2}

1.5.
Statement of the gluing theorem

We recall that an Anosov flow is a flow
Y^{t}
on a closed manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
, such that the whole manifold is a hyperbolic set for the flow. More precisely,

Definition 1.5.1

(Anosov flow)
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a closed manifold equipped with a vector field
Y
of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
.
We say that
Y^{t}
is an
Anosov flow
, or equivalently
Y
is an
Anosov vector field
, if there exists a
Y^{t}
-invariant splitting of the tangent bundle into the sum
T{\mathcal{M}}=E^{ss}\oplus{\mathbb{R}}.Y\oplus E^{uu}
and constants
\lambda>1
, and
C>0
such that

•

\forall v\in E^{uu}
,
\forall t\geq 0
,
\|(Y^{t})_{*}v\|\geq C\lambda^{t}v\|
;

•

\forall v\in E^{ss}
,
\forall t\leq 0
,
\|(Y^{t})_{*}v\|\geq C\lambda^{-t}\|v\|
.

for a certain Riemannian metric on
{\mathcal{M}}
.

We state the main theorem of this paper.

Theorem 1

(Gluing theorem)
.

Let
(P,X)
be a filled building block, and
\varphi
be a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map of
(P,X)
.
There exists a triple
(P_{1},X_{1},\varphi_{1})
strongly isotopic to
(P,X,\varphi)
such that

X_{1}
induces an Anosov vector field on the closed 3-manifold
P_{1}/\varphi_{1}
.

Sections
2
to
6
are devoted to the proof of this theorem, which is organized as follows.
In Section
2
, we show that we can put the initial triple
(P,X,\varphi)
of the theorem in a “normalized” form.
In Section
3
, we study the hyperbolic properties of the crossing map of the flow from the entrance boundary
P^{\mathrm{in}}
to the exit boundary
P^{\mathrm{out}}
.
In Section
4
, we show how to “spread hyperbolicity” by a process of coordinate change on the boundary of
P
.
In Section
5
, we show that we can use this change of coordinates to modify the gluing map
\varphi
in order to create hyperbolicity along the new recurrent orbits of the flow obtained after the gluing process, and this in a way compatible with the natural hyperbolicity of the initial flow.
In Section
6
, we show that for such a choice of gluing map, the flow induced by the initial flow on the glued manifold is Anosov, which completes the proof of Theorem
1
.

2.
Normalization

In this section, we consider a triple
(P,X,\varphi)
consisting of a filled building block
(P,X)
and a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map
\varphi
(see Section
1
for the definitions).
Denote
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}=\{{\mathcal{O}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{O}}_{n}\}
the collection of periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
,
\Lambda
the maximal invariant set of
(P,X)
,
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}={\mathcal{W}}^{s}(\Lambda)
and
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}={\mathcal{W}}^{u}(\Lambda)
the stable and unstable manifolds of
\Lambda
.

The goal of this section is to modify the triple
(P,X,\varphi)
by strong isotopy in order to obtain a “normalized” form.
In short, we want to :

•

linearize by pieces the dynamics on a neighborhood of the
saddle basic pieces
of
\Lambda
;

•

adjust the multipliers of the orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
to
\{\frac{1}{2},2\}
;

•

construct a pair of foliations
({\mathcal{G}}^{s},{\mathcal{G}}^{u})
on the block which extends the laminations
({\mathcal{W}}^{s},{\mathcal{W}}^{u})
;

•

put the boundary of the block in canonical position in the neighborhood of the orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
;

•

explicitly control the gluing map
\varphi
in the neighborhood of the orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.

To formalize this, we need to define a notion of normalized building block (Definition
2.1.4
) and of normalized gluing map (Definition
2.1.13
).
We can then state the normalization result (Proposition
2.1.16
).

Remark 2.0.1
.

In
[
BBY17
]
, the building blocks which are considered are
saddle
: they contains neither attractors or repellers.
The reason why the authors only considered saddle (Béguin-Bonatti-Yu) blocks is to be able to linearize the dynamic in the neighborhood of the maximal invariant set, which is a technical tool for the proof.
We will show here that we can deal separately with the attractors and the repellers while doing the same construction and use the same idea of proof.

2.1.
Definitions and statement

Normalized block

Let
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
be a minimal continuation of
(P,X)
(Definition
1.4.3
).

Notation 2.1.1

(Attracting, Repelling, Saddle basic pieces)
.

Denote
{\mathfrak{R}}
,
{\mathfrak{S}}
and
{\mathfrak{A}}
the union of repelling, saddle and attracting basic pieces respectively.

Definition 2.1.2

(Maximal saddle invariant set)
.

The
maximal saddle invariant set

\Lambda_{s}
is the compact invariant set in
\Lambda
containing neither attractors nor repellers and which is maximal for this property. Equivalently,
\Lambda_{s}
is equal to the union of
{\mathfrak{S}}
and the intersections of the invariant manifolds of
{\mathfrak{S}}
.

Definition 2.1.3

(Local transverse section)
.

We say that
\Sigma\subset\tilde{P}
is a
local transverse section
of
\Lambda_{s}
for the flow of
\tilde{X}
, if:

(1)

\Sigma
is a finite union of disjoint compact embedded disks transverse to
\tilde{X}
;

(2)

\Sigma
intersects any orbit of
\Lambda_{s}
;

(3)

\partial\Sigma\cap\Lambda_{s}={\varnothing}
.

If
D
is a compact subset of
\Sigma
, we say that the first return of
D
is
well defined
if the positive orbit of any point of
D
intersects the interior of
\Sigma
in continuous time. If
D
has this property, we can
renormalize
the vector field
\tilde{X}
so that the return time of
D
on
\Sigma
is equal to
1
.

Definition 2.1.4

(Normalized building block)
.

We will say that a block
(P,X)
is
normalized
if there exists a minimal continuation
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
such that

(1)

(affine coordinates)

There exists a local transverse section
\Sigma
of
\Lambda_{s}
in
\tilde{P}
, and a cover of
\Sigma\cap\Lambda_{s}
in
\Sigma
into a finite collection of disjoint disks
D=\{D_{1},\dots,D_{n}\}
, provided with a coordinate system
\chi_{i}=(x,y)\colon D_{i}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
on each
D_{i}
such that:

(a)

the first return of the disks of
D
on
\Sigma
is well defined,

(b)

for any orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, there exists a unique disk
D_{i}\in D
which intersects
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
at a single point,

(c)

if
f
is the first return map of the flow of
\tilde{X}
on
\Sigma
, then the intersection
D_{i}\cap f_{\Sigma}^{-1}(D_{j})
is connected and the restriction of
f
to
D_{i}\cap f_{\Sigma}^{-1}(D_{j})
is diagonal affine in the coordinates
\chi_{i}
and
\chi_{j}
(in the sense of Remark
2.1.5
).

We say that
\Sigma
is an
affine section of
\Lambda_{s}
, and
\{(D_{i},\chi_{i})\}
is a
affine coordinate system
on
\Sigma
.

(2)

(multipliers)

The multipliers of the orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
are equal to
\{\frac{1}{2},2\}
.

(3)

(foliation)

There exists a pair of smooth 2-dimensional foliation

({\mathcal{G}}^{s},{\mathcal{G}}^{u})
on
\tilde{P}
satisfying :

(a)

{\mathcal{G}}^{s}
and
{\mathcal{G}}^{u}
are
\tilde{X}^{t}
-invariant, transverse to each other, and contain
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
and
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
as sublamination respectively,

(b)

The foliations
{\mathcal{G}}^{s}\cap\Sigma
and
{\mathcal{G}}^{u}\cap\Sigma
coincide with
x=\operatorname{\textit{cst}}
and
y=\operatorname{\textit{cst}}
in the affine coordinates on
\Sigma
given by Item
1
.

We say that
({\mathcal{G}}^{s},{\mathcal{G}}^{u})
is a
pair of affine invariant foliations
of
(P,X)
.

(4)

(straightened boundary)

For any orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, there exists a
linearizing coordinate system
({\mathcal{V}}_{i},\xi_{i}=(x,y,\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}})
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
for the flow of
\tilde{X}
, compatible with the affine coordinate system of the section
\Sigma
, and in which the boundary coincides with the diagonal
\{x=y\}
. More precisely:

(a)

if
D_{i}
is the disk given by Item
1b
which intersects
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
, then it coincides with
\{\theta=0\}
, and the coordinates
\xi_{i}
and
\chi_{i}
coincide on
D_{i}

(b)

P
coincides with the region
\{y\geq x\}
in the chart
\xi_{i}
on
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}

We say that
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
is a
normalized neighborhood
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
and
({\mathcal{V}}_{i},\xi_{i})
is a
normalized coordinate system
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
.

We refer to Figure
16
.

Figure 16
.
Normalized block
(P,X)
in the neighborhood of a periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
in a normalized coordinate system
({\mathcal{V}}_{i},\xi_{i})

Remark 2.1.5
.

Item
1
is formally stated as follows.
For any pair
(i,j)
such that
D_{i}\cap f^{-1}(D_{j})\neq{\varnothing}
, there exists
(a_{i,j},b_{i,j})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
and real numbers
0<|\lambda_{i,j}|<1
and
|\mu_{i,j}|>1
such that for all
p\in D_{i}\cap f^{-1}(D_{j})
, if
(x,y)=\xi_{i}(p)
, we have

(\xi_{j}\circ f\circ\xi_{i}^{-1})(x,y)=(a_{i,j}+\lambda_{i,j}.x,\ b_{i,j}+\mu_{i,j}.y)

Properties of a normalized block

Corollary 2.1.6
.

Let
(P,X)
be a normalized building block and
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
a minimal continuation.
For any periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
contained in
{\partial P}
, if
({\mathcal{V}}_{i},\xi_{i}=(x,y,\theta))
is a normalized coordinate system of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
in
\tilde{P}
(Definition
2.1.4
, Item
4
), then the expression of the flow of
\tilde{X}
in these coordinates is

\tilde{X}^{t}(x,y,\theta)=(2^{-t}x,\,2^{t}y,\,\theta+t)

Proof.

By assumption (Definition
2.1.4
, Item
4a
),
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
is contained in the suspension neighborhood of the disk
D_{i}
which intersects
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
, and the flow of
\tilde{X}
in coordinates
\xi_{i}
coincides with the suspension flow of the first-return map
f
on the disk
D_{i}
in coordinates
\chi_{i}
.
According to Item
1c
, this map is affine in each coordinates of the system
\chi_{i}=(x,y)
on
D_{i}\cap f^{-1}(D_{i})
,
and according to Item
2
, it has multipliers
\frac{1}{2}
and
2
.
The statement follows.
∎

Remark 2.1.7
.

Let
(P,X)
be a normalized block, and
({\mathcal{G}}^{s},{\mathcal{G}}^{u})
a pair of affine invariant foliations on
(P,X)
(Definition
2.1.4
, Item
3
).
The normalized coordinate system
\xi_{i}=(x,y,\theta)\colon{\mathcal{V}}_{i}\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
induce the coordinate system

\rho_{i}=(x,\theta)\colon{\partial P}\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{i}\to{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
on the boundary of
P
by “forgetting” the
y
coordinate.
In these coordinates, if
\tilde{\theta}\in{\mathbb{R}}
is a lift of the coordinate
\theta\in{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
, then the leaves of the foliation
{\mathcal{G}}^{u}\cap\partial P
are the curves

x=\operatorname{\textit{cst}}2^{-\tilde{\theta}}
and the leaves of the foliation
{\mathcal{G}}^{s}\cap\partial P
are the curves

x=\operatorname{\textit{cst}}2^{\tilde{\theta}}
.

Definition 2.1.8

(Boundary of a lamination)
.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}
be a lamination on a surface
S
.
The
boundary
\partial{\mathcal{L}}
of
{\mathcal{L}}
is the complementary
{\mathcal{L}}{\smallsetminus}\operatorname{int}_{S}{\mathcal{L}}
.
It is a closed sublamination of
{\mathcal{L}}
in
S
with empty interior.
A leaf
l\in\partial{\mathcal{L}}
is called a
boundary leaf
of
{\mathcal{L}}
.

See Figure
17
.

Figure 17
.
Boundary (in violet) of a filling quasi-Morse-Smale lamination on an annulus

We state now the following

Proposition 2.1.9
.

Let
(P,X)
be a building block equipped with an affine section
\Sigma
and a pair of affine invariant foliations
({\mathcal{G}}^{s},{\mathcal{G}}^{u})
.
Then

(1)

{\mathcal{G}}^{u,\mathrm{out}}:={\mathcal{G}}^{u}\cap P^{\mathrm{out}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{out}}:={\mathcal{G}}^{s}\cap P^{\mathrm{out}}
are smooth one-dimensional foliations on
P^{\mathrm{out}}
transverse to each other

(2)

{\mathcal{G}}^{u,\mathrm{in}}:={\mathcal{G}}^{u}\cap P^{\mathrm{in}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{in}}:={\mathcal{G}}^{s}\cap P^{\mathrm{in}}
are smooth one-dimensional foliations on
P^{\mathrm{in}}
transverse to each other

(3)

{\mathcal{F}}:={\mathcal{G}}^{u,\mathrm{out}}\,\cup\,{\mathcal{O}}_{*}\,\cup\,{\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{in}}
is a smooth one-dimensional foliation of
{\partial P}
, which contains the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
as sublamination.
The holonomy of compact leaves of
\partial{\mathcal{L}}
for the foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
are conjugated to affine maps
5
5

5

of each of the two sides of the compact leaf when the compact leaf is two sided
.

Remark 2.1.10
.

Every connected component of
{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}
which is a strip
B
accumulates at both end on a compact boundary leaf (Definition
2.1.8
).
It follows from Proposition
2.1.9
, Item
3
that there is a neighborhood of the ends of
B
where the holonomy maps of the foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
are conjugated to affine maps.
This is a key ingredient for the proof of
[
BBY17
, Theorem 1.5]
, which was obtained by considering only
saddle
Béguin-Bonatti-Yu blocks and linearizing globally the dynamic in the neighborhood of the maximal invariant set.
We bypass this problem by linearizing only in the neighborhood of the maximal
saddle
invariant set, and get the same property.

We will need general preliminary results, valid for any building block
(P,X)
.
Let
O
be an orbit of
X
and
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(O)
the stable manifold of
O
.
A
stable separatrix of
O
is a connected component of
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(O){\smallsetminus}O
, denoted
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{+}(O)
.
We say that a stable separatrix
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{+}(O)
of
O
is
free
if it is disjoint from
\Lambda
.
Similarly, we define an unstable separatrix and a free unstable separatrix of
O
.

Lemma 2.1.11
.

Any compact leaf
\gamma
of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
6
6

6

\gamma
is not a periodic orbit of
X
is the transverse intersection of a free stable separatrix
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{+}(O)
of a periodic orbit
O
of
X
with
P^{\mathrm{in}}
.
Similarly, any compact leaf
\gamma
of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
is the transverse intersection of a free unstable separatrix
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{+}(O)
of a periodic orbit
O
with
P^{\mathrm{out}}
.

Proof of Lemma
2.1.11
.

Each orbit of
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}{\smallsetminus}\Lambda
transversely intersects
P^{\mathrm{in}}
at a single point.
Let
C
be the connected component of
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}{\smallsetminus}\Lambda
such that
\gamma=C\cap P^{\mathrm{in}}
.
The orbit space on
C
is a circle, so
C
is a cylinder trivially foliated by the flow.
It follows that
C
is contained in the stable manifold of a periodic orbit
O
.
The orbit
O
is the unique periodic orbit of
X
in
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(O)
, and
C
is flow invariant. It follows that
C
is a connected component of
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(O){\smallsetminus}O
, in other words a stable separatrix of the periodic orbit
O
.
Since
C
is disjoint from
\Lambda
, this separatrix is free.
∎

Recall that
\Lambda_{s}
denote the maximal saddle invariant set (Definition
2.1.2
).

Lemma 2.1.12
.

The boundary sublamination
\partial{\mathcal{L}}
of
{\mathcal{L}}
is contained in the lamination induced by the maximal saddle invariant set
\Lambda_{s}
.
Any periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
contained in
{\partial P}
belongs to
\Lambda_{s}
.

Proof.

The stable manifolds of the attractors
{\mathfrak{A}}
in
P
foliates an open set of
P
(the union of basins of the attractors) with non-empty interior, the stable manifolds of
\Lambda_{s}
forms a lamination with empty interior in
P
, and the stable manifolds of
{\mathfrak{R}}
coincide with
{\mathfrak{R}}
so never intersects the boundary.
It follows that the interior of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
in
P^{\mathrm{in}}
contains the intersection
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}({\mathfrak{A}})\cap P^{\mathrm{in}}
and its complementary
\partial{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
contains
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(\Lambda_{s})\cap P^{\mathrm{in}}
.
Similarly, the interior of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
in
P^{\mathrm{out}}
contains the intersection
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}({\mathfrak{R}})\cap P^{\mathrm{out}}
and its complementary
\partial{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
contains
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(\Lambda_{s})\cap P^{\mathrm{out}}
.
The boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}\cap{\mathcal{O}}_{*}\cap{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
has boundary
\partial{\mathcal{L}}
contained in the union
\partial{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}\cup\partial{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
.
Therefore it remains to show that
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
is contained in
\Lambda_{s}
in order to prove the lemma.
Let
{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
and consider
{\mathcal{V}}
a small tubular neighborhood of
{\mathcal{O}}
.
Suppose that
{\mathcal{O}}
belongs to an attracting basic piece
{\mathfrak{A}}_{i}
.
Consider a point
p\in{\mathfrak{A}}_{i}
in
{\mathcal{V}}
disjoint from
{\mathcal{O}}
.
The unstable manifold
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(p)
crosses
P^{\mathrm{out}}
, so there are orbits of
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}({\mathfrak{A}}_{i})={\mathfrak{A}}_{i}\subset\Lambda
which exit the block, this is a contradiction.
We deduce that
{\mathcal{O}}
is an isolated periodic orbit, so it is a saddle basic piece and the first assumption is wrong.
The argument are similar for proving that
{\mathcal{O}}
can not belong to a repelling basic piece.
It follows that the second assertion, hence the lemma, is true.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.1.9
.

The foliations
{\mathcal{G}}^{s}
and
{\mathcal{G}}^{u}
are smooth and transverse to each other, tangent to the vector field
X
, and the surfaces
P^{\mathrm{in}}
and
P^{\mathrm{out}}
are transverse to
X
.
We deduce the first two items.
Let
{\mathcal{F}}:={\mathcal{G}}^{u,\mathrm{out}}\cup\,{\mathcal{O}}_{*}\,\cup\,{\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{in}}
.
The lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
is a sublamination of
{\mathcal{G}}^{u,\mathrm{out}}
on
P^{\mathrm{out}}
and
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
is a sublamination of
{\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{in}}
on
P^{\mathrm{in}}
.
The foliation
{\mathcal{G}}^{u,\mathrm{out}}
on
P^{\mathrm{out}}{}
and
{\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{in}}
on
P^{\mathrm{in}}{}
reconnect with
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
to form a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-foliation which contains the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}={\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}\cup{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
as sublamination (see the equations of Remark
2.1.7
).

Let
\gamma
be a compact boundary leaf of
{\mathcal{L}}\subset{\mathcal{F}}
.
If
\gamma
is a leaf of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
, then it is the intersection of a cylindrical free stable separatrix
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{+}(O)
of a periodic orbit
O\in\Lambda_{s}
according to Lemma
2.1.11
and Lemma
2.1.12
.
The paths
\gamma
and
O
are freely homotopic in
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{+}(O)
(as non-oriented paths).
The holonomy of
O
for the foliation
{\mathcal{G}}^{s}
is conjugate to the first return map of the flow on a transversal of
O
in
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(O)
.
Since the flow of
X
admits an affine section in the neighborhood of the maximal saddle invariant set
\Lambda_{s}
, this map is conjugate to an affine map (expanding for the orientation induced by the flow).
We deduce that the holonomy maps of
\gamma
for the foliation
{\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{in}}
are conjugate to affine maps (contracting for the orientation induced by the flow).
The same is true for a compact leaf of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
.
In the case
\gamma
is a periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, we consider the holonomy on one side and on the other of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
.
One corresponds to the holonomy of the foliation
{\mathcal{G}}^{s}
along
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
(therefore expanding for the flow orientation) and the other to the holonomy of the foliation
{\mathcal{G}}^{u}
along
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
(therefore contracting), and they are conjugated to affine maps by the same proof.
∎

Normalized gluing map

Definition 2.1.13

(Normalized gluing map and triple)
.

Let
(P,X)
be a normalized block with a pair of affine invariant foliations
({\mathcal{G}}^{s},{\mathcal{G}}^{u})
. Denote
{\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{in}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}^{u,\mathrm{out}}
the induced foliations on
P^{\mathrm{in}}
and
P^{\mathrm{out}}
.
We say that a gluing map
\varphi\colon{\partial P}\to{\partial P}
of
(P,X)
is
normalized
if:

(1)

For each periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, there exist normalized coordinate systems
({\mathcal{V}}_{i},\xi_{i}=(x,y,\theta))
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
and
({\mathcal{V}}_{j},\xi_{j}=(x,y,\theta))
of for
{\mathcal{O}}_{j}=\varphi({\mathcal{O}}_{i})
such that the expression for
\varphi
in these coordinates is

\varphi(x,x,\theta)=(-x,-x,\theta)

(2)

The foliation
\varphi_{*}{\mathcal{G}}^{u,\mathrm{out}}
is transverse to the foliation
{\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{in}}
on
P^{\mathrm{in}}
.

Then we say that
(P,X,\varphi)
is a normalized triple.

Remark 2.1.14
.

The boundary
{\partial P}
has equation
x=y
in a normalized coordinate system
({\mathcal{V}}_{i},\xi_{i})
, which justifies the expression of Item
1
.
More formally (but we don’t write it down for sake of simplicity), this expression means that for any
(x,x,\theta)
in the image
\xi_{i}({\mathcal{V}}_{i}\cap\partial P)
, we have

\xi_{j}\circ\varphi\circ\xi_{i}^{-1}(x,x,\theta)=(-x,-x,\theta)
.

Proposition 2.1.15
.

A gluing map
\varphi
of a normalized block
(P,X)
satisfying Item
1
of Definition
2.1.13
is a dynamical gluing map of
(P,X)
(in the sense of Definition
1.3.3
).

Proof.

Let
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
be a minimal continuation of
(P,X)
.
It must be shown that
\varphi
is the restriction of a diffeomorphism
\tilde{\varphi}
on a neighborhood of
{\partial P}
in
\tilde{P}
which maps the vector field
\tilde{X}
to itself.
Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
be the collection of periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
.
For each periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, if
({\mathcal{V}}_{i},\xi_{i})
and
({\mathcal{V}}_{j},\xi_{j})
denote the normalized coordinate systems in the neighborhood of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{j}:=\varphi({\mathcal{O}}_{i})
, then the formula
(x,y,\theta)\mapsto(-x,-y,\theta)
defines an extension
\tilde{\varphi}
of
\varphi
to a neighborhood of
\partial P
in
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
and
{\mathcal{V}}_{j}
.
Let
{\mathcal{V}}_{*}
be the union of the normalized neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
, and
{\mathcal{V}}
a neighborhood of
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
strictly contained in
{\mathcal{V}}_{*}
.
Then
{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{V}}
is a compact surface transverse to
X
.
By the flow box theorem for
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
vector fields, there exists a small tubular neighborhood
U
of
{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{V}}
with coordinates
(p,t)\in({\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{V}})\times(-1,1)
in which the vector field
\tilde{X}
is the trivial field
\partial/\partial t
and
P
is identified with the set
\{-1<t\leq 0\}
.
We extend
\varphi
by the map
(p,t)\mapsto(\varphi(p),-t)
.
These two local definitions are compatible.
Indeed the set
{\partial P}\cap({\mathcal{V}}_{*}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{V}})
is uniformly transverse to
\tilde{X}
and there is only one way to extend
\varphi
on a tubular neighborhood by a diffeomorphism which maps the vector field
\tilde{X}
to itself.
We thus obtain coordinate systems on a tubular neighborhood of
{\partial P}
in
\tilde{P}
in which the diffeomorphism
\varphi
extends to a diffeomorphism
\tilde{\varphi}
which maps the vector field
\tilde{X}
to itself.
∎

Statement

The aim of this section is to show the following proposition.
Recall that a building block
(P,X)
is said to be saddle if its maximal invariant set
\Lambda
contains neither attractors nor repellers, and filled if the connected components of the complementary of the boundary lamination
{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}
are strips.

Proposition 2.1.16

(Normalization of a triple)
.

Let
(P_{0},X_{0},\varphi_{0})
be a filled building block, provided with a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map.
Then there exist a normalized triple
(P,X,\varphi)
strongly isotopic to
(P_{0},X_{0},\varphi_{0})

We refer to Definition
1.4.9
for the strong triple isotopy.

Remark 2.1.17
.

The building block
(P,X)
obtained by Proposition
2.1.16
is filled and the gluing map
\varphi
is strongly quasi-transverse (indeed those properties are preserved under strong isotopy).

Remark 2.1.18
.

We follow closely the techniques of “normalization” of blocks and gluing maps of Béguin-Bonatti-Yu (
[
BBY17
, Section 5]
).
The first difference is that our blocks can contains attractors and repellers.
We will isolate the attractors and repellers and we recover the same techniques of linearization by considering the dynamic near the maximal saddle invariant set.
The other difference is that we want to “straighten” the boundary in the neighborhood of the periodic orbits to put it in the canonical position (Item
4
of Definition
2.1.4
).
This operation done at Subsection
2.6
is typically obtained by pushing the boundary along the flow for an infinite time in the neighborhood of the periodic orbits contained in the boundary.
This takes us out of the orbit equivalence class of the initial block, and the gluing map no longer extends to the periodic orbits.
We will say that we obtain an
incomplete gluing map
.
Then we have to show that we can modify this incomplete gluing map in its strong isotopy class so that it extends into a true gluing map.
On the other hand, some technical difficulties are added by the fact that the blocks we work with are not structurally stable, unlike the Béguin-Bonatti-Yu blocks.
Therefore, perturbing the vector field of a block, as it is done in the sections
2.2
and
2.3
to linearize the flow and adjust the multipliers (Item
1
and
2
, Definition
2.1.4
), will force us to consider a new block carried by another manifold.

Proof summary

The proof of this proposition is organized as follows.

•

(affine section)

In Subsection
2.2
, we show that we can perturb a block
(P,X)
in its orbit equivalence class, so as to linearize the dynamics in the neighborhood of the maximal saddle invariant set
\Lambda_{s}
.
More precisely, we show that there exists a block
(P_{1},X_{1})
isotopic to
(P,X)
among the orbit equivalent blocks which admits an affine section
\Sigma
of
\Lambda_{s}
(Proposition
2.2.2
).
This block then satisfies Item
1
of Definition
2.1.4
.
The key point is that the return map on a local transverse section
\Sigma
of the maximal saddle invariant set
\Lambda_{s}
is arbitrarily close to an affine map if the section is small enough.
One can then use a (weakened) structural stability result on building blocks (Proposition
2.2.3
).

•

(multipliers)

In Subsection
2.3
, we show that any block
(P,X)
which admits an affine section is isotopic to an orbit equivalent block
(P_{1},X_{1})
which admits an affine section and such that the multipliers of the periodic orbits contained in the boundary are equal to
\{\frac{1}{2},2\}
, in other words, which satisfies Items
1
and
2
of Definition
2.1.4
.
This is Proposition
2.3.1
.
The proof consists in continuously modifying the return map on a Markov partition in the neighborhood of the periodic orbits of the boundary.

•

(pair of affine invariant foliations)

In Subsection
2.4
, we show that we can foliate a filled building block
(P,X)
which admits an affine section
\Sigma
by a pair of affine invariant foliations
({\mathcal{G}}^{s},{\mathcal{G}}^{u})
, in other words which satisfies Item
3
of Definition
2.1.4
.
This is Proposition
2.4.1
.
The key point is that one can push through the flow of
X
the horizontal and vertical foliations induced by the affine coordinates on
\Sigma
, in order to obtain a pair of transverse and
X^{t}
-invariant foliations on an invariant neighborhood of
\Lambda_{s}
in
P
, which contain the stable and unstable manifolds of
\Lambda_{s}
.
Since the block
(P,X)
is a filled block, there is a unique way to complete these foliations in a pair of affine invariant foliations on
P
.

•

(strongly isotopic incomplete gluing map)

In Subsection
2.5
, we consider a block
(P_{1},X_{1})
with a pair of affine invariant foliations, and isotopic to an orbit equivalent block
(P_{0},X_{0})
with a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map
\varphi_{0}
.
We show (Proposition
2.5.5
) that we can obtain an “almost” gluing map of
(P,X)
: an involution
\hat{\varphi}\colon{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}\to{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
which maps the exit boundary
P^{\mathrm{out}}
to the entrance boundary
P^{\mathrm{in}}
but does not extend a priori to the periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
We will say that it is a
incomplete gluing map
(Definition
2.5.1
).
We require that
\hat{\varphi}
and
\varphi_{0}
respect a relation compatible with Definition
1.4.9
, Item
3
, in other words we want the pattern of the intersection of the laminations to be the same.
We will say that they are
strongly isotopic
(Definition
2.5.2
).

•

(boundary straightening)

In Subsection
2.6
, we show that if
(P,X)
is building block which admits an affine section
\Sigma
, then there exists a block
(P_{1},X_{1})
in a minimal continuation of
(P,X)
which satisfies Item
4
of Definition
2.1.4
, in other words the boundary
{\partial P}_{1}
is diagonal in the affine coordinates of the section
\Sigma
in the neighborhood of the periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
contained in the boundary.
This is Proposition
2.6.1
.
If the initial block
(P,X)
satisfies Items
1
,
2
and
3
of Definition
2.1.4
, it follows that the block
(P_{1},X_{1})
is a normalized block.
In a final paragraph, we explain that we can recover an incomplete gluing map on the new block
(P_{1},X_{1})
which is strongly isotopic to the gluing map of the initial block
(P,X)
(Lemma
2.6.3
).
Such an incomplete gluing map is typically obtained by pushing the initial gluing map by the flow on the boundary of
P_{1}
for an infinite time, and will in general not extend to periodic orbits.

•

(gluing map normalization)

In Subsection
2.7
, we consider a normalized block
(P,X)
equipped with an incomplete gluing map
\hat{\varphi}
which maps the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
to a strongly transverse lamination on
{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
We show that there exists a normalized (complete) gluing map
\varphi_{1}
of
(P,X)
strongly isotopic to
\hat{\varphi}
.
This is Proposition
2.7.1
.
We first put the foliations transverse to each other in
{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
(Proposition
2.7.2
).
We then modify the incomplete gluing map
\hat{\varphi}
by strong isotopy on the complementary of the periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
contained in
{\partial P}
, so as to control the image by
\hat{\varphi}
of the foliations induced on the boundary in the neighborhood of
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, and such that the new incomplete gluing map extends trivially on
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.

•

(conclusion)

In a last Subsection
2.8
, we finally show that, starting from a filled building block
(P,X)
provided with a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map
\varphi
, we can put together the results of the previous subsections in order to construct a normalized triple
(P_{1},X_{1},\varphi_{1})
strongly isotopic to
(P,X,\varphi)
which satisfies Proposition
2.1.16
.

2.2.
Affine section

In this section we show that we can perturb a block
(P,X)
by isotopy in its orbit equivalence class in a block which admits an affine section in the sense of Definition
2.1.4
, Item
1
.

Definition 2.2.1

(Affine block)
.

We say that a building block
(P,X)
is
affine
if it admits an affine section of the maximal saddle invariant set, in the sense of Definition
2.1.4
, Item
1
.

Proposition 2.2.2
.

Let
(P,X)
be a building block.
There exists an affine building block
(P_{1},X_{1})
, isotopic to
(P,X)
among orbit equivalent building blocks.

It is crucial to work near the saddle basic pieces, as it ensures the existence of a local transverse section arbitrarily small, and allows us to linearize the return map and use structural stability.
We follow closely the proof of
[
BBY17
, Lemma 5.3]
for
saddle hyperbolic plugs
which works the same when restricted to the maximal saddle invariant set.
We perturb the return map on a local transverse section
\Sigma
of the maximal saddle invariant set
\Lambda_{s}
.
If the section is small enough, there exists a perturbation
X^{\prime}
arbitrarily
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-close to
X
such that the first-return map on
\Sigma
is affine in some coordinates on a neighborhood of
\Sigma\cap\Lambda_{s}
.
A difference is that the blocks that we consider are not structurally stable.

Weak structural stability of building blocks

The following proposition gives a “weakened” structural stability result for building blocks.

Proposition 2.2.3
.

Let
(P,X)
be a building block and
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
be a minimal continuation (Definition
1.4.3
).
If
\tilde{Y}
is a vector field on
\tilde{P}
close enough to
\tilde{X}
in the
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
topology, then there exists a block
(Q,Y)=(Q,\tilde{Y}|_{Q})
contained in
\tilde{P}
which is isotopic to
(P,X)
among the orbit equivalent building blocks.

Proof.

According to Lemma
1.2.8
, up to shrink the minimal continuation
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
, we can embed
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
into a building block
(\hat{P},\hat{X})
whose boundary
\partial\hat{P}
is transverse to the vector field
\hat{X}
.
A small enough perturbation
\tilde{Y}
of
\tilde{X}
on
\tilde{P}
extends to
\hat{P}
into a
\epsilon
-perturbation of
\hat{X}
, denoted
\hat{Y}
.
By structural stability (Lemma
1.4.5
), there exists a homeomorphism
h\colon\hat{P}\to\hat{P}
isotopic to the identity which realizes an orbit equivalence between the flow of
\hat{X}
and the flow of
\hat{Y}
.
Moreover,
h
is
K.\epsilon
close to the identity in the
{\mathcal{C}}^{0}
topology on
\hat{P}
, with
K
a uniform constant.
Then
h
induces an orbit equivalence between
(P,X)
and
(Q,Y)
where
Q:=h(P)
is contained in a
K.\epsilon
-neighborhood of
P
in
\hat{P}
, and
Y:=\hat{Y}|_{h(P)}
.
Let
{\mathcal{V}}_{0}
be a disjoint union of small tubular neighborhoods of periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
, contained in linearizing neighborhoods.
Then
{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{V}}_{0}
is a compact surface uniformly transverse to
\hat{X}
.
For
\epsilon
small enough,
h({\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{V}}_{0})
is a compact surface uniformly transverse to
\hat{Y}
in a
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{V}}_{0}
.
Up to push the surface along the orbits of
\hat{X}
and modify
h
, we can assume that
h({\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{V}}_{0})={\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{V}}_{0}
.
Then for
\epsilon
small enough,
Q=h(P)
is contained in the minimal continuation
\tilde{P}
of
(P,X)
.
Moreover (up to do an isotopy along the orbits of the flow),
Q
is a surface quasi-transverse to
Y
, so
(Q,Y)
is an orbit equivalent building block to
(P,X)
.
Since
h
is isotopic to the identity, the vector fields
\tilde{X}
and
\tilde{Y}
are isotopic.
Moreover, the boundary of
P
and
Q
are contained in the same minimal continuation, so according to Proposition
1.4.4
, Item
2
, the blocks
(P,X)
and
(h(P),\tilde{X}|_{h(P)})
are isotopic among building blocks.
We deduce that
(P,X)
and
(h(P),\tilde{Y}|_{h(P)})=(h(P),Y)
are isotopic among the orbit equivalent building blocks.
The block
(h(P),Y)
satisfies Proposition
2.2.3
.
∎

In general one cannot have
h(P)=P
, i.e.
h
does not preserve the boundary of
P
, and if
Y
is a small perturbation of
X
on
P
, the pair
(P,Y)
is not always a building block (Remark
1.4.7
).

Markov partition

We will need the notion of Markov partition.
We give the definition following
[
BB02
, Section 2.1]
.
Let
\Sigma
be a transverse local section of
\Lambda_{s}
in a building block
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
,
f
the first return map of the flow of
\tilde{X}
on
\Sigma
(Definition
2.1.3
).

•

A
rectangle

R
on
\Sigma
is an embedding
h
of
I\times J
into
\Sigma
, where
I
and
J
are two non-trivial closed segments of
{\mathbb{R}}
.
The
horizontal boundary
of
R
, denoted
\partial^{s}R
is the image of
I\times\partial J
and the
vertical boundary
of
R
, denoted
\partial^{u}R
is the image of
\partial I\times J
.

•

We will always suppose that a rectangle
R
is equipped with a pair of trivial transversal
{\mathcal{C}}^{0,1}
-foliation
(\zeta^{s},\zeta^{u})
, containing the trace of
({\mathcal{W}}^{s},{\mathcal{W}}^{u})
, invariant by
f
, and such that the horizontal boundary of
R
is formed by two leaves of
\zeta^{s}
and the vertical boundary of
R
is formed by two leaves of
\zeta^{u}
.

•

A horizontal subrectangle
H
of
R
is a rectangle contained in
R
such that
\partial^{u}H\subset\partial^{u}R
and
\partial^{s}H
is formed by two leaves of
\zeta^{s}
.
A
vertical subrectangle

V
of
R
is a rectangle contained in
R
such that
\partial^{s}V\subset\partial^{s}R
and
\partial^{u}V
is formed by two leaves of
\zeta^{u}
.

Definition 2.2.4

(
[
BB02
, Definition 2.1]
)
.

A
Markov partition
of
\Lambda_{s}
is the data
(\Sigma,R=\cup_{i}R_{i})
of a transverse local section
\Sigma
of
\Lambda_{s}
and a finite collection
R_{1},\dots,R_{n}
of foliated rectangles on
\Sigma
such that:

(1)

The union of rectangles
R_{i}
covers the set
\Lambda\cap\Sigma

(2)

For any
i
, the first return
f(R_{i})
of
R_{i}
on
\Sigma
is well defined.

(3)

For any
i,j
the intersection
f(R_{i})\cap R_{j}
has a finite number of components.
Each of these components is both a horizontal subrectangle of
f(R_{i})
and a vertical subrectangle of
R_{j}
.
Moreover, we require that this intersection be disjoint from the vertical boundary of
R_{j}
and the horizontal boundary of
f(R_{i})
.

(4)

For any
x\in R_{i}\cap f^{-1}(R_{j})
, any vector
v
tangent at
x
to the foliation
\zeta^{s}
is uniformly contracted by the differential
d_{x}f
and any vector
w
tangent at
x
to the foliation
\zeta^{u}
is uniformly expanded by the differential
d_{x}f
for a metric on
\Sigma
.

If
{\mathcal{R}}=(\Sigma,R)
is a Markov partition, we will always assume that we have renormalized
\tilde{X}
so that the first return time of
R
on
\Sigma
is equal to
1
.
We call
suspension neighborhood of
R
the union
{\mathcal{C}}=\cup_{t=0}^{1}\tilde{X}^{t}(R)
of the orbit segments between a point of
R
and its first return on
\Sigma
.

Lemma 2.2.5
.

Let
(P,X)
be a block,
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
a minimal continuation, and
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
the collection of periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
.
Then there exists a Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}=(\Sigma,R)
(in the sense of Definition
2.2.4
) of
\Lambda_{s}
such that
\Sigma
intersects each orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
at a single point
p_{i}
and the intersection of an orbit of
\tilde{X}
with the suspension neighborhood
{\mathcal{C}}
of
R
is connected.

Proof of Lemma
2.2.5
.

According to Lemma
1.2.8
, up to shrink the minimal continuation, we can embed
(P,X)
into a building block
(U,Y)
whose boundary
\partial U
is transverse to the vector field
Y
and which contains the minimal continuation
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
.
Then
\Lambda_{s}
is a locally maximal saddle hyperbolic invariant compact set of index
(1,1)
for the flow of
Y
and
U
contains a filtrating neighborhood of
\Lambda_{s}
for
Y
(Claim
1.4.6
).
The existence of a Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}=(\Sigma,R)
of
\Lambda_{s}
(in the sense of Definition
2.2.4
) for the flow of
Y
in
U
and such that the intersection of the orbit segments with the suspension neighborhood
{\mathcal{C}}
of
{\mathcal{R}}
is connected is shown in
[
BB02
, Proposition 2.8]
.
Up to shrink the section
\Sigma
, we can suppose that it is contained in the minimal continuation
\tilde{P}
.
Every orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
belongs to
\Lambda_{s}
(Lemma
2.1.12
), so up to cutting
\Sigma
and removing some connected components we can assume that
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
intersects
\Sigma
at a single point
p_{i}
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.2.2
.

Let
(P,X)
be a block and let
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
be the collection of periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
.
Up to do a small perturbation in
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
norm and use Proposition
2.2.3
, we can suppose that the vector field
X
is of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{2}
.
Let
{\mathcal{R}}=(R,\Sigma)
be a Markov partition of
\Lambda_{s}
on a transverse local section
\Sigma
given by Lemma
2.2.5
.
Denote
f\colon R\to\Sigma
the first return map and
(\zeta^{s},\zeta^{u})
the pair of
f
-invariant transverse foliations on
R
.
Any orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
intersects
\Sigma
at a single point
p_{i}
.
We will perturb the vector field
X
into a vector field
Y
arbitrarily
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-close and such that
\Sigma
is an affine section for
Y
.
Let
R^{n}
be the
order
n
refinement
of the partition
R
, defined by

R^{n}:=\underset{k=-n}{\overset{n}{\bigcap}}f^{k}R\subset\Sigma.

Let
\epsilon_{n}
be the diameter of the partition
R_{n}
, in other words the maximum of the diameter of the connected components of
R^{n}
.
The diameter
\epsilon_{n}
is bounded above (up to a multiplicative constant independent of
n
) by the maximum of the norm of
df^{n}
in the stable direction (tangent to
\zeta^{s}
) and of
df^{-n}
in the unstable direction (tangent to
\zeta^{u}
) on the rectangles, so
\epsilon_{n}\to 0
when
n\to+\infty
.
Let
R^{n}_{1},\dots,R^{n}_{k_{n}}
be the connected components of
R^{n}
.
For any
p\in R^{n}_{i}
, there exists a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-identification of the tangent space
T_{p}\Sigma
with the rectangle
R^{n}_{i}
.
More precisely, there exists
\varphi_{p}\colon R^{n}_{i}\to T_{p}\Sigma
, such that

•

\varphi_{p}
is a diffeomorphism on its image
V:=\varphi_{p}(R_{i}^{n})
which is a
\epsilon_{n}
-neighborhood of the origin
O
in
T_{p}\Sigma
, and maps
p
to
O
;

•

there exists a
{\mathcal{C}}^{0}
-identification
\chi_{i,p}=(x,y)\colon T_{p}\Sigma\to{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
such that the foliation
(\varphi_{p})_{*}\zeta^{u}
coincides with the vertical foliation
x=\operatorname{\textit{cst}}
and
(\varphi_{p})_{*}\zeta^{s}
coincides with the vertical foliation
y=\operatorname{\textit{cst}}
.

For any
n\geq 1
, the intersection
R_{(i,j)}^{n}:=R_{i}^{n}\cap f^{-1}(R_{j}^{n})
is connected, and for any pair
(i,j)
such that this intersection is nonempty we denote the corresponding restriction of
f
by

f_{n,(i,j)}\colon R_{i}^{n}\cap f^{-1}(R_{j}^{n})\to R_{j}^{n}.

Let
\{p_{n,(i,j)}\}_{(i,j)}
be a set of points of
\Sigma\cap\Lambda
such that
p_{n,(i,j)}\in R_{(i,j)}^{n}
, and such that if
R_{i,i}^{n}
is the rectangle intersecting the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, then
p_{n,(i,i)}=p_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
is the intersection point of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
with
\Sigma
.
Let
\hat{f}_{n,(i,j)}
be the conjugate of
d_{p_{n,(i,j)}}f
by
\varphi_{p_{n,(i,j)}}
and
\varphi_{f(p_{n,(i,j)})}
, in other words

(2.1)

\hat{f}_{n,(i,j)}:=\varphi_{f(p_{n,(i,j)})}^{-1}\circ d_{p_{n,(i,j)}}f\circ\varphi_{p_{n,(i,j)}}\colon R_{(i,j)}^{n}\to R_{j}^{n}

By construction, there exist coordinates
\chi_{i}=(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
on
R_{i}^{n}
and
\chi_{j}=(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
on
R_{j}^{n}
such that the map
\hat{f}_{n,(i,j)}
is affine in coordinates
\chi_{i}
and
\chi_{j}
, and the foliations
(\zeta^{s},\zeta^{u})
coincide with the horizontal and vertical foliations in these coordinates.

Claim 2.2.6
.

There exists
n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}
and
\hat{f}=\hat{f}_{n}\colon R\to\Sigma
which is a diffeomorphism onto its image, such that

(1)

\hat{f}
coincides with
\hat{f}_{n,(i,j)}
on
R_{(i,j)}^{n}
for each pair
(i,j)
;

(2)

\hat{f}
coincides with
f
on a neighborhood of the boundary of
R
;

(3)

\hat{f}
is arbitrarily
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-close to
f
for
n
large enough.

Proof.

Since
f
is
{\mathcal{C}}^{2}
, according to Equation (
2.1
), the map
\hat{f}_{n,(i,j)}
differs from
f_{n,(i,j)}
by a term uniformly proportional to
\epsilon_{n}^{2}
, i.e. there exists a constant
\operatorname{\textit{cst}}>0
such that for any
p\in R_{(i,j)}^{n}
,

|\hat{f}_{n,(i,j)}(p)-f_{n,(i,j)}(p)|\leq\operatorname{\textit{cst}}\epsilon_{n}^{2}.

For rectangles of diameter
\epsilon_{n}
, if the distance between the rectangles
R^{n}_{i}
in
\Sigma
is a
O(\epsilon_{n})
, we can set
\hat{f}=\hat{f}_{n,(i,j)}
on
R_{(i,j)}^{n}
and extend on
R
so as to reconnect this map with the map
f
on the boundary of
R
, remaining
\epsilon_{n}
-close to
f
in
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
topology.
It is therefore sufficient to check that the distance in
\Sigma
between the rectangles of a refinement
R^{n}
does not decrease too fast.
Since the expansion and contraction of the differential of
f
is uniform on a neighborhood of
\Lambda_{s}\cap\Sigma
, the distance in
\Sigma
between the rectangles is also of order
\epsilon_{n}
.
∎

The suspension of
\hat{f}
on
R
defines a flow generated by a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-vector field
Y
on a neighborhood of
\Lambda
in
\tilde{P}
, which coincides with
\tilde{X}
on a neighborhood of the boundary, and with first return
\hat{f}
on
\Sigma
.
Let
\tilde{Y}
denote the extension of
Y
by
\tilde{X}
on
\tilde{P}
.
Then
\tilde{Y}
is arbitrarily
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-close to
\tilde{X}
.
By Proposition
2.2.3
, there exists a block
(Q,Y)
isotopic to
(P,X)
among the orbit equivalent blocks in
\tilde{P}
, where
Y=\tilde{Y}|_{Q}
.
By construction, the flow of
Y
admits an affine section
\Sigma
of its maximal saddle invariant set
\Lambda_{s}
.
Indeed, there exists a cover of
\Sigma\cap\Lambda_{s}
, given by the collection of rectangles
R^{n}=\{R^{n}_{1},\dots,R^{n}_{k_{n}}\}
of a large enough refinement of a Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}
on
\Sigma
, and coordinates
\chi_{i}
on
R^{n}_{i}
such that the map
\hat{f}
restricted to
R_{(i,j)}^{n}=R^{n}_{i}\cap f^{-1}(R^{n}_{j})
is affine in coordinates
\chi_{i}
and
\chi_{j}
.
Hence the block
(Q,Y)
satisfies Proposition
2.2.2
.
∎

Remark 2.2.7
.

Note that one can apply Proposition
2.2.2
while preserving the multipliers of the periodic orbits contained in the boundary, which will be useful in the next subsection.
Indeed, in the neighbourhood of a periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
we have replaced the first return of
X
on
\Sigma
by its differential at the point
p_{i}={\mathcal{O}}_{i}\cap\Sigma
, which preserves the multipliers of the periodic orbits of the new flow in the boundary.
More precisely, using the notations from the proof, let
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
be a periodic orbit of
X
in
{\partial P}
, and let
R_{i}^{n}
be the rectangle of
R^{n}
intersecting
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
. Let
R_{(i,i)}^{n}=R^{n}_{i}\cap f^{-1}(R_{i}^{n})
.
According to Claim
2.2.6
the return map
\hat{f}
of the flow of
\tilde{Y}
on
R_{(i,i)}^{n}
coincides with the
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-conjugate of the differential of
f
in
p_{i}={\mathcal{O}}_{i}\cap\Sigma
, where
f
is the return map of the flow of
\tilde{X}
on
\Sigma
.
It follows that
p_{i}
is a fixed point of
\hat{f}
on
\Sigma
, contained in the boundary of
P
, and its multipliers are those of
d_{p}f
. The orbit equivalence between
X
and
Y
fixes the periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
, and the multipliers of these orbits for the flow of
X
and the flow of
Y
are the same.

2.3.
Multipliers of periodic orbits

In this subsection, we perturb the block by isotopy in its orbit equivalence class so that the multipliers of the periodic orbits contained in the boundary are
\{\frac{1}{2},2\}
.
More precisely, we show

Proposition 2.3.1
.

Let
(P,X)
be an affine building block.
There exists an affine block
(P_{1},X_{1})
isotopic to
(P,X)
among the orbit equivalent building blocks, such that the multipliers of the periodic orbits of
X_{1}
contained in
{\partial P}_{1}
are equal to
\{\frac{1}{2},2\}
.

Proof.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
be the collection of periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
.
Let
\Sigma
be an affine section of
(P,X)
, and
{\mathcal{R}}=(\Sigma,R)
a Markov partition of
\Lambda_{s}
in the sense of Definition
2.2.4
which satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
2.2.5
.
Up to shrink
R
, we assume that the Markov partition is contained in a domain of affine coordinates on
\Sigma
(Definition
2.1.4
, Item
1
).
The map
f\colon R\to\Sigma
is well defined and preserves a pair of transverse foliations
(\zeta^{s},\zeta^{u})
on
R
which are the horizontal and vertical foliations in affine coordinates.
These foliations are now of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
on
R
and contains the trace of the stable and unstable manifold of
\Lambda
.
Each orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
is associated with a single fixed point
p_{i}
of
f
and positive multipliers according to Claim
1.2.1
.
We want the eigenvalues of the differential
D_{p_{i}}f
to be equal to
\{\frac{1}{2},2\}
.
We will therefore continuously modify the derivative at the points
p_{i}
, for
i=1,\dots,n
.

Let
R/\zeta^{s}
be the quotient of the rectangles by the foliation
\zeta^{s}
.
The map
f\colon R\cap f^{-1}(R)\to R\cap f(R)
induces on
R/\zeta^{s}
a map
f^{u}\colon\cup_{k,l}I_{k}^{l}\to\cup_{k}I_{k}
, where
I_{1},\dots,I_{N}
are disjoint closed ordered intervals of
{\mathbb{R}}
, and
I_{k}^{1},\dots,I_{k}^{n_{k}}
are closed disjoint ordered strict subintervals of
I_{k}
, and the restriction of
f^{u}
to
I_{k}^{l}
is a uniformly expanding affine map in some
I_{i}
.
Let
p^{u}_{i}\in I_{k_{i}}^{l_{i}}
be the projection of the fixed point
p_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
of
f
. Then

Claim 2.3.2
.

For any
\lambda>1
, there exists a diffeomorphism
g^{u}=g^{u}_{\lambda}\colon\cup_{k,l}I_{k}^{l}\to\cup_{k}I_{k}
such that

(1)

g^{u}=f^{u}
on
I_{k}^{l}
for
(k,l)\neq(k_{i},l_{i})
,

(2)

the restriction
g^{u}\colon I_{k_{i}}^{l_{i}}\to I_{k_{i}}
is a uniformly expanding diffeomorphism which coincides with
f^{u}
near the boundary of
I_{k_{i}}^{l_{i}}
,

(3)

p_{i}
is a fixed point of
g^{u}
and
g^{u}{}^{\prime}(p)=\lambda
,

(4)

g^{u}_{\lambda}
depends continuously on
\lambda
and is conjugate to
f^{u}
by a homeomorphism
H\colon\cup_{k}I_{k}\to\cup_{k}I_{k}
, equal to the identity outside
I_{k_{i}}^{l_{i}}
and on the boundary of
I_{k_{i}}^{l_{i}}
.

Proof.

To simplify the notation, let
(k_{1},l_{1})=(1,1)
, and let
p=p_{1}
. The set
K:=\cap_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}(f^{u})^{n}(\cup_{k}I_{k})
is a Cantor set disjoint from the boundary of
I_{k}^{l}
, and
|I_{1}|>|I_{1}^{1}|
.
Let
V_{\lambda}
be a neighbourhood of
p
in
I_{1}^{1}
, whose boundary is disjoint from the boundary of
I_{1}^{1}
, small enough so that
|I_{1}|>|I_{1}^{1}|-(\lambda-1)|V_{\lambda}|
.
Then
g^{u}(x)=\lambda(x-p)+p
on
V_{\lambda}
.
Then
|I_{1}{\smallsetminus}g^{u}(V_{\lambda})|>|I_{1}^{1}{\smallsetminus}V_{\lambda}|
, so we can extend
g^{u}
on
I_{1}^{1}
into a uniformly expanding diffeomorphism which we still denote
g^{u}
, and which coincides with
f^{u}
on the boundary of
I_{1}^{1}
.
We can choose the neighbourhood
V_{\lambda}
which depends continuously on
\lambda
, and extend
g^{u}=g^{u}_{\lambda}
on
I_{1}^{1}
continuously into
\lambda
.
Then
g^{u}
and
f^{u}
are two expanding diffeomorphisms on
I_{1}^{1}\to I_{1}
, which coincide on the boundary of
I_{1}^{1}
. Let
H\colon\cup_{k}I_{k}{\smallsetminus}I_{1}^{1}\to\cup_{k}I_{k}{\smallsetminus}I_{1}^{1}
be a map equal to the identity.
There is a unique way of extending
H
on
I_{1}^{1}
so that we have a conjugation
H
between
f^{u}
and
g^{u}
.
We can repeat the same procedure on each fixed point
p^{u}_{i}
, by noticing that the map
g^{u}
obtained still satisfies the hypotheses of the fact, and each
p^{u}_{i}
is still a fixed point of
g^{u}
.
∎

Let
g^{u}
be the map given by Claim
2.3.2
for
\lambda=2
.
In the same way, when quotienting
R
by the foliation
\zeta^{u}
, the map
f^{-1}
induces an affine map
f^{s}\colon\cup_{k,l}J_{k}^{l}\to\cup_{k}J_{k}
which satisfies the hypotheses of Claim
2.3.2
.
Let
p^{s}_{i}
be the projection in
R/\zeta^{u}
of the fixed point
p_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
of
f:R\to\Sigma
on the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
There exists a map
g^{s}\colon\cup_{k,l}J_{k}^{l}\to\cup_{k}J_{k}
expanding on each
J_{k}^{l}
, with
g^{s}(J_{k}^{l})=f^{s}(J_{k}^{l})
for any pair
(k,l)
, and such that each
p^{s}_{i}\in J_{k_{i}}^{l_{i}}
is a fixed point of
g^{s}
with
(g^{s})^{\prime}(p^{s}_{i})=2
, and
g^{s}
is conjugate to
f^{s}
.
Since the foliations
(\zeta^{s},\zeta^{u})
are of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
, the product coordinates
R\to R/\zeta^{s}\times R/\zeta^{u}
are of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
and the product map
\left((g^{s})^{-1},g^{u}\right)
induces a diffeomorphism
g
of
R\cap f^{-1}(R)
with image in
R\cap f(R)
.
According to Claim
2.3.2
, Item
4
, the map
g
is isotopic to
f
via a continuous family of diffeomorphisms
f_{t}\colon R\cap f^{-1}(R)\to R\cap f(R)
, with
f_{0}=f
and
f_{1}=g
, satisfying:

•

each point
p_{i}\in\Sigma\cap{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
is a fixed point of
f_{t}
, and the multipliers of the differential of
d_{p_{i}}f_{1}=d_{p_{i}}g
are
\{\frac{1}{2},2\}
;

•

the map
f_{t}\colon R\cap f^{-1}(R)\to R\cap f(R)
is conjugate to
f
by a homeomorphism
h_{t}:R\to R
, equal to the identity on the edge of
R
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}}=\cup_{t=0}^{1}\tilde{X}^{t}R
be the suspension neighborhood of the Markov partition associated with
\tilde{X}
in
\tilde{P}
.
It is a neighbourhood of
\Lambda
, whose intersection with any orbit of
\tilde{X}
is connected (Lemma
2.2.5
), homeomorphic to the suspension manifold (with edge and corner)
R_{f}:=R\times[0,1]/(x,1)\sim(f(x),0)
by a homeomorphism which conjugates the flow of
\tilde{X}
with the suspension flow induced by
\partial_{s}
, where
s
is the coordinate on
[0,1]
.
Moreover, the suspension flow on
R_{f}
is topologically conjugate to the suspension flow on
R_{f_{t}}:=R\times[0,1]/(x,1)\sim(f_{t}(x),0)
.
We deduce that
R_{f_{t}}
is naturally embedded in
\tilde{P}
via a homeomorphism which maps
R_{f_{t}}
on
{\mathcal{C}}
and which maps the orbits of the flow of
\partial_{t}
of
R_{f_{t}}
on the orbits of the flow of a vector field
\tilde{X}_{t}
on
{\mathcal{C}}
whose first return on
R\subset{\mathcal{C}}
is equal to
f_{t}
, and which coincides with
\tilde{X}
on the boundary of
{\mathcal{C}}
.
The vector fields are orbit equivalent via a homeomorphism
h_{t}\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
, equal to the identity on the boundary of
{\mathcal{C}}
.

Extend
\tilde{X}_{t}
by
\tilde{X}
on the rest of the manifold, and we obtain a continuous family (for the
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-norm) of vector fields on
\tilde{P}
, which we still denote
\tilde{X}_{t}
.
As any orbit which exits
{\mathcal{C}}
never enters it again, the orbit equivalence
h_{t}
on
{\mathcal{C}}
extends by the identity into an orbit equivalence
h_{t}\colon\tilde{P}\to\tilde{P}
between
\tilde{X}_{t}
and
\tilde{X}
, with
h_{0}=\operatorname{Id}
.
Let
P_{t}=h_{t}^{-1}(P)
.
Up to make an isotopy along the flow of
\tilde{X}_{t}
, the surface
\partial P_{t}
is quasi-transverse to
\tilde{X}_{t}
.
The family
(P_{t},X_{t})
is an isotopy of orbit equivalent building blocks, where
X_{t}=\tilde{X}_{t}|_{P_{t}}
.
Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{*,t}
be the collection of periodic orbits of
X_{t}
contained in
{\partial P}_{t}
.
Each orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i,t}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*,t}
intersects the section
\Sigma
at a single point
p_{i,t}=p_{i}
.
By the properties of
f_{1}=g
, each
p_{i}
is a fixed point of
p
with multipliers
\{\frac{1}{2},2\}
. It follows that the periodic orbits of
X_{1}
contained in
{\partial P}_{1}
have multipliers
\{\frac{1}{2},2\}
.

The section
\Sigma
is a priori no longer an affine section for the flow of
X_{1}
, because we have modified the first return map on the neighbourhood of the maximal invariant set.
We can apply Proposition
2.2.2
and Remark
2.2.7
to the block
(P_{1},X_{1})
to obtain a block
(P^{\prime}_{1},X^{\prime}_{1})
, isotopic to
(P_{1},X_{1})
among the orbit equivalent blocks, which admits an affine section and whose periodic orbits contained in the boundary have multipliers
\{\frac{1}{2},2\}
.
This block satisfies Proposition
2.3.1
.
∎

2.4.
Pair of affine invariant foliation

In this subsection, we show that we can foliate a filled affine block
(P,X)
with a pair of affine invariant foliations
({\mathcal{G}}^{s},{\mathcal{G}}^{u})
(Definition
2.1.4
, Item
3
).

Proposition 2.4.1
.

Let
(P,X)
be a filled affine building block, and
\Sigma
an affine section of
(P,X)
.
There exists a pair of affine invariant foliations

({\mathcal{G}}^{s},{\mathcal{G}}^{u})
, in the sense of Definition
2.1.4
, Item
3
.

We repeat here the idea of the proof of
[
BBY17
, Lemma 5.6, Proposition 5.7]
.
The key points of the proof are the following.

•

We can push the horizontal and vertical foliations induced by the affine coordinate system on the section
\Sigma
, in order to obtain a pair of transverse
X^{t}
-invariant foliations on an invariant neighborhood of
\Lambda
in
P
, which extends the stable and unstable manifolds of
\Lambda
(Lemma
2.4.2
).
These foliations will naturally satisfy Item
3b
of Definition
2.1.4
.

•

We then show that there is a (unique) way to complete these foliations into a pair of affine invariant foliations on
P
. We use the fact that the boundary lamination is filling.

Affine invariant foliations on a neighborhood of
\Lambda

Lemma 2.4.2
.

Let
(P,X)
be an affine building block equipped with an affine section
\Sigma
. There exist an invariant neighborhood
{\mathcal{U}}
of
\Lambda
, endowed with a pair of smooth transverse invariant 2-dimensional foliations
(\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{s},\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{u})
, containing
({\mathcal{W}}^{s},{\mathcal{W}}^{u})
as sublaminations, and such that the trace on
\Sigma
are the vertical and horizontal foliations in the affine coordinate.

Proof.

Let
(\zeta^{s},\zeta^{u})
be the pair of transverse foliations, invariant by the first return map
f
, which coincide with the horizontal and vertical foliations on
\Sigma
in the affine coordinate system on a neighborhood of
\Lambda_{s}\cap\Sigma
in
\Sigma
.
According to Lemma
2.2.5
, there exists a Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}=(\Sigma,R)
on
\Sigma
in a minimal continuation
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
such that the intersection of the orbits of the flow of
\tilde{X}
with the suspension neighborhood
{\mathcal{C}}=\cup_{t=0}^{1}\tilde{X}^{t}(R)
associated with the partition is connected, and such that (up to take a refinement of the partition)
R
is contained in the domain of affine coordinates of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
on
\Sigma
.
Recall that the map
f\colon R\to\Sigma
is well defined and coincides with time 1 of the flow
\tilde{X}
restricted to
R
.
Consider the union
{\mathcal{U}}
of the flow-saturated set of
{\mathcal{C}}
with the basins of
{\mathfrak{A}}
and
{\mathfrak{R}}
.
It is an invariant neighborhood of
\Lambda
, hence of
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cup{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
.
By pushing the horizontal and vertical foliations in affine coordinates on
R
by the flow of
X
, containing the trace of the stable and unstable lamination, we obtain two foliations
\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{s}
and
\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{u}
of dimension
2
and class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
, transverse to each other,
X^{t}
-invariant, which extend
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
and
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
on
{\mathcal{U}}
.
By the
\lambda
-lemma, they continuously extend
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
and
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
on
{\mathcal{U}}
.
They satisfy Lemma
2.4.2

∎

Adapted neighborhoods

Let
(P,X)
a building block satisfying Lemma
2.4.2
.
The trace
U={\mathcal{U}}\cap{\partial P}
is a neighborhood of the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
foliated by the trace of
\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{u}
and
\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{s}
on
{\partial P}
.
Denote
(\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{in}},\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{u,\mathrm{in}})
the trace of
(\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{s},\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{u})
on
P^{\mathrm{in}}
and
(\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{out}},\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{u,\mathrm{out}})
the trace on
P^{\mathrm{out}}
.
We adapt the notions of
[
BBY17
, Sections 5.3]
.
The definitions of Item
2
and
3
are not used in the subsection but will be useful later.

Definition 2.4.3

(Adapted neighborhoods)
.

(1)

Let
U
be a neighborhood of
{\mathcal{L}}
on
{\partial P}
.
We say that a neighborhood
U
of
{\mathcal{L}}
in
{\partial P}
is an
adapted neighborhood of
{\mathcal{L}}
if the closure of connected components
{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{U}}
are squares
C
diffeomorphic to
[0,1]^{2}
with two sides on leaves of
{\mathcal{G}}^{s,*}
and two sides on leaves of
\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{u,*}
with
*=\mathrm{in}
or
\mathrm{out}
depending on whether
C
is contained in
P^{\mathrm{in}}
or
P^{\mathrm{out}}
.
We say that
C
is an
adapted square
.

(2)

Let
U^{\mathrm{in}}
be a neighborhood of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
on
P^{\mathrm{in}}
.
We say that
U^{\mathrm{in}}
is an
adapted neighborhood of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
if the closure of connected components
P^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}U^{\mathrm{in}}
are either squares diffeomorphic to
[0,1]^{2}
, band
{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,1]
or half-band
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times[0,1]
, with horizontal sides on leaves of
{\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{in}}
and vertical sides (when well defined) on leaves of
\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{u,\mathrm{in}}
, or ends (when well defined) accumulating on
\partial P^{\mathrm{in}}={\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
We say that
C
is an
adapted square, band or half-band
.

(3)

Let
U^{\mathrm{out}}
be a neighborhood of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
on
P^{\mathrm{out}}
.
We say that
U^{\mathrm{out}}
is an
adapted neighborhood of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
if the closure of connected components
P^{\mathrm{out}}{\smallsetminus}U^{\mathrm{out}}
are either squares diffeomorphic to
[0,1]^{2}
, band diffeomorphic
{\mathbb{R}}\times[0,1]
or half-band
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times[0,1]
, with horizontal sides on leaves of
\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{u,\mathrm{out}}
and vertical sides (when well defined) on leaves of
{\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{out}}
, or ends (when well defined) accumulating on
\partial P^{\mathrm{out}}={\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
We say that
C
is an
adapted square, band or half-band.

In order to point out the difference between adapted neighborhoods of
{\mathcal{L}}
and of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
we pictured the neighborhoods inside a connected component
B
of
P^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
in the case of Item
1
and in the case of Item
2
in Figure
18
.

Figure 18
.
Adapted square of an adapted neighborhood
U
of
{\mathcal{L}}
(left) and adapted half-band of an adapted neighborhood
U^{\mathrm{in}}
of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
(right)

One can easily show using the fact that
{\mathcal{L}}
is a filling quasi-Morse-Smale lamination that

Lemma 2.4.4
.

Adapted neighborhoods of
{\mathcal{L}}
(resp.
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
, resp.
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
) are a basis of neighborhoods of
{\mathcal{L}}
(resp.
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
, resp.
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
) in
{\partial P}
(resp.
P^{\mathrm{in}}
, resp.
P^{\mathrm{out}}
).

Global affine invariant foliations

Proof of Proposition
2.4.1
.

Let
(P,X)
a filled affine building block, and suppose that
(P,X)
satisfied Lemma
2.4.2
.
Up to shrink
{\mathcal{U}}
, we can suppose according to Lemma
2.4.4
that
\partial{\mathcal{U}}\cap{\partial P}
is an adapted neighborhood of
{\mathcal{L}}
(Definition
2.4.3
).
The complementary
P^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{U}}
is a finite number of adapted squares
C_{i}
in
P^{\mathrm{in}}
.
We complete the pair of foliations trivially on each square
C_{i}
of the complementary
P^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{U}}
by the horizontal and vertical foliations in the horizontal and vertical coordinate.
We obtain a pair of transverse smooth foliations
{\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{in}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}^{u,\mathrm{in}}
on
P^{\mathrm{in}}
, respectively extending
\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{in}}
and
\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{u,\mathrm{in}}
.
Any point of a component
C_{i}
is uniformly far from the entrance lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
.
Any future orbit of a point of
C_{i}
intersects
P^{\mathrm{out}}
at a single point, and the orbit segment is uniformly bounded in length.
There exists
X^{\prime}
a re-normalization of
X
such that the
X^{\prime}
-orbit of each point of
C_{i}
intersects transversely
P^{\mathrm{out}}
in time
t=1
.
It follows that the orbit
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}=\cup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}X^{t}(C_{i})
of
C_{i}
by the flow of
X
in
P
is homeomorphic to
C_{i}\times[0,1]
, on which the vertical field
\partial/\partial t
is orbit equivalent to
X
.
We push the foliations
{\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{in}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}^{u,\mathrm{in}}
of
C_{i}
by the flow of
X
in each
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
cylinder and we obtain two transverse foliations
{\mathcal{G}}^{s}
and
{\mathcal{G}}^{u}
of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
on
P
.
These foliations are
X^{t}
-invariant, and extend the foliations
\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{s}
and
\hat{\mathcal{G}}^{u}
respectively.
They satisfy Proposition
2.4.1
.
∎

2.5.
Incomplete gluing maps and strong isotopy

In this section, we show that we can obtain a weakened version of a gluing map on a building block
(P,X)
, isotopic to a building block
(P_{0},X_{0})
equipped with a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map
\varphi_{0}
, and
which satisfies a relation compatible with the strong isotopy relation of triples.
Let us define formally.

Definition 2.5.1

(Incomplete gluing map)
.

Let
(P,X)
be a building block,
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
the periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
,
P^{\mathrm{in}}
the entrance boundary and
P^{\mathrm{out}}
the exit boundary of
(P,X)
.
An
incomplete gluing map of
(P,X)
is a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-involution
\hat{\varphi}\colon{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}\to{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
such that

(1)

there exists a partition
{\partial P}=\partial_{1}P\,\sqcup\,\partial_{2}P
where
\partial_{1}P
and
\partial_{2}P
are unions of connected components of
{\partial P}
, and such that
\hat{\varphi}(\partial_{1}P{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*})=\partial_{2}P{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
,

(2)

\hat{\varphi}(P^{\mathrm{out}})=P^{\mathrm{in}}

In particular, the restriction of a gluing map
\varphi
of a block
(P,X)
to the complementary of the periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
in
{\partial P}
is an incomplete gluing map, but the interest of introducing this notion is to consider diffeomorphisms that do not extend to
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
We consider the following relation on incomplete gluing maps.

Definition 2.5.2

(Strongly isotopic incomplete gluing maps)
.

We will say that two incomplete gluing maps
\hat{\varphi}_{0}
of
(P_{0},X_{0})
and
\hat{\varphi}_{1}
of
(P_{1},X_{1})
are strongly isotopic if there exists a block isotopy
\{(P_{t},X_{t})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
and a continuous family of homeomorphisms
\{h_{t}\colon{\partial P}_{0}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{0,*}\to{\partial P}_{t}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{t,*}\}_{t\in[0,1]}
, such that

(1)

h_{0}=\operatorname{Id}

(2)

h_{t}
maps the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{0,*}
on the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{t,*}

(3)

h_{1}
maps the lamination
(\varphi_{0})_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{0}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{0,*})
on the lamination
(\varphi_{1})_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{1}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{1,*})
.

Remark 2.5.3
.

We recover Item
3
of Definition
1.4.9
of strongly isotopic triples, i.e. if
(P_{0},X_{0},\varphi_{0})
and
(P_{1},X_{1},\varphi_{1})
are two strongly isotopic triples in the sense of Definition
1.4.9
, then the restriction of
\varphi_{0}
and the restriction of
\varphi_{1}
are strongly isotopic incomplete gluing maps.
Intuitively, this relation ensures that the intersection patterns of the laminations are the same.

We will consider incomplete gluing maps which achieve a “good” lamination intersection pattern.
More precisely

Definition 2.5.4

(Strongly transverse incomplete gluing map)
.

Let
(P,X)
be a building block and
\hat{\varphi}
an incomplete gluing map of
(P,X)
.
We say that it is a
strongly transverse incomplete gluing map
if
({\mathcal{L}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*},\hat{\varphi}_{*}({\mathcal{L}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}))
is a pair of strongly transverse laminations on
(P,X)
(Definition
1.3.5
).

In particular, the restriction of a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map of a block
(P,X)
to the complementary of the periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
in
{\partial P}
is a strongly transverse incomplete gluing map.
We show the following

Proposition 2.5.5
.

Let
(P,X)
a filled affine block
and
(P_{0},X_{0})
a block isotopic to
(P,X)
among orbit equivalent blocks.
Let
\hat{\varphi}_{0}
be a strongly transverse incomplete gluing map of
(P_{0},X_{0})
.
Then there exists a strongly transverse incomplete gluing map
\hat{\varphi}
of
(P,X)
strongly isotopic to
\hat{\varphi}_{0}
.

Proof.

Let
\{(P_{t},X_{t})\}_{t\in[0,1]}
be the isotopy of orbit equivalent blocks, with
(P_{1},X_{1})=(P,X)
.
Let
H_{t}:P_{0}\to P_{t}
be a continuous family of orbit equivalences between the flow of
X_{0}
and the flow of
X_{t}
.
Let
\hat{\varphi}
be the conjugate of
\hat{\varphi}_{0}
by the orbit equivalence
H
.
It satisfies Definition
2.5.1
, but is only continuous.
We will say that it is a
topological incomplete gluing map
of
(P,X)
.
There exists a continuous family of homeomorphisms

h_{t}\colon{\partial P}_{0}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*,0}\to{\partial P}_{t}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*,t}

(this is the restriction of the
H_{t}
orbit equivalence) which maps the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{0}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*,0}
on the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{t}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{t,*}
and such that
h_{1}
maps the lamination
(\hat{\varphi}_{0})_{*}({\mathcal{L}}_{0}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*,0})
on the lamination
(\hat{\varphi}_{1})_{*}({\mathcal{L}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*})
.
The map
\hat{\varphi}_{1}
satisfies the definition of strong isotopy (Definition
2.5.2
) of incomplete gluing maps, except that it is not differentiable.
Let
({\mathcal{G}}^{s},{\mathcal{G}}^{u})
a pair of affine invariant foliations and
{\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{in}}
,
{\mathcal{G}}^{u,\mathrm{out}}
the trace on
P^{\mathrm{in}}
and
P^{\mathrm{out}}
respectively (Proposition
2.1.9
).
We can smooth the image of the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
in
P^{\mathrm{in}}
in small charts
\{D_{i}\}_{i}
for the foliation
{\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{in}}
, so that in any chart
D_{i}
, every leaf of
(\hat{\varphi}_{1})_{*}{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}\cap D_{i}
intersect every leaf of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}\cap D_{i}
at a unique point in
D_{i}
, which is possible by (topological) transversality.
We can do the smoothing so that the new smooth lamination
{\mathcal{K}}
is smoothly transverse to
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
and satisfies the same previous property of intersection in
D_{i}
.
It follows that the two laminations are strongly isotopic, in the sense that they are isotopic via homeomorphism of
P^{\mathrm{in}}
preserving
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
.
Now, there exist a smooth map
\hat{\varphi}
of
{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
,
{\mathcal{C}}^{0}
-close to
\hat{\varphi}_{1}
, which map
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
to
{\mathcal{K}}
.
By construction, this diffeomorphism is an incomplete gluing map strongly isotopic to
\hat{\varphi}
, hence to
\hat{\varphi}_{0}
.
∎

2.6.
Straightening the boundary of a building block

In this subsection, we will “straighten” the boundary of the block. More precisely, we show

Proposition 2.6.1
.

Let
(P,X)
an affine block and
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
a minimal continuation.
There exists a building block
(P_{1},X_{1})
, such that
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
is a minimal continuation of
(P_{1},X_{1})
, and such that for any orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
of
X_{1}
in
{\partial P}_{1}
, there exists a
linearizing coordinate system
({\mathcal{V}}_{i},\xi_{i}=(x,y,\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}})
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
for the flow of
\tilde{X}
, compatible with the affine coordinate system
(D_{i},\chi_{i})
of the section
\Sigma
, and in which
{\partial P}_{1}
coincides with the diagonal
\{x=y\}
. More precisely:

(1)

if
D_{i}\subset\Sigma
is the disk which intersects
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
, then it coincides with
\{\theta=0\}
, and the coordinates
\xi_{i}
and
\chi_{i}
coincide on
D_{i}

(2)

P_{1}
coincides with the region
\{y\geq x\}
in coordinates
\xi_{i}
on
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}

This block is unique up to isotopy along the flow.

We refer to Figure
16
.

Corollary 2.6.2
.

If
(P,X)
satisfies Item
1
,
2
,
3
of Definition
2.1.4
, then the block
(P_{1},X_{1})
given by Proposition
2.6.1
is a normalized block isotopic to
(P,X)
.

Proof of Corollary
2.6.2
.

The isotopy is a consequence of the fact that the blocks
(P_{0},X_{0})
and
(P_{1},X_{1})
have a common minimal continuation and of Proposition
1.4.4
, Item
2
.
The block
(P,X)
satisfies Items
1
,
2
and
3
of Definition
2.1.4
, hence so does
(P_{1},X_{1})
by the property of having a common minimal continuation.
According to Proposition
2.6.1
,
(P_{1},X_{1})
satisfies Item
4
.
The block
(P_{1},X_{1})
thus satisfies Definition
2.1.4
of a normalized block.
∎

Figure 19
.
Straightening the boundary of the block
(P,X)
in a minimal continuation
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
.

Proof of Proposition
2.6.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
be the collection of periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
.
For each orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
, let
D_{i}
be the unique disk which intersects the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
with affine coordinates
\chi_{i}=(x,y)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
(Definition
2.1.4
, Item
1
)
Let
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
be a tubular neighborhood of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
contained in the suspension neighborhood of
D_{i}
, in other words contained in
\cup_{t=0}^{1}\tilde{X}^{t}(D_{i})
(we assume that we have renormalized the vector field
\tilde{X}
such that every disk
D_{i}
returns on
\Sigma
in time
1
).
Let
\xi_{i}=(x,y,\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
be a linearizing coordinate system of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
on
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
such that
D_{i}
coincides with
\{\theta=0\}
in
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
and
\xi_{i}
and
\chi_{i}
coincide on
D_{i}\cap{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.
Up to make a translation, we can suppose that
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}=\{x=y=0\}
.

\tilde{X}^{t}(x,y,\theta)=(2^{-t}x,2^{t}y,\theta+t)
.
Two components of
{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
adjacent in
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
are contained in two opposite quadrants of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
(Claim
1.2.1
), namely (up to reverse the orientation) the quadrants
\{x\geq 0,y\geq 0\}
and
\{x\leq 0,y\leq 0\}
.
We can thus define a smooth surface
S
, which coincides with
{\partial P}
outside the neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
, and such that in each
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
, we have

•

S
coincides with the diagonal annulus of equation
\{x=y\}
in an
\epsilon
-neighborhood of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
strictly contained in
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
which we denote
{\mathcal{V}}_{\epsilon}
.

•

S
is a annulus transverse to the vector field
\tilde{X}
in the annulus
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}\,{\smallsetminus}\,{\mathcal{V}}_{\epsilon}
, and coincides with
{\partial P}
in the neighborhood of the boundary of
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.

Then
S
is a closed surface, quasi-transverse to the vector field
\tilde{X}
, and cuts in
\tilde{P}
a submanifold
P_{1}
which coincides with
P
outside the union of the neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.
If we denote
X_{1}
the restriction of
\tilde{X}
to
P_{1}
, then the pair
(P_{1},X_{1})
is a building block with minimal continuation
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
.
By construction, it satisfies Proposition
2.6.1
.
If
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
is another block which satisfies the proposition, we can make a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
change of coordinate to get back to the same linearizing coordinate system
({\mathcal{V}}_{i},\xi_{i})
.
The boundaries
{\partial P}^{\prime}
and
{\partial P}_{1}
in this neighborhood are annuli transverse to the stable and unstable manifolds of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
contained in the same two opposite quadrants, and which coincide in their boundary.
We deduce that they are isotopic along the flow of
\tilde{X}
in
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.
∎

Strongly isotopic incomplete gluing maps

In this paragraph, we explain how to obtain a strongly transverse incomplete gluing map (Definitions
2.5.1
and
2.5.4
) on a block
(P,X)
having a common minimal continuation with a block
(P_{0},X_{0})
equipped with a strongly quasi-transverse incomplete gluing map
\hat{\varphi}_{0}
, and so as to satisfy the strong isotopy relation (Definition
2.5.2
).
More precisely:

Lemma 2.6.3
.

Let
(P,X)
and
(P_{0},X_{0})
be two blocks with a common minimal continuation
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
.
Let
\hat{\varphi}_{0}
be a strongly transverse incomplete gluing map of
(P_{0},X_{0})
.
Then there exists a strongly transverse incomplete gluing map
\hat{\varphi}
of
(P,X)
strongly isotopic to
\hat{\varphi}_{0}
.

Remark 2.6.4
.

Even if the initial incomplete gluing map
\hat{\varphi}_{0}
extends into a gluing map, this will not be the case for the incomplete gluing map
\hat{\varphi}
obtained by Lemma
2.6.3
.
Typically, one will obtain
\hat{\varphi}
by pushing
\hat{\varphi}_{0}
by an isotopy along the flow for a time which may tend to infinity as one approaches the periodic orbits contained in the boundary.

Proof.

According to Proposition
1.4.4
, there exists a continuous family
(P_{t},X_{t})
of building blocks having a common minimal continuation
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
, and which joins
(P_{0},X_{0})
to
(P,X)
.
These blocks all coincide outside a union
{\mathcal{V}}_{*}
of linearizing neighborhoods of the periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
of
X_{0}
contained in
{\partial P}_{0}
.
There exists
H_{t}\colon{\partial P}_{0}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}\to{\partial P}_{t}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
a unique isotopy along the orbits of
X_{0}
trivial on the complementary of
{\mathcal{V}}_{*}
.
It does not extend in general along the orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
The family

\hat{\varphi}_{t}=(H_{t})_{*}\hat{\varphi}\colon{\partial P}_{t}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}\to{\partial P}_{t}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}

is a continuous family of incomplete gluing maps of
(P_{t},X_{t})
.
Since the isotopy
\{H_{t}\}_{t}
preserves the stable and unstable manifolds of the flow of
\tilde{X}
, we deduce that the incomplete gluing maps
\hat{\varphi}:=\hat{\varphi}_{1}
and
\hat{\varphi}_{0}
are strongly isotopic in the sense of Definition
2.5.2
.
Since the isotopy is of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
, the strong transversality of
\hat{\varphi}
leads to the strong transversality of
\hat{\varphi}
.
∎

2.7.
Normalization of gluing map

In this subsection,

(P,X)
denotes a normalized filled block (Definition
1.2.14
and
2.1.4
).
We consider a strongly transverse incomplete gluing map
\hat{\varphi}\colon{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}\to{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
Suppose that
\hat{\varphi}
is strongly isotopic to the restriction of a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map
\varphi_{0}
(in the sense of Definition
2.5.2
)

7
7

7

\hat{\varphi}
does not extend a priori to
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, but is strongly isotopic to an incomplete gluing map which extends to a gluing map
\varphi_{0}
.
Note that
\varphi_{0}
is not in general a gluing map of
(P,X)
, but only a gluing map of a block
(P_{0},X_{0})
isotopic to
(P,X)
.
Typically, the block
P_{0}
is obtained by pushing
\partial P
along the orbits of the flow for an infinite time
.
The block
(P,X)
is provided with a pair of affine invariant foliations
({\mathcal{G}}^{s},{\mathcal{G}}^{u})
.
We denote
({\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{out}},{\mathcal{G}}^{u,\mathrm{out}})
the pair of induced foliations on
P^{\mathrm{out}}
and
({\mathcal{G}}^{s,\mathrm{in}},{\mathcal{G}}^{u,\mathrm{in}})
the pair of induced foliations on
P^{\mathrm{in}}
.
