) but it follows from Proposition
1.4.12
, Item
3
that the combinatorial type of the bifoliation which extends the pair
(\varphi^{\prime}_{*}{\mathcal{L}}_{X^{\prime}},{\mathcal{L}}_{X^{\prime}})
and of the bifoliation which extends the pair
(\varphi_{*}{\mathcal{L}}_{X},{\mathcal{L}}_{X})
are equal.
It follows that the combinatorial type of the bifoliation of
({\mathcal{F}}_{1},{\mathcal{F}}_{2})
is equal to
\sigma
.
∎

8.
Embedding a filled block in an Anosov flow

The goal of this section is to show Theorem
D
.
We recall that a block is said to be
filled
if the boundary lamination is filling.
We refer to Section
1
for the definitions.
In this section, we consider orientable blocks.

Theorem 3

(Theorem
D
)
.

For any (transitive) orientable, filled block
(P,X)
, there exists a (transitive) Anosov vector field
Z
on a closed orientable 3-manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
, such that
(P,X)
is embedded in
({\mathcal{M}},Z)
.
More precisely, there exists a finite collection of incompressible tori
{\mathcal{T}}
embedded in
{\mathcal{M}}
, quasi-transverse to
Z
, such that the closure of one connected component of
{\mathcal{M}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{T}}
is a compact submanifold diffeomorphic to
P
and such that the restriction of
Z
on
P
is orbit equivalent to
X
.

Let us summarize the proof.
First, we notice that for any quasi-Morse-Smale foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
, there exists a quasi-Morse-Smale foliation
{\mathcal{G}}
quasi-transverse to
{\mathcal{F}}
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
(Lemma
8.1.1
).
For a given block
(P,X)
, we associate to each (quasi-transverse) boundary component
T_{k}
of
{\partial P}
a block
(N_{k},Y_{k})
given by Proposition
7.0.2
, whose filling boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y_{k}}
extends to a foliation topologically equivalent to a foliation quasi-transverse to the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
of
(P,X)
on
T_{k}
.
The block
(P,X)
and
(N_{k},Y_{k})
are glued together along the boundary
T_{k}
by a strongly quasi-transverse (partial) gluing map which reverses the orientation, and we proceed in this way for each boundary component
T_{k}
.
Up to make a strong isotopy of the triples, the Gluing Theorem
1
tells us that the vector fields of the different blocks induce an Anosov vector field on the quotient (orientable) manifold, and transitive according to the criterion of Proposition
6.4.2
.

8.1.
Construction of a quasi-transverse foliation

A first step is to show that for any quasi-Morse-Smale foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
, there exists a quasi-Morse-Smale foliation
{\mathcal{G}}
quasi-transverse to
{\mathcal{F}}
on
{\mathbb{T}}^{2}
.

Lemma 8.1.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
be a quasi-Morse-Smale foliation on the torus
T
.
Then there exists a quasi-Morse-Smale foliation
{\mathcal{G}}
on
T
such that
{\mathcal{G}}
is quasi-transverse to
{\mathcal{F}}
.

Proof.

Let
\Gamma_{\mathcal{F}}
denote the compact leaves of
{\mathcal{F}}
,
\Gamma_{*}
the marked compact leaves of
{\mathcal{F}}
, and
T^{\mathrm{in}}\cup T^{\mathrm{out}}
the (in,out)-splitting of
T
for
{\mathcal{F}}
.
Let
A
be the closure of a
T\setminus\Gamma_{\mathcal{F}}
.
Suppose that this connected component belongs to
T^{\mathrm{in}}
.
Let
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathcal{F}}
be a connected component of
\partial A
.
It is a compact leaf of
\Gamma_{\mathcal{F}}
oriented by the dynamical orientation (Definition
7.2.1
, Remark
7.2.3
), such that the holonomy of the oriented leaf
\gamma
of the foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
is expanding.
Let
N=N(\gamma)
be a small collar neighborhood of
\gamma
in
\partial A
and
(x,\theta):N\to[0,1]\times{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
coordinates on
A
such that
\gamma=\{x=0\}
is oriented in the positive direction of
\theta
.
Two foliations whose holonomy is contracting are topologically equivalent, so we can assume that the direction of
{\mathcal{F}}
is given by
dx+xd\theta=0
.
We distinguish two cases:

(1)

If
\gamma\in\Gamma_{*}
is a marked leaf of
{\mathcal{F}}
, we define
{\mathcal{G}}
on
N
by the foliation of direction
dx-xd\theta=0
.

(2)

If
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\mathcal{F}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma_{*}
is an unmarked compact leaf of
{\mathcal{F}}
, we define
{\mathcal{G}}
on
N
by the direction
d\theta-xdx=0
.

We do a similar construction on a collar neighborhood
N(\gamma^{\prime})
of the second boundary component
\gamma^{\prime}
of
\partial A
.
We obtain a foliation
{\mathcal{G}}
on a collar neighborhood
V=N(\gamma)\cup N(\gamma^{\prime})
of
\partial A
in
A
.
Choose the metric
\|\cdot\|^{2}=dx^{2}+d\theta^{2}
on
N(\gamma)
and
N(\gamma^{\prime})
.
Then by construction,
{\mathcal{G}}
is orthogonal for this metric to
{\mathcal{F}}
on the boundary of
V
, and we extend
{\mathcal{G}}
on
A
by the foliation defined by the vector field
X^{\prime}
orthogonal to a vector field
X
generating the foliation
{\mathcal{F}}
in the metric
\|\cdot\|
.
By density of Morse-Smale vector fields, there exists a Morse-Smale vector field
X^{\prime\prime}
close to
X^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
topology on
A
, which coincides with
X^{\prime}
on a neighborhood of
\partial A
.
This vector field defines a foliation
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
on
A
, which contains a finite number of compact leaves, all of which have an orientation such that the holonomy is expanding (this is the dynamical orientation), and such that
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
is transverse to
{\mathcal{F}}
on the interior of
A
(transversality is an open property).
We thus construct a foliation
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}_{k}
on each of the connected components
A_{k}
of
T{\smallsetminus}\Gamma_{{\mathcal{F}}}
, all of which reconnect into a foliation
{\mathcal{G}}^{\prime}
on
T
, which is a quasi-Morse-Smale foliation quasi-transverse to
{\mathcal{F}}
on
T
.
∎

8.2.
Proof of Theorem
D

Proof of Theorem
3
.

Let
(P,X)
be a transitive filled orientable building block.
Let
{\mathcal{L}}
be the boundary lamination of
(P,X)
.
We denote
T_{1},\dots,T_{n}
the connected components of
(P,X)
, and assume to begin with that none of them is transverse to the vector field
X
.
For each
k=1,\dots,n
, let
{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
be a quasi-Morse-Smale foliation on
T_{k}
containing
{\mathcal{L}}
as sublamination (Lemma
7.2.4
).
Let
{\mathcal{G}}_{k}
be a quasi-Morse-Smale foliation on
T_{k}
quasi-transverse to
{\mathcal{F}}_{k}
given by Lemma
8.1.1
.
Let
(N_{k},Y_{k})
be the building block given by Proposition
7.0.2
associated to two copies of the lamination
{\mathcal{G}}_{k}
on the oriented torus
\check{T}_{k}
(which denotes the torus
T_{k}
provided with the reversed orientation).
The boundary
\partial N_{k}
is formed by two connected components
T_{k,1}
and
T_{k,2}
and the boundary lamination of
(N_{k},Y_{k})
on
T_{k,i}
extends to a foliation topologically equivalent to the foliation
{\mathcal{G}}_{k}
on
\check{T}_{k}
.
We deduce that there exists a diffeomorphism
\varphi_{k,i}:T_{k,i}\to T_{k}
which maps the boundary lamination of
(N_{k},Y_{k})
to a lamination strongly quasi-transverse to the boundary lamination of
(P,X)
, and which reverses the orientation.
The product
\varphi_{k}
of the
\varphi_{k,i}
(made involutive) is a strongly quasi-transverse partial gluing map of the block formed from the union of two copies of
(P,X)
and the block
(N_{k},Y_{k})
, and which reverses the orientation.
The product
\varphi
of
\varphi_{k}
(made involutive) is a (non-partial) strongly quasi-transverse gluing map of the filled block
(M,Y)
formed from two copies of
(P,X)
and the union
\cup_{k}(N_{k},Y_{k})
(Figure
72
), and which reverses the orientation.
According to the Theorem
1
, up to modify
(M,Y,\varphi)
by strong isotopy, the quotient
{\mathcal{M}}:=M/\varphi
is a closed, orientable manifold, with a vector field
Z:=Y_{\varphi}
induced by
Y
which is Anosov.
The transitivity is deduced from the strong connectedness of the associated graph
G=G(M,Y,\varphi)
(Proposition
6.4.2
).
Indeed, the maximal invariant set of the block
(M,Y)
is the union of the maximal invariant sets of the two copies of
(P,X)
and the maximal invariant sets of the blocks
(N_{k},Y_{k})
.
Each of these sets is transitive, thus a basic piece.
The gluing map
\varphi_{k}
pairs a boundary component
T_{k}
of each of the two copies of
(P,X)
with one of the two boundary components of
(N_{k},Y_{k})
.
By assumption, each boundary component of
(P,X)
and
(N_{k},Y_{k})
contains a non-zero number of periodic orbits, so the stable and unstable manifolds of the maximal invariant set of each of the blocks intersects each of the boundary components of the block.
It follows that the image of the entrance lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{k}
of
(N_{k},Y_{k})
intersects the exit boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{X}
of each of the two copies of
(P,X)
via the gluing map
\varphi
, and similarly the image of the exit lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{k}
of
(N_{k},Y_{k})
intersects the entrance boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{X}
of each of the two copies of
(P,X)
via the gluing map
\varphi
(all boundary laminations are filling).
We deduce that the maximal invariant set of the two copies of
(P,X)
and
(N_{k},Y_{k})
are connected by a cycle in the graph
G(M,Y,\varphi)
.
Since this result is true for all
k
, the graph
G
is strongly connected.
The block
P
is naturally embedded in
{\mathcal{M}}
in a submanifold whose boundary is the union of tori quasi-transverse to the Anosov vector field
Z
.
Finally, we know from
[
Bar95
]
and
[
Bru93
]
that any torus embedded in a quasi-transverse position in an Anosov flow is incompressible.

The proof generalizes to any
(P,X)
by using the results of
[
BBY17
]
to pairs the connected components that are transverse to the vector field.
∎

Figure 72
.
Gluing two copies of
(P,X)
and
(N_{k},Y_{k})

9.
Realize an abstract geometric type in a filled block

In this section we are interested in the dynamics in the neighborhood of the maximal invariant set of a filled building block.
All the manifolds we consider will be orientable.
We show Theorem
E
which gives a necessary and sufficient criterion for an orientable filled block to realize an
abstract geometric type
as the geometric type of a Markov partition of its maximal invariant set.
This will be done using a correspondence between building blocks and Béguin-Bonatti-Yu blocks given by operations of handles attaching and removal (Subsection
9.2
), and results from
[
BB02
]
and
[
BBV99
]
which give the existence and uniqueness of a canonical Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block (the
model
) which realizes an abstract geometric type.
We then describe a simple algorithmic method which allows us to check these criteria from the geometric type (Subsection
9.4
).
Let us start with some precise definitions.

Definition 9.0.1

(Model block)
.

An orientable building block
(U,X)
is said to be a
model block
if, denoting
\Lambda
the maximal invariant set and
{\mathcal{L}}
the boundary lamination of
(U,X)
, we have

(1)

X\pitchfork\partial U
,

(2)

any connected component of
U
intersects
\Lambda
,

(3)

\Lambda
does not contains neither attractors or repellers (we say that
\Lambda
is
saddle
),

(4)

any simple closed curve
c
embedded in
\partial U
and disjoint from
{\mathcal{L}}
is the boundary of a disk
D\subset\partial U
disjoint from
{\mathcal{L}}
.

In particular, a model block is a Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block.

Remark 9.0.2
.

This definition is compatible with the definition
[
BB02
, Definition 0.2]
of the model of the germ of a saddle saturated set
\Lambda
of a Smale vector field
X
on a compact orientable manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
of dimension 3.
A compact invariant set
\Lambda
for a vector field
X
is said to be
saddle saturated
if it is a hyperbolic set of index
(1,1)
, without attractor or repeller, and contains the intersection of its stable and unstable manifolds, in other words
\Lambda={\mathcal{W}}^{s}(\Lambda)\cap{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(\Lambda)
.
The
germ

[X,\Lambda]
is the equivalence class of the couple
(X,\Lambda)
for the relation
(X,\Lambda)\sim(X^{\prime},\Lambda^{\prime})
if there exists a neighborhood of
V
of
\Lambda
and
V^{\prime}
of
\Lambda^{\prime}
such that the vector fields
X
on
V
and
X^{\prime}
on
\Lambda^{\prime}
are orbit equivalent.
A
model of germ of a saddle saturated set

[X,\Lambda]
is then a pair
(U,Y)
where
U
is a compact orientable manifold with boundary of dimension 3, provided with a vector field
Y
transverse to the boundary, such that if
\Lambda_{Y}
is the maximal invariant set of the flow of
Y
in
U
, then
[Y,\Lambda_{Y}]=[X,\Lambda]
, and Items
2
and
4
of Definition
9.0.1
are satisfied.
We can indeed speak of boundary lamination of
(U,Y)
because
(U,Y)
is then a building block in the sense of Definition
1.1.2
.
A model block
(U,X)
according to Definition
9.0.1
is then a model of the germ
[X,\Lambda]
of its maximal invariant set.
In
[
BB02
, Theorem 0.3]
the authors show that the model of the germ of a saddle saturated set is unique up to orbit equivalence, which justifies the term of
model
.

We follow the definition of
[
BB02
, Definition 2.3 and Definition 2.4]
for the geometric type.

Definition 9.0.3

(Abstract geometric type)
.

An abstract geometric type
{\mathscr{T}}
is the data of:

•

an integer
n\in{\mathbb{N}}^{*}

•

for any
1\leq i\leq n
, the data of two integers
h_{i}
and
v_{i}
such that
\sum_{i}h_{i}=\sum_{i}v_{i}
,

•

a map
\phi
from

\{i\in\{1,\dots,n\},j\in\{1,\dots,h_{i}\}\}
to
\{k\in\{1,\dots,n\},l\in\{1,\dots,v_{k}\}\}\times\{\pm 1\}

which induces a bijection by forgetting the signs.

Let
{\mathcal{R}}=(\Sigma,R)
be a Markov partition in the sense of Definition
2.2.4
.
We consider partitions embedded in orientable manifolds, with a choice of orientation on
\Sigma
.
Then a choice of an orientation of the verticals of
{\mathcal{R}}
induces an orientation of the horizontals of
{\mathcal{R}}
.

Definition 9.0.4

(Geometric type of a Markov partition)
.

Let
{\mathcal{R}}=(\Sigma,R)
be a Markov partition with an orientation of verticals and horizontals, and let
f:R\to\Sigma
be the return map of the Markov partition.
The geometric type
{\mathscr{T}}
of
{\mathcal{R}}
is an abstract geometric type defined by

•

n
is the number of rectangle of
R

•

h_{i}
is the number of connected components of the intersection of
R_{i}\cap f^{-1}(R)
, these are horizontal subrectangles
H_{i}^{1},\dots H_{i}^{h_{i}}
of
R_{i}
, numbered in the order induced by the orientation of the verticals of
R_{i}

•

v_{k}
is the number of connected components of the intersection
R_{k}\cap f(R)
, they are vertical subrectangles
V_{k}^{1},\dots V_{k}^{v_{k}}
of
R_{k}
, numbered in the order induced by the orientation of the horizontals of
R_{k}

•

\phi(i,j)=((k,l),\epsilon)
if
f(H_{i}^{j})=V_{k}^{l}
, and
\epsilon=+
if the orientation of the verticals of
f(H_{i}^{j})
coincides with the orientation of the verticals of
V_{k}^{l}
, and
\epsilon=-
otherwise.

Example 9.0.5
.

The geometric type of the fake horseshoe (Figure
73
) is

{\mathscr{T}}=\{n=1,\ h_{1}=2,\ v_{1}=2,\ \ \phi(1,1)=(1,1,+),\,\phi(1,2)=(1,2,+)\}

Figure 73
.
Partition of the fake horseshoe

Theorem 9.0.6

(
[
BBV99
, Theorem 0.1]
and
[
BB02
, Theorem 0.4]
)
.

For any abstract geometric type
{\mathscr{T}}
, there exists a model block
(U,X)
, whose maximal invariant set admits a Markov partition of geometric type
{\mathscr{T}}
, and this one is unique up to orbit equivalence.
We say that
(U,X)
is
the model of the geometric type
{\mathscr{T}}
.

The aim of this section is to show the following main proposition.

Theorem 4

(Theorem
C
)
.

Let
{\mathscr{T}}
be an abstract geometric type.
There exists an orientable filled block
(P,X)
, admitting a Markov partition of geometric type
{\mathscr{T}}
if and only if the model
(U,Y)
of
{\mathscr{T}}
satisfies the following conditions;

(1)

\partial U
is a union of tori and spheres, each sphere contains exactly two open disks
D_{i}
and
D_{j}
disjoint from the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
, bounded by two distinct compact leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
;

(2)

{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
is a pre-foliation on the complementary
\partial U{\smallsetminus}\cup_{i}D_{i}
.

Such a block
(P,X)
is then unique up to isotopy.
Moreover, the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
is topologically equivalent on
\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}
to the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
on
U^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}\cup_{i}D_{i}
, and the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
is topologically equivalent on
\overline{P^{\mathrm{out}}}
to the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
on
U^{\mathrm{out}}{\smallsetminus}\cup_{i}D_{i}
.

The proof consists in going from a building block to a Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block (and vice versa) by handles attaching and removal.
We analyze the boundary lamination of these corresponding blocks obtained by such surgeries.

•

In Subsection
9.1
, we build a correspondence between building blocks and “intermediate” blocks (Definition
9.1.1
), which will be “standard” minimal continuations of building blocks, whose boundary decomposes into an entrance boundary, an exit boundary and a tangent boundary.
These will be the pieces of our surgeries.
We will be able to glue handles along the tangent boundary to build blocks whose boundary is transverse to the vector field.

•

In Subsection
9.2
, we describe two mutually reciprocal surgical operations which allow us to go from building blocks to Béguin-Bonatti-Yu blocks and vice-versa.
More precisely, we show that every building blocks is associated with a single Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block obtained by gluing spheres together along the boundary (Lemma
9.2.1
).
The reciprocal operation consists of, given a Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block and a finite collection of connected components of the complementary of the lamination which are bordered by compact leaves (satisfying some assumptions), to construct a building block by a finite number of removal of handles which has the effect of “killing” the components of the collection to “replace” them by periodic orbits contained in the boundary (Lemma
9.2.4
).

•

Finally, we use these two reciprocal operations, to show the necessary and sufficient criterion of Theorem
4
.

9.1.
Intermediate Blocks

This subsection is a preliminary technical section in order to perform surgery between building blocks and Béguin-Bonatti-Yu blocks in the next subsection.

Definition 9.1.1

(Intermediate block)
.

Let
\check{P}
be a3-manifold with boundary, and
\check{X}
a vector field of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
on
\check{P}
.
We say that
(\check{P},\check{X})
is an
intermediate block
if:

(1)

\check{P}
is a smooth manifold outside a finite number of simple closed curves
c_{1}^{\mathrm{in}},\dots,c_{n}^{\mathrm{in}}
and
c_{1}^{\mathrm{out}},\dots,c_{n}^{\mathrm{out}}
contained in
\partial\check{P}

(2)

\partial\check{P}
splits into the union
\partial\check{P}=\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}\cup\check{P}^{\mathrm{tan}}\cup\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}
where

•

\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
is a surface with boundary, bordered by
c_{1}^{\mathrm{in}},\dots,c_{n}^{\mathrm{in}}
, the vector field
\check{X}
is transverse to
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
and points inward

•

\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}
is a surface with boundary, bordered by
c_{1}^{\mathrm{out}},\dots,c_{n}^{\mathrm{out}}
, the vector field
\check{X}
is transverse to
\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}
and points outward

•

\check{P}^{\mathrm{tan}}
is a union of annuli
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
tangent to the vector field
\check{X}
, such that each
A_{i}
is bounded by the curves
c^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}
and
c^{\mathrm{out}}_{i}
, and the orbits of
\check{X}
on
A_{i}
go from
c^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}
to
c^{\mathrm{out}}_{i}

(3)

The maximal invariant set
\check{\Lambda}:=\bigcap_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\check{X}^{t}(P)
of
\check{X}
in
\check{P}
is a hyperbolic set of index
(1,1)
for
\check{X}
.

(4)

If we denote
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
and
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
the stable and unstable manifolds of
\check{\Lambda}
in
\check{P}
, then for any
i
there exist two compact leaves
\gamma^{s}_{i}
of
\check{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}:={\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cap\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
and
\gamma^{u}_{i}
of
\check{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}:={\mathcal{W}}^{u}\cap\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}
, such that
A_{i}
is contained in an annulus
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}\subset\partial\check{P}
, bounded by
\gamma^{s}_{i}
and
\gamma^{u}_{i}
and disjoint from
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cup\check{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
.

We refer to Figure
74
.

Figure 74
.
Boundary of an intermediate block
(\check{P},\check{X})
in the neighborhood of an annulus
A_{i}
tangent to
\check{X}

Definition 9.1.2

(Entrance, Exit, Tangent boundary)
.

Let
(\check{P},\check{X})
be an intermediate block and
\partial\check{P}=\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}\cup\check{P}^{\mathrm{tan}}\cup\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}
be the decomposition of
\partial\check{P}
given by Item
2
, Definition
9.1.1
.
We say that :

•

\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
is the
entrance boundary
of
(\check{P},\check{X})

•

\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}
is the
exit boundary
of
(\check{P},\check{X})

•

\check{P}^{\mathrm{tan}}
is the
tangent boundary
of
(\check{P},\check{X})
.

Claim 9.1.3
.

With these same notations, we have

(1)

the stable lamination
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
induces a lamination
\check{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}:=\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cap\partial\check{P}=\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cap\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
of dimension 1 on
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
, called
entrance lamination
of
(\check{P},\check{X})

(2)

the unstable lamination
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
induces a lamination
\check{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}:=\check{\mathcal{W}}^{u}\cap\partial\check{P}=\check{\mathcal{W}}^{u}\cap\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}
of dimension 1 on
(\check{P},\check{X})
, called
exit lamination
of
(\check{P},\check{X})

(3)

the union
\check{\mathcal{L}}=\check{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}\cup\check{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
is a lamination of dimension 1 on
\partial\check{P}
, called
boundary lamination
of
(\check{P},\check{X})

Proof.

Each orbit of a point of a connected component
A_{i}
of
\check{P}^{\mathrm{tan}}
is contained in the annulus
A_{i}
and crosses
A_{i}
from one boundary of the annulus to the other in finite time (Item
2
, Definition
9.1.1
).
We deduce that the stable lamination
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
of the maximal invariant set
\check{\Lambda}
is disjoint from the tangent boundary
\check{P}^{\mathrm{tan}}
.
It is disjoint from
\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}
, because all orbits intersecting
\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}
exit
\check{P}
by definition, so cannot accumulate on
\check{\Lambda}
in the future.
We deduce that
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cap\partial\check{P}=\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cap\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
.
Since the vector field
\check{X}
is transverse to
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
, and
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
is tangent to the vector field
\check{X}
, we deduce that the leaves of
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
transversely intersect the surface
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
, and the set
\check{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}:=\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}\cap\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
is a lamination of dimension 1 on
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
.
The other statements of the fact are proved in a similar way.
∎

Let
A_{*}=\{A_{1},\dots,A_{n}\}
be the collection of the connected components of the tangent boundary of
(\check{P},\check{X})
,
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{*}=\{{\mathcal{A}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{A}}_{n}\}
the collection of the connected components
\partial\check{P}{\smallsetminus}\check{\mathcal{L}}
such that
A_{i}\subset{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
, where
\check{\mathcal{L}}
is the boundary lamination of
(\check{P},\check{X})
.
With these notations, the curves
\gamma^{s}_{i}
and
\gamma^{u}_{i}
are compact leaves of
\check{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
and
\check{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
respectively, and
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
is the connected component of
\partial\check{P}{\smallsetminus}\check{\mathcal{L}}
which contains the annulus
A_{i}
.

Let
(P,X)
be a building block.
Let
\Lambda
be its maximal invariant set,
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
the collection of periodic orbits of
X
contained in
\partial P
,
P^{\mathrm{in}}
the entrance boundary, and
P^{\mathrm{out}}
the exit boundary of
(P,X)
.
Recall that the closure of
P^{\mathrm{in}}
, denoted
\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}
, coincides with
P^{\mathrm{in}}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
Similarly,
\overline{P^{\mathrm{out}}}=P^{\mathrm{out}}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
The following lemma states that we can canonically embed
(P,X)
into an intermediate block
(\check{P},\check{X})
, which is then a “standard” minimal continuation of
(P,X)
.

Lemma 9.1.4
.

There exists an intermediate block
(\check{P},\check{X})
such that, if
\check{\Lambda}
denotes the maximal invariant set,
(\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s},\check{\mathcal{W}}^{u})
the stable and unstable manifolds of
\check{\Lambda}
, and
{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
the collection of connected components of
\partial\check{P}{\smallsetminus}\check{\mathcal{L}}
containing the tangent boundary (Definition
9.1.2
), we have

(1)

(minimal continuation)

(\check{P},\check{X})
is a minimal continuation of
(P,X)
(Definition
1.4.3
);

(2)

(lamination)

\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
is isotopic to
\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}
along the lamination
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
and
\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
is isotopic to
\overline{P^{\mathrm{out}}}
along the lamination
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
;

(3)

(uniqueness)

such an intermediate block is unique up to topological equivalence.

Proof.

We repeat the proof of Lemma
1.2.8
which gives a method of constructing an intermediate block
(\check{P},\check{X})
associated to a building block
(P,X)
.
Let
(\tilde{P},\tilde{X})
be a minimal continuation (Definition
1.4.3
) of
(P,X)
.
Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}=\{{\mathcal{O}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{O}}_{n}\}
be the collection of periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
.
For each
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, up to local orbit equivalence of the flow of
\tilde{X}
, we can consider
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
a linearizing tubular neighborhood of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
for the flow of
\tilde{X}
in
\tilde{P}
, provided with a linearizing coordinate system
(x,y,\theta)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
.
The boundary
\partial P
crosses the opposite quadrants
\{x>0,y>0\}
and
\{x<0,y<0\}
(Claim
1.2.1
).
Let
S
be a topological surface, smooth outside a finite number of simple closed curves
c_{1}^{\mathrm{in}},\dots,c_{n}^{\mathrm{in}}
and
c_{1}^{\mathrm{out}},\dots,c_{n}^{\mathrm{out}}
, which coincides with
{\partial P}
outside of the
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
neighborhoods, and decomposes into the union
S=S^{\mathrm{in}}\cup S^{t}\cup S^{\mathrm{out}}
(Figure
75
) where

•

S^{\mathrm{in}}
is a surface with boundary transverse to the vector field
X
and bordered by
c_{1}^{\mathrm{in}},\dots,c_{n}^{\mathrm{in}}
, which coincides with
P^{\mathrm{in}}
outside the union of neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}

•

S^{\mathrm{out}}
is a surface with boundary transverse to the vector field
X
and bordered by
c_{1}^{\mathrm{out}},\dots,c_{n}^{\mathrm{out}}
, which coincides with
P^{\mathrm{out}}
outside the union of neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}

•

S^{t}
is a union of annuli
A_{i}
tangent to the vector field
X
, each contained in the neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
in the quadrant
\{x>0,y<0\}
, and bounded by the curves
c^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}
and
c^{\mathrm{out}}_{i}
.

Figure 75
.
The manifold
\check{P}
in a linearizing neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}

The surface
S
cuts into
\tilde{P}
a compact submanifold
\check{P}
with boundary, which contains
P
, and coincides with
P
outside the union of the neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.
Let
\check{X}
be the restriction of
\tilde{X}
to
\check{P}
.
The pair
(\check{P},\check{X})
is by construction a minimal continuation of
(P,X)
.
The maximal invariant set
\check{\Lambda}
of
(\check{P},\check{X})
coincides with the maximal invariant set
\Lambda
of
(P,X)
because an orbit of
\check{X}
is either an orbit of the flow of
X
contained in
P
, or intersects the boundary of
\check{P}
.
Moreover,
\partial\check{P}=S
, so
(\check{P},\check{X})
satisfies Items
1
,
2
and
3
of Definition
9.1.1
of an intermediate block.
Let
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
and
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
be the stable and unstable manifolds of the set
\check{\Lambda}=\Lambda
for the flow of
\check{X}
in
\check{P}
.
Then
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
is the union of the flow-saturated set of the laminations
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
under the flow of
\check{X}
and the local stable manifold of periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
in the linearizing neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.
Similarly
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
is the union of the flow-saturated set of the laminations
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
under the flow of
\check{X}
and the local unstable manifolds of the periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
in the linearizing neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.
For any
i
, the local stable and unstable manifold of orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
in neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
intersect
\partial\check{P}
along two compact leaves
\gamma^{s}_{i}
and
\gamma^{u}_{i}
, which borders a connected component
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
of
\partial\check{P}{\smallsetminus}\check{\mathcal{L}}
.
This is an annulus which contains the annulus
A_{i}\subset S
tangent to the vector field
\check{X}
(Figure
75
).
It follows that
(\check{P},\check{X})
satisfies Item
4
of Definition
9.1.1
, so it is an intermediate block.

Let us denote
{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
the collection of annuli
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
.
The surfaces
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
and
\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}
coincide outside the union of neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
, and in each
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
the surface
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
is an annulus whose interior is transverse to the lamination
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
.
There is therefore an isotopy between
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
and
\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}
, supported in the linearized neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
, which preserves the lamination
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
leaf-to-leaf.
The argument is symmetric for
\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
and
\overline{P^{\mathrm{out}}}
, and
(\check{P},\check{X})
satisfies Item
2
of Lemma
9.1.4
.
It remains to prove Item
3
, in other words the uniqueness of such an intermediate block up to orbit equivalence.
Let
(Q,Y)
be another intermediate block satisfying Lemma
9.1.4
.
Up to reduce the initial minimal continuation used to construct
(\check{P},\check{X})
and make an orbit equivalence, we can make the hypothesis that
\check{P}
is contained in
Q
, and that they are contained in a common minimal continuation of
(P,X)
according to Item
1
of Lemma
9.1.4
.
It is sufficient to show the existence of a local orbit equivalence on a linearizing neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
of a periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
, because the intermediate blocks
(Q,Y)
and
(\check{P},\check{X})
coincide each with
(P,X)
outside of the union of
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
, so coincide between them.
By transversality of
P^{\mathrm{in}}
and
Q^{\mathrm{in}}
with the vector field
Y
, up to an isotopy of
\partial Q
along the (positive) orbits of the flow of
Y
(which does not change the orbit equivalence class), we can suppose that
Q^{\mathrm{in}}
contains
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
, and up to an isotopy of
\partial\check{P}
along the positive orbits of the flow of
Y
, we can suppose that
Q^{\mathrm{out}}
contains
\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}
(Figure
76
).
We introduce a third intermediate block
(Q^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
which satisfies Lemma
9.1.4
, which is strictly contained in
\check{P}
, and such that
(Q^{\prime})^{\mathrm{in}}\subset\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
and
(Q^{\prime})^{\mathrm{out}}\subset\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}
(Figure
76
).
Then the vector field
\check{X}
restricted to
\check{P}{\smallsetminus}Q^{\prime}
and the vector field
Y
restricted to
Q{\smallsetminus}Q^{\prime}
are both orbit equivalent to the trivial field
\partial/\partial_{t}
on
I\times J\times{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
given coordinates
(s,t,\theta)
.
Since the intermediate blocks
(\check{P},\check{X})
and
(Q,Y)
coincide on
Q^{\prime}
, we deduce that they are orbit equivalent.

Figure 76
.
orbit equivalence of intermediate blocks in a linearizing neighborhood of a periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}

∎

Let
(\check{P},\check{X})
be an intermediate block and
{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
be the collection of connected components of
(\check{P},\check{X})
containing the tangent boundary of
(\check{P},\check{X})
(Definition
9.1.2
), where
\check{\mathcal{L}}
is the boundary lamination of
(\check{P},\check{X})
.
Recall that a stable separatrix is a connected component of
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(O){\smallsetminus}O
, where
O
is an orbit of
\check{X}
.

Lemma 9.1.5
.

For any
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
, if
\gamma^{s}_{i}
and
\gamma^{u}_{i}
are the two compact leaves of
\check{\mathcal{L}}
which border
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
(Item
2
, Definition
9.1.1
), then there exists a periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
of
\check{X}
such that

\gamma^{s}_{i}
is the intersection of a free stable separatrix of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
with
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
and
\gamma^{u}_{i}
is the intersection of a free unstable separatrix of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
with
\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}
.
Up to orbit equivalence of
(\check{P},\check{X})
, we can suppose that
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
is contained in a linearizing neighborhood of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
.

Proof.

We can adapt Lemma
2.1.11
for intermediate blocks, and
\gamma^{s}_{i}
is a compact leaf of the lamination
\check{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
, thus the intersection of a free stable separatrix
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{+}({\mathcal{O}}_{i})
of a periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
of
\check{X}
.
If
p\in\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
is a point on
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
arbitrarily close to
\gamma^{s}_{i}
, then the orbit of
p
by the flow of
\check{X}
exits
\check{P}
at a point
q\in\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}
arbitrarily close to the unstable manifold
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}({\mathcal{O}}_{i})
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
.
Now we know that the flow of
\check{X}
on the annulus
A_{i}\subset{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
crosses
A_{i}
from one boundary
c^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}
to the other boundary
c^{\mathrm{out}}_{i}
(Item
2
, Definition
9.1.1
).
Since
c^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}
is the boundary of
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}\cap{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
, and
c^{\mathrm{out}}_{i}
is the boundary of
\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}\cap{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
, we deduce by continuity that the orbit of a point of
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}\cap{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
exits
P
by the component
\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}\cap{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
.
It follows that
\gamma^{u}_{i}\in{\mathcal{W}}^{u}({\mathcal{O}}_{i})
, and
\gamma^{u}_{i}
is the intersection of a free unstable separatrix
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{+}({\mathcal{O}}_{i})
of the same periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
.

Let us show the second statement of Lemma
9.1.5
.
For each
i
, consider
c_{i}
a simple closed curve in (the interior of)
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}\cap{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
parallel to
\gamma^{s}_{i}
and close enough to
\gamma^{s}_{i}
so that the orbit of
c_{i}
by the flow of
\check{X}
intersects a linearizing neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
.
Let
C_{i}:=\cup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\check{X}^{t}(c_{i})
the orbit of
c_{i}
by the flow of
\check{X}
.
Each point of
c_{i}
exits
\check{P}
in uniformly bounded time, because
c_{i}
is uniformly far from
\check{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
.
It follows that
C_{i}
is homeomorphic to the cylinder
c_{i}\times[0,1]
, and the vector field
\check{X}
is orbit equivalent to the vertical field
\partial_{t}
on
C_{i}
.
The union of cylinders
C_{i}
cuts into
\check{P}
a submanifold with boundary
Q
, such that if
Y
is the restriction of
\check{X}
on
Q
, the pair
(Q,Y)
is an intermediate block, embedded in
(\check{P},\check{X})
, such that the maximal invariant set of
(Q,Y)
coincides with the maximal invariant set of
X
.
Let us show that the intermediate block
(Q,Y)
is orbit equivalent to
(\check{P},\check{X})
.
Let
(Q^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
be a third intermediate block contained in
(Q,Y)
obtained by cutting
(P,X)
along the orbit of a simple closed curve
c^{\prime}_{i}
contained in the interior of the annulus bounded by
\gamma^{s}_{i}
and
c_{i}
and parallel to the boundary of this annulus.
Then
Q{\smallsetminus}Q^{\prime}
is homeomorphic to the product
I\times J\times{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
, and the vector field
Y
on
Q{\smallsetminus}Q^{\prime}
is orbit equivalent to the vector field
\partial_{t}
on
I\times J\times{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
given coordinates
(s,t,\theta)
.
Similarly,
\check{P}{\smallsetminus}Q^{\prime}
is homeomorphic to the product
I\times J\times{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
, and the vector field
Y
on
Q{\smallsetminus}Q^{\prime}
is orbit equivalent to the vector field
\partial_{t}
on
I\times J\times{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
with coordinates
(s,t,\theta)
(Figure
76
)
It follows that the vector fields
Y
on
Q{\smallsetminus}Q^{\prime}
and
\check{X}
on
\check{P}{\smallsetminus}Q^{\prime}
are orbit equivalent.
Since they coincide outside
\check{P}{\smallsetminus}Q
, we deduce that
(\check{P},\check{X})
is orbit equivalent to
(Q,Y)
.
The tangent boundary
Q^{\mathrm{tan}}
of
\partial Q
coincides with the union of the cylinders
\cup_{i}C_{i}
defined previously, and we denote
\partial Q=Q^{\mathrm{in}}\cup Q^{\mathrm{tan}}\cup Q^{\mathrm{out}}
the decomposition of the boundary given by Item
2
, Definition
9.1.1
.
By construction, the boundary components
C_{i}
intersect a linearizing neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
of the periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
.
Let
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
be the connected component of
\partial Q{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
containing
C_{i}
.
By transversality of
Q^{\mathrm{in}}
with the vector field
Y
, up to push
Q^{\mathrm{in}}
along the (positive) orbits of
Y
and
Q^{\mathrm{out}}
along the (negative) orbits of
Y
, we can assume that
{\mathcal{C}}_{i}
is contained in
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.
This operation does not change the orbit equivalence class of
(Q,Y)
.
∎

Definition 9.1.6

((h)-hypothesis)
.

We will say that the
(\check{P},\check{X})
satisfies the
(h)
hypothesis
if the periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
associated to the annuli
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
by Lemma
9.1.5
are pairwise distinct, and have positive multipliers.

Remark 9.1.7
.

In the case of a
(\check{P},\check{X})
associated to a
(P,X)
by Lemma
9.1.4
, the periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
are the periodic orbits of
X
contained in
\partial P
.
Indeed,
(\check{P},\check{X})
is a minimal continuation of
(P,X)
(Item
1
, Lemma
9.1.4
) and the annuli
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
are contained in
\partial\check{P}{\smallsetminus}\partial P
, thus in the linearizing neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
of the periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
Consequently, Claim
1.2.1
implies that each
(\check{P},\check{X})
satisfies the
(h)
-hypothesis.
There are some intermediate blocks which do not satisfy the
(h)
-hypothesis : see for that Lemma
10.2.3
and Figure
83
.

The following lemma is the counterpart of Lemma
9.1.4
.
Let
(\check{P},\check{X})
be an intermediate block satisfying the
(h)
-hypothesis.
Let
\check{\Lambda}
be its maximal invariant set,
(\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s},\check{\mathcal{W}}^{u})
be the pair of stable and unstable manifolds of
\check{\Lambda}
,
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
be the entrance boundary,
\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}
be the exit boundary, and

{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
the collection of connected components of
\partial\check{P}{\smallsetminus}\check{\mathcal{L}}
containing the tangent boundary of
(\check{P},\check{X})
(Definition
9.1.2
).

Lemma 9.1.8
.

There exists a building block
(P,X)
such that, if
\Lambda
denotes the maximal invariant set of
(P,X)
,
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
the collection of periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
,
P^{\mathrm{in}}
the entrance boundary and
P^{\mathrm{out}}
the exit boundary of
(P,X)
, then:

(1)

(embedding)

there exists an embedding
h:P\to\check{P}
which maps the oriented orbits of
X
on the oriented orbits of
\check{X}|_{\check{P}}
and
\Lambda
on
\check{\Lambda}
;

(2)

(lamination)

\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
is isotopic to
\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}
along the lamination
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
and
\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
is isotopic to
\overline{P^{\mathrm{out}}}
along the lamination
\check{\mathcal{W}}^{u}
;

(3)

(uniqueness)

such a building block
(P,X)
is unique up to isotopy among the building blocks.

We refer to Definition
1.4.1
for the isotopy of building blocks.

Proof.

In order to make an orbit equivalence of
(\check{P},\check{X})
, we assume that each annulus
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
is contained in the linearizing neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
of a periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
(Lemma
9.1.5
).
By assumption, the multipliers of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
are positive, and the orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
are pairwise disjoint, so we can assume that the linearizing neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
are pairwise disjoint.
The entrance boundary
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
and the exit boundary
\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}
of
(\check{P},\check{X})
lie in two opposite quadrants of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
on the neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
(see the proof of Claim
1.2.1
).
So there exists a smooth surface
S
in
\check{P}
, which coincides with
\partial\check{P}
outside the union of
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
, and such that in each
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
,
S
is an annulus which contains the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
, which coincides with
\partial\check{P}
in the neighborhood of the boundary of
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
and crosses
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
in two opposite quadrants of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
, and which is transverse to the vector field
\check{X}
on the complementary of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
.
Then
S
cuts into
\check{P}
a submanifold
P
such that if
X
is the restriction of
\check{X}
to
P
, then
(P,X)
is a building block, embedded in
(\check{P},\check{X})
and such that the maximal invariant set
\Lambda
of
(P,X)
coincides with the maximal invariant set
\check{\Lambda}
of
(\check{P},\check{X})
.
We show Item
2
in an analogous way to the proof of Lemma
9.1.4
, Item
2
.
Finally, two building blocks
(P,X)
and
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
which satisfy Lemma
9.1.8
have a common minimal continuation (up to orbit equivalence of
(\check{P},\check{X})
, according to Lemma
9.1.5
).
They are therefore isotopic among the building blocks (Proposition
1.4.4
).
∎

9.2.
Building block and Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block

Let
(P,X)
be a building block.
Let
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
be the set of periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
,
\Lambda_{X}
the maximal invariant set,
P^{\mathrm{in}}
the entrance boundary and
P^{\mathrm{out}}
the exit boundary.

Lemma 9.2.1
.

There exists a Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block
(U,Y)
such that, if
\Lambda_{Y}
denotes the maximal invariant set of
(U,Y)
,

({\mathcal{W}}_{Y}^{s},{\mathcal{W}}_{Y}^{u})
the pair of stable and unstable manifolds of
\Lambda_{Y}
,

U^{\mathrm{in}}
the entrance boundary,
U^{\mathrm{out}}
the exit boundary, and
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}={\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{Y}\cup{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{Y}
the boundary lamination, then:

(1)

(embedding)

there exists an embedding
h:P\to U
which maps the oriented orbits of
X
to the oriented orbits of
Y
in
h(P)
and
\Lambda_{X}
to
\Lambda_{Y}
;

(2)

(lamination)

there exists a finite collection
D_{*}=\{D_{1},\dots,D_{n}\}
of open disks contained in
\partial U
, disjoint from
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
, bounded by pairwise distinct compact leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
, such that
U^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
is isotopic to
\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}
along the lamination
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{Y}
and
U^{\mathrm{out}}{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
is isotopic to
\overline{P^{\mathrm{out}}}
along the lamination
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{Y}
;

(3)

(uniqueness)

such a block
(U,Y)
is unique up to orbit equivalence

Proof.

We refer to the proof of Lemma
1.2.8
for Items
1
and
2
.
Let us show the last Item
3
.
Let
(U,Y)
be a Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block which satisfies Lemma
9.2.1
.
Let
\bar{D}_{1},\dots,\bar{D}_{n}
be a collection of compact disks such that
\bar{D}_{i}\subset D_{i}
.
Since each point
p
is uniformly far from the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
, the orbit of
p
exits
U
in uniformly bounded time.
We deduce that the orbit of
\bar{D}_{i}
by the flow of
Y
into
U
, denoted
\bar{C}_{i}
, is homeomorphic to a compact cylinder
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}\times I
, on which the vector field
Y
is orbit equivalent to the vertical vector field
\partial/\partial t
, where
t
is the coordinate on
I
.
The complementary
\check{P}:=U{\smallsetminus}\cup_{i}\bar{C}_{i}
equipped with the restriction
\check{X}
of the vector field
Y
is an intermediate block which satisfies Lemma
9.1.4
associated to the block
(P,X)
.
The orbit equivalence class of the block
(\check{P},\check{X})
obtained by such a surgery does not depend on the choice of the compact disk
\bar{D}_{i}
contained in
D_{i}
since they are all isotopic.
We conclude with Item
3
of Lemma
9.1.4
.
∎

We now describe the reciprocal operation.

Definition 9.2.2

(Simple collection)
.

Let
(U,Y)
be a building block and
D_{*}=\{D_{1},\dots,D_{n}\}
be a collection of connected components of the complementary of the boundary lamination
\partial U{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
bounded by compact leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
.
We say that
D_{*}
is a
simple collection
if two distinct compact leaves of the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
contained in the boundary of
D_{*}
are on distinct leaves of
{\mathcal{W}}_{Y}^{s}
or
{\mathcal{W}}_{Y}^{u}
, and the periodic orbits contained in these leaves have positive multipliers.

Recall that any compact leaf
\gamma
of the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
is the trace of a free separatrix of a periodic orbit
O
on
\partial U
(Lemma
2.1.11
).
This justifies that we can speak of (the only) periodic orbit contained in the leaf of
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{Y}
(or
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{Y}
) which contains
\gamma
.

Remark 9.2.3
.

Let
D_{*}
be the collection given by Lemma
9.2.1
, Item
2
.
Then it is a simple collection.
The isotopy of Item
2
induces a diffeomorphism
H^{s}:U^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}D_{*}\to\overline{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
which preserves the stable lamination
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{Y}
leaf to leaf.
If
\gamma^{s}
is a compact leaf of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{Y}
, boundary of a component
D_{i}
, then
\gamma^{s}
is mapped by
H^{s}
on a periodic orbit of
{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
along the stable leaf
{\mathcal{W}}_{Y}^{s}({\mathcal{O}})
.
The multipliers of
{\mathcal{O}}
are positive according to Claim
1.2.1
.
By injectivity of
H^{s}
it is the only boundary component of
D_{*}
mapped on
{\mathcal{O}}
.
It follows that the leaves bordering
D_{*}
are on disjoint leaves of
{\mathcal{W}}_{Y}^{s}
.
Similarly, the compact leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{Y}
which border a component of
D_{*}
are on disjoint leaves of
{\mathcal{W}}_{Y}^{u}
.

The following lemma is a reciprocal of Lemma
9.2.1
.
Let
(U,Y)
be a Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block, and let
\Lambda_{Y}
be the maximal invariant set,
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
be the boundary lamination of
(U,Y)
,
U^{\mathrm{in}}
be the entrance boundary and
U^{\mathrm{out}}
be the exit boundary.
Let
D_{*}=\{D_{1},\dots D_{n}\}
be a simple collection (Definition
9.2.2
) of connected components of
\partial P{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}
bounded by compact leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
and invariant by the crossing map
f_{Y}:U^{\mathrm{in}}\to U^{\mathrm{out}}
of the flow of
Y
from the entrance boundary to the exit boundary.

Lemma 9.2.4
.

There exists a building block
(P,X)
such that, if
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
denotes the set of periodic orbits of
X
contained in
(P,X)
,
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
the boundary lamination of
(P,X)
and
\Lambda_{X}
the maximal invariant set of
(P,X)
, we have:

(1)

(embedding)

there exists an embedding
h:P\to U
which maps the oriented orbits of
X
to the oriented orbits of
Y
in
h(P)
and
\Lambda_{X}
to
\Lambda_{Y}
;

(2)

(lamination)

U^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
is isotopic to
\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}
along the lamination
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{Y}
, and
U^{\mathrm{out}}{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
is isotopic to
\overline{P^{\mathrm{out}}}
along the lamination
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{Y}
;

(3)

(uniqueness)

such a building block
(P,X)
is unique up to isotopy among the building blocks.

Remark 9.2.5
.

Let us justify why Lemma
9.2.4
does give a reciprocal of Lemma
9.2.1
.
If
(U,Y)
is the Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block associated to a building block
(P,X)
by Lemma
9.2.1
, and
D_{*}
is the collection of disks given by Item
2
of this lemma, then it is a simple collection, and we can apply Lemma
9.2.4
to
(U,Y)
and to the collection
D_{*}
.
We obtain a building block
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
isotopic to
(P,X)
by Item
3
.

Proof of Lemma
9.2.4
.

By
f_{Y}
-invariance of
D_{*}
, we can rename the components of
D_{*}
as follows
D_{*}=\{D^{\mathrm{in}}_{1},\dots,D^{\mathrm{in}}_{n},D^{\mathrm{out}}_{1},\dots,D^{\mathrm{out}}_{n}\}
such that
D^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}\subset U^{\mathrm{in}}
, and
D^{\mathrm{out}}_{i}=f_{Y}(D^{\mathrm{in}}_{i})\subset U^{\mathrm{out}}
.
Let
\gamma_{i}^{s}
be a compact leaf that borders
D^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}
.
It is the intersection of a free stable separatrix of a periodic orbit
O_{i}
.
By
f_{Y}
-invariance of
D_{*}
, there exists a compact leaf
\gamma_{i}^{u}
in the boundary of
D^{\mathrm{out}}_{i}
which is on the unstable manifold of the periodic orbit
O_{i}
(it suffices to notice that the orbit of a point in
D^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}
arbitrarily close to
\gamma_{i}^{s}
exits through
U^{\mathrm{out}}
on
D^{\mathrm{out}}_{i}
at a point arbitrarily close to
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(O_{i})
).
If we push
U^{\mathrm{in}}
and
U^{\mathrm{out}}
along the orbits of the flow of
Y
we can assume that a neighborhood of
\gamma^{s}
in
\partial U
(contained in
U^{\mathrm{in}}
) and a neighborhood of
\gamma^{u}
in
\partial U
(contained in
U^{\mathrm{out}}
) are contained in a linearizing neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
of
O_{i}
, and any orbit that enters through
D^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}
close to
\gamma_{i}^{s}
exits through
D^{\mathrm{out}}_{i}
close to
\gamma_{i}^{u}
and is entirely contained in
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.
We refer to Figure
77
.

Figure 77
.
Isotopy along the flow and handles removal in
(U,Y)

By repeating this same argument, we obtain a collection
O_{1},\dots,O_{n}
of periodic orbits and linearizing neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{V}}_{n}
.
Since the collection is simple, the periodic orbits and the linearizing neighborhoods are pairwise distinct.
Let
D_{0}\subset U^{\mathrm{in}}
be a compact set contained in the union
\cup_{i}D^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}
, whose interior is isotopic to
\cup_{i}D^{\mathrm{in}}_{i}
and whose boundary is a union of simple closed curves contained in
\cup_{i}{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.
By construction, the orbit by the flow of
Y
of each of the connected components of
\partial D_{0}
is a cylinder contained in a single
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.
The orbit
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}:=\cup_{t\in{\mathbb{R}}}Y^{t}(D_{0})
of
D_{0}
by the flow of
Y
in
U
is homeomorphic to
D_{0}\times[0,1]
and the vector field
Y
is orbit equivalent to the trivial vector field
\partial/\partial_{t}
,
t\in[0,1]
.
Let
\check{P}:=U{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
and
\check{X}
be the restriction of
Y
to
\check{P}
.
Then
(\check{P},\check{X})
is an intermediate block embedded in
(U,Y)
and whose maximal invariant set coincides with
\Lambda_{Y}
.
Indeed by construction, the tangent boundary
\check{P}^{\mathrm{tan}}
of
(\check{P},\check{X})
is a union of annuli
A_{i}
homeomorphic to
\partial D_{0}\times[0,1]
, each contained in a single linearizing neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.
The connected component
{\mathcal{A}}_{i}
of
\check{P}{\smallsetminus}\check{\mathcal{L}}
containing
A_{i}
is an annulus bounded by the compact leaves
\gamma^{s}_{i}
and
\gamma^{u}_{i}
arising from the free stable and local free unstable separatrices of
O_{i}
.
Moreover,
(\check{P},\check{X})
satisfies the
(h)
-hypothesis (Definition
9.1.6
).
We use Lemma
9.1.8
which gives the existence of a building block
(P,X)
embedded in
(\check{P},\check{X})
, such that
(\check{P},\check{X})
is a
(P,X)
minimal continuation.
Let us show that
(P,X)
satisfies Lemma
9.2.4
.

(1)

By construction, the intermediate block
(\check{P},\check{X})
is contained in
(U,Y)
.
Recall that
\check{P}=U{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
, and each orbit of
Y
in
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
enters and exits
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
, hence
U
, in finite time.
The maximal invariant set
\Lambda_{Y}
of
(U,Y)
is thus contained in
\check{P}
, and coincides with the maximal invariant set of
(\check{P},\check{X})
.
Item
1
follows from Item
1
of Lemma
9.1.8
.

(2)

We have the equality
\partial\check{P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{A}}_{*}=\partial U{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
, and the lamination
\check{\mathcal{L}}
on
\partial\check{P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
coincides with the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
on
\partial U{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
, because
\check{\mathcal{L}}
is the intersection of the manifolds
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{Y}
and
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{Y}
with
\partial\check{P}
.
Item
2
follows from Item
2
of Lemma
9.1.8
.

(3)

Let
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
be another building block which satisfies Lemma
9.2.4
.
Then, up to make an orbit equivalence, according to Item
1
of Lemma
9.2.4
, we can assume that
P
and
P^{\prime}
are contained in
U
, and
X
and
X^{\prime}
coincide with the restrictions of
Y
to
P
and
P^{\prime}
, and the vector fields coincide on a neighborhood of
\Lambda_{X}=\Lambda_{X^{\prime}}=\Lambda_{Y}
.
In particular,
X
and
X^{\prime}
coincide on linearizing neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
periodic orbits contained in the boundary of
P
.
According to Item
2
,
P^{\mathrm{in}}
is isotopic to
U^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
along the
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}_{Y}
lamination.
On the complementary of the
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
neighborhoods, the surfaces
P^{\mathrm{in}}
and
U^{\mathrm{in}}
are compact and transverse to the vector field
Y
, so we can choose an isotopy which preserves the orbits of the
Y
flow on the complementary of the neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.
With the same argument for
P^{\prime}
, we can assume (up to orbit equivalence) that the boundary of
P
and the boundary of
P^{\prime}
coincide outside the neighborhoods
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
.
It follows that the blocks
(P,X)
and
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
have a common minimal continuation.
According to Proposition
1.4.4
they are isotopic.∎

Remark 9.2.6
.

We can generalize the previous lemma to a building block
(U,Y)
which is not a Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block, in other words which admits a non-empty collection
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
of periodic orbits contained in the boundary.
We obtain the same result if we suppose moreover that the closure of the elements of
D_{*}
are disjoint from
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.
The proof is identical because the surgeries are far from
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
.

9.3.
Proof of Theorem
E

We show the realization criterion of an abstract geometric type in a filled block.

Step 1: Necessary condition.

Suppose that
(P,X)
is a filled orientable building block which admits a Markov partition of geometric type
{\mathscr{T}}
.
Let
(U,Y)
be the Béguin-Bonatti-Yu block associated by Lemma
9.2.1
.
Let
D_{*}=\{D_{1},\dots,D_{n}\}
be the collection of connected components of
\partial U{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
given by Item
2
of Lemma
9.2.1
.
Let us show that
(U,Y)
is a model block.
Let
c
be a simple closed curve embedded in
\partial U
disjoint from
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
.
If
c
is contained in a disk
D_{i}\in D_{*}
, then
c
borders a disk disjoint from the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
.
Otherwise
c
is contained in
U^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
or
U^{\mathrm{out}}{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
.
Suppose that
c
is contained in
U^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
.
It follows from Item
2
that the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
restricted to
U^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}D
is topologically equivalent to the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{X}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
on
\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}
.
Since the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
is filling on
\partial P
, it follows that any connected component of the complementary of the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{X}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
on
\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}
is a strip.
By topological equivalence, the same is true for the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
restricted to
U^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}D
, and the curve
c
is contained in a strip.
It follows that
c
borders a disk disjoint from the lamination.
Since the boundary lamination of
(P,X)
is filling, it intersects every connected component of
{\partial P}
.
The same is true for the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
of
(U,Y)
from Item
2
, and we deduce that every connected component of
U
intersects the maximal invariant set
\Lambda_{Y}
.
Therefore,
(U,Y)
is a model block.
According to Item
1
, the block
(P,X)
is embedded in
(U,Y)
, it follows that the block
(U,Y)
admits a Markov partition of geometric type
{\mathscr{T}}
.
Let us show that it satisfies the two conditions of Theorem
4
.
The lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{Y}
restricted to
U^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
is topologically equivalent to the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{X}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
on
\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}
, hence is a prefoliation, which shows Item
2
.
It follows that
U^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
is a union of tori and closed annuli bounded by compact leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{Y}
.
Let
A
be such an annulus, and let
\gamma_{i},\gamma_{j}
be the two (distinct) compact leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{Y}
which border
A
.
There exist two disks
D_{i}
and
D_{j}
of
D_{*}
such that
\gamma_{i}=\partial D_{i}
and
\gamma_{j}=\partial D_{j}
.
We conclude that the annulus
A
is contained in a
\partial U
which is a sphere
S_{i,j}
, obtained by joining
A
and the two disks
D_{i}
and
D_{j}
along their boundary.
Item
1
is satisfied.

Step 2: Sufficient condition

Let
{\mathscr{T}}
be a geometric type whose model
(U,Y)
satisfies Theorem
4
.

Claim 9.3.1
.

The lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
on
\partial U{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
is filling.

Proof.

According to Item
2
, the connected components of
\partial U{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
are annuli bordered by compact leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
or tori, and according to Proposition
1.2.11
, the connected components of the complementary of the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
on
\partial U{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
are annuli bordered by compact leaves, or tori.
Now let us notice that if
A
is a connected component of
\partial U{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
which is an annulus, then there exists a simple closed curve
c
embedded in
A
disjoint from the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
and which does not border any disk disjoint from the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
, which contradicts with the fact that
(U,Y)
is a model block (Item
4
, Definition
9.0.1
).
∎

Claim 9.3.2
.

The collection
D_{*}
given by Item
2
is a simple and
f_{Y}
-invariant collection (Definition
9.2.2
)

Proof.

The collection
D_{*}
is the collection of all connected components of the complementary of
\partial U{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
which are not strips, so it is
f_{Y}
-invariant.
Suppose there are two compact leaves
\gamma^{s}_{i}=\partial D_{i}
and
\gamma^{s}_{j}=\partial D_{j}
in the boundary of
D_{*}
from the same stable manifold
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(O)
of a periodic orbit
O
.
Then
f_{Y}(D_{i})
and
f_{Y}(D_{j})
are two disks of
D_{*}
bordered by two distinct compact leaves, both arising from the unstable manifold
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(O)
of
O
.
It follows that all separatrices of
O
are free, so
O
is an isolated periodic orbit.
The compact leaves induced on the boundary by the separatrices are therefore isolated in the boundary lamination.
This is impossible because we have shown that the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
is filling on
\partial U{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
.
∎

We can therefore apply Lemma
9.2.4
, with the collection
D_{*}=\{D_{1},\dots,D_{n}\}
of disks given by Item
1
of Theorem
4
.
It is a simple and
f_{Y}
-invariant collection.
We obtain a block
(P,X)
embedded in
(U,Y)
by an embedding which maps the maximal invariant set
\Lambda_{X}
to
\Lambda_{Y}
and the oriented orbits of
X
to the oriented orbits of
Y
.
It follows that
(P,X)
is an orientable block, and
\Lambda
admits a Markov partition of geometric type
{\mathscr{T}}
.
It follows from Item
2
that the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{X}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
on
\overline{P^{\mathrm{in}}}
is topologically equivalent to the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{Y}
restricted to
U^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}D_{*}
, hence is filling according to Claim
9.3.1
.
It is the same for
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{X}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, so for the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}={\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{X}\cup{\mathcal{O}}_{*}\cup{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{X}
on
\partial P
, so
(P,X)
is a filled block.

Step 3: Uniqueness and topological equivalence of laminations.

Let
(P,X)
and
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
be two building blocks satisfying Theorem
4
.
Let
(U,Y)
and
(U^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
be the Béguin-Bonatti-Yu blocks associated by Lemma
9.2.1
to
(P,X)
and
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
respectively.
Then as noticed in the first step,
(U,Y)
and
(U^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
are model blocks.
Moreover, they admit a Markov partition of geometric type
{\mathscr{T}}
.
By Theorem
9.0.6
, the model blocks
(U,Y)
and
(U^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
are orbit equivalent.
By Lemma
9.2.4
, seen as a reciprocal of Lemma
9.2.1
(Remark
9.2.5
) we deduce that the blocks
(P,X)
and
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
are isotopic.
The topological equivalence of the laminations follows directly from Item
2
of Lemma
9.2.4
applied to
(U,Y)
and to the collection
D_{*}
.

9.4.
Example of model of Markov partition

Theorem
4
is useful because the criteria can be checked by a algorithm consisting of gluing rectangles.
It allows us, given an abstract geometric type, to build the entrance boundary and the entrance lamination of the model.
We refer to the proof of
[
BB02
, Lemma 2.13]
, and repeat the idea here.

Let
{\mathscr{T}}=\{n,\{h_{i}\},\{v_{i}\},\phi\}
be an abstract geometric type.
Consider a union
R
of
n
rectangles
R_{1},\dots,R_{n}
, provided with an orientation of the verticals and horizontals of
R_{i}
, such that each
R_{i}
contains
h_{i}
horizontal subrectangles
H_{i}^{1},\dots,H_{i}^{h_{i}}
and
v_{i}
vertical subrectangles
V_{i}^{1},\dots,V_{i}^{v_{i}}
.
Denote
R
the union of
R_{i}
,
H
the union of
H_{i}^{j}
and
V
the union of
V_{i}^{j}
.
Let
\partial^{u}
denote the vertical boundary of a rectangle or subrectangle of
R
, and let
\partial^{u}_{g}
and
\partial^{u}_{d}
denote the left and right vertical boundary of the subrectangle respectively relative to the orientations of the verticals and horizontals of
R
.
Let
(i,j)
be a fixed pair with
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq j\leq v_{i}
.
Let
(k,l)
be the unique pair such that

\phi(k,l)=((i,j),\epsilon)
.
Let
h_{(i,j)}
be a homeomorphism which maps
\partial^{u}_{g}V_{i}^{j}
on
\partial^{u}_{g}H_{k}^{l}
and
\partial^{u}_{d}V_{i}^{j}
on
\partial^{u}_{d}H_{k}^{l}
if
\epsilon=+
and which maps
\partial^{u}_{g}V_{i}^{j}
on
\partial^{u}_{d}H_{k}^{l}
and
\partial^{u}_{d}V_{i}^{j}
on
\partial^{u}_{s}H_{k}^{l}
if
\epsilon=-1
.
The set
A:=R{\smallsetminus}V
is a finite union of semi-open vertical subrectangles of
R
, whose closure are pairwise disjoint.
The closure
\bar{A}
contains the set
\partial^{u}V
and the set
\partial^{u}H
in its boundary.
Consider the set
\Sigma^{s}:=\bar{A}/h
where
h
is the product of homeomorphisms
h_{(i,j)}
.
Then
h
connects the rectangles of
\bar{A}
along a finite number of segments of their boundaries.
The surface
\Sigma^{s}
is a surface with corners and boundary.
It is provided with a foliation
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}
, image by the gluing map
h
of the horizontal foliation
R
induced on
\bar{A}
.

A Markov partition such that the intersection of every orbit with the suspension neighborhood of
R
is connected is called
essential
.
Such a partition always exists in a block without attractors or repellers (Lemma
2.2.5
).

Claim 9.4.1
.

Let
(U,Y)
be a model block with an essential Markov partition
{\mathcal{R}}
of geometric type
{\mathscr{T}}
.
Then
\Sigma^{s}
is a compact surface, with boundary and corners, homeomorphic to the intersection of the flow-saturated set of
{\mathcal{R}}
under the flow of
Y
on the entrance boundary
U^{\mathrm{in}}
.
The lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
on
U^{\mathrm{in}}
is topologically equivalent to a sublamination of the foliation
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}
on
\Sigma^{s}
.
The boundary
U^{\mathrm{in}}
is obtained by gluing disks to the boundary of
\Sigma^{s}
.

Proof idea.

The idea of the proof is based on the following two observations, using the previous notations

•

The past orbit of a point of
\bar{A}=R{\smallsetminus}V
intersects the boundary
U^{\mathrm{in}}
in uniformly bounded time and at a single point.

•

The orbits of the points of
\operatorname{int}A
are pairwise distinct,

•

The negative orbit of a point of
\bar{A}
reconnects with a negative orbit of a point of the vertical boundary of
A
after time 1.

With these observations, we see that the gluing map
h
on the set
\bar{A}{\smallsetminus}A
corresponds to the identification of points on the same orbit.
It then follows from the first two items that the flow-saturated set of
R
under the negative flow of
Y
intersects the boundary of
U
on a surface homeomorphic to the quotient of
\bar{A}
by
h
.
A surface obtained by gluing a finite number of segments, immersed in a smooth surface, is a compact surface with boundary and corner.
We refer to the proof
[
BB02
, Lemma 2.13]
for a complete argument.
The stable lamination on
R
is a sublamination of the horizontal foliation on
R
, which justifies the second statement.
It follows from the criterion
4
of Definition
9.0.1
of a model block that the complementary
U^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}\Sigma^{s}
is a finite union of disks (otherwise we would have a circle disjoint from the lamination which borders no disk).
∎

Example 9.4.2

(Fake horseshoe)
.

Recall that the geometric type of the fake horseshoe is given in Figure
73
, and the example
9.0.5
.
The following figure shows the construction of the entrance boundary
U^{\mathrm{in}}
and the entrance lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
of the horseshoe model by gluing rectangles.
We see that
U^{\mathrm{in}}
is a sphere containing exactly two disks
D_{1}
and
D_{2}
of interiors disjoint from the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
, and bordered by two distinct compact leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}
.
The complementary of these disks in
U^{\mathrm{in}}
is an annulus bordered by two compact leaves, on which the lamination is a (filling) prefoliation, with no compact leaves other than the two boundary leaves, and each noncompact leaf accumulates on a compact leaf with no Reeb component.
In particular, the fake horseshoe model satisfies the criterion of Theorem
4
, so the geometric type of the fake horseshoe is realizable in a orientable filled building block
(P,X)
.

Figure 78
.
Constructing the surface
\Sigma^{s}
and the boundary lamination on the entrance boundary
U^{\mathrm{in}}
of the model
(U,Y)
of the fake horseshoe

The boundary lamination on the exit boundary
U^{\mathrm{out}}
is symmetric to that on
U^{\mathrm{in}}
.
According to Theorem
4
, we can also compute the boundary lamination of the associated block
(P,X)
.
The boundary of
(P,X)
is a torus which contains exactly two periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
of the vector field
X
(corresponding to the two compact leaves of
U^{\mathrm{in}}
).
The lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
on
\partial P
is a filling lamination, with no compact leaves other than the periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
and no Reeb component.
Therefore, there exists a diffeomorphism
\varphi:{\partial P}\to{\partial P}
which maps the periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{1}
to the orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{2}
, the exit boundary
P^{\mathrm{out}}
to the entrance boundary
P^{\mathrm{in}}
, and the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
to a lamination strongly quasi-transverse to
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
.
This diffeomorphism induces a gluing map for the building block formed by two copies
(P_{1},X_{1})
and
(P_{2},X_{2})
of
(P,X)
.
Up to make a strong isotopy of the triple
(Q,Y,\varphi):=(P_{1}\cup P_{2},X_{1}\cup X_{2},\varphi)
, the vector fields
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
induce an Anosov vector field
Z
on a closed orientable manifold
{\mathcal{M}}=P_{1}\cup P_{2}/\varphi
according to the Gluing Theorem
1
.
The maximal invariant set of the fake horseshoe is transitive, and the graph
G(Q,Y,\varphi)
is strongly connected, therefore the Anosov vector field is transitive (Proposition
6.4.2
).

This construction is already known.
Indeed, the block given by Theorem
4
for the fake horseshoe is orbit equivalent to a block of geodesic flow on the compactified
modular orbifold
.
The modular orbifold is the quotient
S_{\mathrm{Mod}}:={\mathbb{H}}^{2}/\mathrm{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
.
It is a hyperbolic orbifold, which is a sphere with two singular points of order 2 and 3 and a
cusp
.
It is a known result (
[
Mil83
]
) that the unitary tangent bundle
T^{1}S_{\mathrm{Mod}}\simeq\mathrm{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{R}})/\mathrm{PSL}_{2}({\mathbb{Z}})
is homeomorphic to the complementary of the trefoil knot
K
in
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
, and the geodesic flow is the flow transverse to the fibration of
K
.
In
[
Ghy07
]
, the author shows that one can deform the surface
S_{\mathrm{Mod}}
so as to obtain a compact hyperbolic orbifold
\overline{S}_{\mathrm{Mod}}
with geodesic boundary.
The geodesic flow
X^{t}
induced on the tangent unitary fibered
P:=T^{1}\overline{S}_{\mathrm{Mod}}
is an Axiom A flow whose basic piece admits a Markov partition of geometric type the fake horseshoe (see for example
[
BP21
]
).
It is a building block with a single boundary component, two incoherently oriented periodic orbits in the boundary and a filling boundary lamination with no compact leaves other than the periodic orbits and no Reeb components.
It follows from the uniqueness in Theorem
4
that the block given by this proposition is isotopic to
(P,X)
.
In
[
CP21
]
, the authors show, in the manner of Handel-Thurston, the existence of a family of Anosov flows obtained by gluing back finite covers of two copies of
(P,X)
.

10.
Periodic orbit complement as JSJ piece of a transitive Anosov flow

The Jaco-Shalen-Johansson (JSJ) theorem (
[
JS78
]
,
[
Joh79
]
) allows us to split a compact, irreducible, orientable manifold of dimension three into components which admit a Seifert fibration, or which are atoroidal.
This splitting is done along embedded incompressible tori, and there is a minimal collection of such tori which is unique up to isotopy.
We recall that an embedded surface in a manifold is said to be
incompressible
if the embedding induces an injective morphism of the fundamental groups.
We recall that a Seifert fibration (
[
Hem04
]
) is a circle fibration over a 2-orbifold (possibly with boundary).
A manifold which admits such a structure (there may be several of them) is a
Seifert fibered space
.
There is a finite number of
singular fibers
, which are the fibers above the singular points of the orbifold.
The others are called
regular
.
A manifold is said to be
atoroidal
if every incompressible embedding of the torus is homotopic to a boundary component.

Theorem 10.0.1

(Jaco-Shalen, Johansson)
.

Let
{\mathcal{M}}
be a closed, orientable, irreducible manifold of dimension three.
Then there exists a finite collection
{\mathcal{T}}=\{T_{1},\dots,T_{n}\}
of incompressible pairwise disjoint embedded tori,
such that the closure of the connected components
P_{i}
of
{\mathcal{M}}{\smallsetminus}\cup_{i}T_{i}
are either Seifert fibers or atoroidal.
The decomposition is unique up to isotopy for
T_{i}
and
P_{i}
if the collection
{\mathcal{T}}
is minimal.

Any orientable 3-manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
which carries an Anosov vector field
X
is irreducible (since its universal covering is
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
,
[
Cal07
]
,
[
Bar05
]
), and thus admits a JSJ decomposition along incompressible tori.
Let
P
be a Seifert piece of the JSJ decomposition of
{\mathcal{M}}
. We say that

•

P
is a
periodic Seifert piece
if there exists a Seifert fibration on
P
such that the regular fiber is homotopic to a power of a periodic orbit of
X
.

•

P
is a
free Seifert piece
otherwise.

Definition 10.0.2

(Pseudo-Anosov flow)
.

A pseudo-Anosov flow
X^{t}
on a closed 3-manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
is a flow which is locally modeled on a semi-branched covering of an Anosov flow.

We follow the definition of
[
Cal07
, Definition 6.41]
.
A pseudo-Anosov flow is thus a continuous flow, differentiable outside a finite collection of periodic orbits
\Gamma_{*}=\{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\}
, called
singular orbits
.
On the complementary of these orbits, the flow admits a hyperbolic splitting, in other words it preserves a splitting
T{\mathcal{M}}=E^{ss}\oplus{\mathbb{R}}.X\oplus E^{uu}
of the tangent bundle above
{\mathcal{M}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma_{*}
, uniformly expands the vectors of
E^{uu}
and uniformly contracts the vectors of
E^{ss}
in the future.
Equivalently, we say that
X
is a
pseudo-Anosov vector field
.
The bundles
E^{ss}\oplus{\mathbb{R}}X
and
E^{uu}\oplus{\mathbb{R}}X
are uniquely integrable into
X^{t}
-invariant foliations
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{u}
of dimension 2 called respectively the (weak) stable and (weak) unstable foliations, which admit
p_{i}
-prong singularities with
p_{i}\geq 3
along the
\gamma_{i}
orbits (Figure
79
).
In the neighborhood of a singular orbit
\gamma_{i}
, the coordinates of the semi-branched covering are of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
outside the singularity.

Remark 10.0.3
.

There is another common definition of a
topological pseudo-Anosov flow
, for example used in
[
BF13
, Definition 2.1]
, where one considers a flow only continuous on
{\mathcal{M}}
.
We refer to
[
AT22
, Definition 5.8]
to compare the two definitions.
The authors show in this paper (
[
AT22
, Theorem 5.10]
) that a transitive topological pseudo-Anosov flow is orbit equivalent to a transitive pseudo-Anosov flow (in the smooth sense).
This is a generalization (more or less immediate) of M. Shannon’s theorem
[
Sha20
]
which states that a topologically transitive Anosov flow is orbit equivalent to a transitive Anosov flow.

Figure 79
.
Transverse image of a
3
-prong singularity in a pseudo-Anosov flow

The purpose of this chapter is to show Theorem
F
, which allows us to realize atoroidal JSJ pieces in a transitive Anosov flow as a complement of periodic orbits of transitive Anosov or pseudo-Anosov flow.

Theorem 5

(Theorem
F
)
.

Let
\Gamma=\{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\}
be a finite collection of periodic orbits of a transitive pseudo-Anosov vector field
X
on an orientable 3-manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Assume that all the singular orbits of
X
are contained in
\Gamma
and that the complementary
{\mathcal{M}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma
is atoroidal.
Then there exists an orientable 3-manifold
{\mathcal{N}}
carrying a transitive Anosov vector field
Y
such that the JSJ decomposition of
{\mathcal{N}}
is made of two atoroidal pieces
P
and
P^{\prime}
, both homeomorphic to
{\mathcal{M}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma
, and a periodic Seifert piece.
The restriction of
Y
to
P
and
P^{\prime}
is obtained from
X
by a DA bifurcation on the orbits of
\Gamma
.

Remark 10.0.4
.

The tori
T_{i}
in the JSJ collection of
{\mathcal{N}}
are each isotopic to a quasi-transverse torus
T_{i}^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{N}}
, but the collection of
T_{i}^{\prime}
is not embedded: the tori intersect along periodic orbits.
This property will be checked during the proof, but we refer to
[
BF13
]
for an analysis of the quasi-transverse position of JSJ tori in a dimension 3 Anosov flow.
An obstruction to embed the “modified” collection into quasi-transverse position occurs for tori adjacent to periodic Seifert pieces.

In fact, we will prove the following more general statement:

Theorem 10.0.5
.

Let
\Gamma
be a finite collection of periodic orbits of a transitive pseudo-Anosov vector field
X
on an orientable 3-manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
, containing the set of singular periodic orbits of
X
.
Then there exists an orientable 3-manifold
{\mathcal{N}}
with a transitive Anosov vector field
Y
, and a collection
{\mathcal{T}}
of incompressible tori embedded in
{\mathcal{N}}
and not homotopic,
which cut
{\mathcal{N}}
into three components
P_{1},P_{2},P_{3}
, where
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are homeomorphic to
{\mathcal{M}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma
and
P_{3}
admits a Seifert fibration with a fiber homotopic to a multiple of a periodic orbit.
The restriction of
Y
to
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
is obtained from
X
by a DA bifurcation on the orbits of
\Gamma
.

Theorem
5
follows directly from Theorem
10.0.5
in the case
{\mathcal{M}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma
is atoroidal, since the collection
{\mathcal{T}}
is then the JSJ collection of tori, and
P_{3}
is a periodic Seifert fibration.

Section summary

The section is organized as follows

•

In Subsection
10.1
we give a general procedure for constructing a filled transitive block by
Double blow up – Excision
of a collection
\Gamma
of periodic orbits of a pseudo-Anosov flow on a manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
containing the set of singular periodic orbits (Proposition
10.1.2
)
The resulting block is homeomorphic to the complementary
{\mathcal{M}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma
of the union of periodic orbits of the collection, and the boundary lamination is
coherent elementary
on each boundary component.

•

In Subsection
10.2
, we show a general result that allows us to “add” a compact (unmarked) leaf on an boundary lamination of a building block
(P,X)
by gluing an “elementary” intermediate block, which is a tubular neighborhood of a periodic saddle orbit with negative multipliers.
Topologically, the block thus obtained is the union of
P
and a “simple” Seifert fibered piece, adjoined by an incompressible torus.
This result is summarized in Proposition
10.2.1
.

•

In Subsection
10.3
, we show Theorem
10.0.5
using the previous results.
We construct a block
(P,X)
by
Double Blow-up – Excision
along the collection of orbits given by Proposition
10.1.2
, and add compact leaves on each connected component of the complement of the periodic orbits of the boundary with a well-chosen dynamical orientation using Proposition
10.2.1
.
The resulting block
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
is filled, transitive, and glues back to a copy of
(P,X)
via a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map
\varphi
.
We use the Gluing Theorem and the transitivity criterion, and the Anosov flow obtained will satisfy Theorem
10.0.5
.

•

In Subsection
10.4
, we give as a corollary a criterion for the complementary of a
hyperbolic knot
in
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
to be realized as an atoroidal JSJ piece of a transitive Anosov flow.
We describe a construction by
Hopf plumbing
which gives a family of knots satisfying this criterion.

10.1.
Building block by
Double blow up – Excision

In this preliminary section, we present the general result of creating building block by
Double blow-up – Excision
of periodic orbits.
We refer to Subsection
7.2
for the definition of a quasi-Morse-Smale combinatorial type (Definitions
7.2.7
and
7.2.9
).

Definition 10.1.1

(Elementary, Coherent, Alternating quasi-Morse-Smale prefoliation)
.

A quasi-Morse-Smale prefoliation on the torus is said to be

•

elementary
if it admits no compact leaves other than marked leaves,

•

coherent
if the oriented marked leaves are all freely homotopic,

•

alternating
if any oriented marked leaf is freely homotopic to the successive marked leaf for a cyclic order.

Proposition 10.1.2
.

Let
\Gamma=\{\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{n}\}
be a collection of periodic orbits of a pseudo-Anosov vector field
X
on an orientable closed manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
of dimension 3, which contains all (possible) singular orbits of
X
.
There exists a building block
(P,Y)
such that

(1)

(manifold)

P={\mathcal{M}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{V}}
, where
{\mathcal{V}}
