is a union of disjoint tubular neighborhoods of the orbits
\gamma_{i}
;

(2)

(boundary lamination)

the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
is filling elementary coherent, each connected component
T_{i}
of
\partial P
contains
2p_{i}
periodic orbits, where
p_{i}
is the number of stable separatrices of the orbit
\gamma_{i}
;

(3)

(transitivity)

if
X
is transitive, then
(P,Y)
is a transitive block

We say that
(P,Y)
is a building block obtained by
Double blow-up – Excision
on the collection
\Gamma
of periodic orbits of the pseudo-Anosov vector field
X
.

In particular,
P
is homeomorphic to the complementary
{\mathcal{M}}{\smallsetminus}\Gamma
.

Idea of the proof.

We explain this construction for a single periodic orbit
\gamma
of an Anosov flow, the generalization for a finite collection
\Gamma
being similar.
Let us assume for the sake of argument that the multipliers of
\gamma
are negative.
We make an attracting DA bifurcation on the periodic orbit
\gamma
.
We refer to
[
GHS97
, Subsection 2.2.2]
and
[
BBY17
, Section 8]
for a detailed description of this bifurcation, and to Figure
69
.
Let us also mention
[
BBGRH21
, Section 3.3]
, where the authors give an explicit construction of an DA bifurcation on a codimension 2 geodesic flow, but which can be adapted in our case.

This bifurcation consists in “opening” the stable manifold of
\gamma
, and creates an attracting periodic orbit
\gamma_{+}
in place of
\gamma
and a saddle periodic orbit
\gamma^{\prime}
with positive multipliers in place of the boundary of the local stable manifold of
\gamma
.
More precisely, there exists a small perturbation
X^{\prime}
of
X
on
{\mathcal{M}}
such that
X^{\prime}
coincides with
X
outside a small tubular neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}_{0}
of the
\gamma
orbit, and such that the vector field
X^{\prime}
on
{\mathcal{V}}_{0}
admits an attracting periodic orbit
\gamma_{+}
with negative multipliers, and a saddle periodic orbit
\gamma
with negative multipliers homotopic to the double of
\gamma_{+}
.
We then make a repelling Anosov derivative bifurcation on the periodic orbit
\gamma^{\prime}
, which consists in “opening” the unstable manifold of
\gamma^{\prime}
.
More precisely, there exists a small perturbation
X^{\prime\prime}
of
X^{\prime}
on
{\mathcal{M}}
such that
X^{\prime}
coincides with
X
outside a small tubular neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}_{0}
of the orbit
\gamma^{\prime}
, and such that the vector field
X^{\prime\prime}
on
{\mathcal{V}}^{\prime}_{0}
admits a repelling periodic orbit
\gamma^{\prime}_{-}
of positive multipliers instead of
\gamma^{\prime}
and two saddle periodic orbits
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
, instead of the boundary of the local unstable manifold of
\gamma^{\prime}
, freely homotopic to
\gamma^{\prime}_{-}
and with positive multipliers, and the vector field has not been modified in the neighborhood of
\gamma^{+}
(Figure
80
).
There exists a torus
T
which is the union of two annuli
A_{+}
and
A_{-}
such that:

•

the boundary of
A_{+}
is the union of the periodic orbits
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
and the interior of
A_{+}
is contained in the basin of attraction
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(\gamma_{+})
of
\gamma_{+}
, and is transverse to the vector field
X^{\prime\prime}
;

•

the boundary of
A_{-}
is the union of the periodic orbits
\gamma^{\prime}_{1}
and
\gamma^{\prime}_{2}
and its interior is contained in the basin of repulsion
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(\gamma^{\prime}_{-})
of
\gamma^{\prime}_{-}
, and is transverse to the vector field
X^{\prime\prime}

The torus
T
is drawn in Figure
80
.
It follows that
T
is a torus quasi-transverse to the vector field
X^{\prime\prime}
which contains the two saddle periodic orbits
\gamma^{\prime}_{i}
.
It is the boundary of a solid torus
{\mathcal{V}}
contained in
{\mathcal{V}}_{0}
and which contains in its interior the attracting periodic orbit
\gamma_{+}
and the repelling periodic orbit
\gamma^{\prime}_{-}
.
Let
P:={\mathcal{M}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{V}}
, and denote
Y
the restriction of
X^{\prime\prime}
to
P
.

Figure 80
.
Double blow-up and Excision on an orbit
\gamma
with negative multipliers

Then
(P,Y)
is a building block satisfying Proposition
10.1.2
.
We refer to
[
BBGRH21
, Proposition 3.8]
for a detailed proof of the hyperbolicity of the maximal invariant set which we can easily adapt to our case.
For the rest, we refer to the proof of Lemma
7.4.2
.

If
\gamma
has positive multipliers, the first attracting DA bifurcation produces two saddle periodic orbits
\gamma_{1}
and
\gamma_{2}
and one attracting periodic orbit
\gamma_{+}
, all with positive multipliers.
We then perform a repelling DA bifurcation on
\gamma_{1}
and on
\gamma_{2}
.
This creates two repelling periodic orbits
\gamma_{1,-}
and
\gamma_{2,-}
and four saddle periodic orbits
\gamma_{1,1}
and
\gamma_{1,2}
and
\gamma_{2,1}
and
\gamma_{2,2}
.
There exists then a solid torus
{\mathcal{V}}
whose boundary is a torus
T
quasi-transverse to the vector field
X^{\prime\prime}
, which contains the four saddle periodic orbits
\gamma_{i,j}
and the interior of
{\mathcal{V}}
contains the attracting and repelling periodic orbits.
We show in the same way that the manifold
P={\mathcal{M}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{V}}
equipped with the vector field
Y
equal to the restriction of
X^{\prime\prime}
is a building block which satisfies the lemma.

In the case where
X
is a pseudo-Anosov vector field and
\gamma
is the unique singular orbit of
p
-prong type, the procedure is essentially the same and this operation will have the effect of “blowing-up” the
p
-prong singularity.
Let us explain briefly.
Suppose that
\gamma
admits
p
-stable separatrices and
p
-unstable separatrices.
We perform an attracting bifurcation
Derived from Pseudo-Anosov (DpA)
on the orbit
\gamma
, in other words a bifurcation analogous to the DA bifurcation, which consists in opening each of the stable separatrices of
\gamma
.
More precisely, there exists a tubular neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}
of
\gamma
on which the flow of
X
is orbit equivalent to a semi-branched covering of a saddle hyperbolic periodic orbit.
This neighborhood is partitioned into
p
regions
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
,
i=1,\dots,p
, each bordered by two unstable separatrices of
\gamma
, and on each of these regions there are coordinates
(x,y,t):{\mathcal{V}}_{i}\to{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\times{\mathbb{R}}\times{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
, of regularity
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
outside
x=y=0
, in which the flow of
X
is the suspension of
(x,y)\mapsto(\lambda^{-1}x,\lambda y)
, with
\lambda>1
, restricted to the half space.
We can then return to the attracting DA bifurcation of the previous case on each region
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
and to the explicit descriptions of
[
BBGRH21
, Section 3.3]
or
[
BBY17
, Section 8]
in linearizing coordinates restricted to the half-space
x\geq 0
.
We obtain a new vector field
X_{i}^{\prime}
on each
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
, of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
outside
x=y=0
.
The blow-up vector fields
X_{i}^{\prime}
on the region
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
reconnect in a
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
-way along the unstable separatrices by a reflection, and we obtain a field
X^{\prime}
of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
outside
\gamma
.
This vector field
X^{\prime}
coincides with
X
outside a small tubular neighborhood of
\gamma
, admits an attracting periodic orbit
\gamma^{+}
in place of
\gamma
and
p
periodic
\gamma_{i}
orbits in place of each boundary component of the local unstable separatrices of
\gamma
, and which are now saddle hyperbolic periodic orbits in the neighborhood of which the flow is of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
.
The hyperbolicity (of index
(1,1)
) for the orbits of the flow of
X^{\prime}
on the complementary of the basin of attraction of
\gamma^{+}
can be shown by following the proof of
[
BBGRH21
, Proposition 3.8]
by cone criterion.
We can reduce to coordinates
(x,y,t)
on each region
{\mathcal{V}}_{i}
and adapt the proof for half-spaces
x\geq 0
.
We then perform a repelling DA bifurcation on each of these
p
saddle hyperbolic orbits in small and disjoint neighborhoods, far from
\gamma^{+}
.
We can then return to the classical construction, and we obtain a vector field
X^{\prime\prime}
which coincides with
X^{\prime}
outside these small neighborhoods, of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
outside
\gamma^{+}
, which admits
p
repelling periodic orbits
\gamma_{i}^{-}
in place of
\gamma_{i}
, and
2p
saddle hyperbolic periodic orbits in place of the two boundaries of the local stable separatrices of the
p
orbits
\gamma_{i}
.
The saddle hyperbolicity for the orbits in the complementary of the union of the repulsion basins of
\gamma_{i}^{-}
and the attraction basin of
\gamma_{i}^{+}
is shown by following the proof
[
BBGRH21
, Proposition 3.8]
, since we are far from the singularity.
There exists a torus
T
quasi-transverse to the vector field
X^{\prime\prime}
described in Figure
81
, which contains each of the
2p
saddle hyperbolic periodic orbits, and the complementary of these orbits in
T
are annuli contained in the basins of attraction of
\gamma^{+}
and basins of repulsion of
\gamma_{i}^{-}
.
The torus
T
is the boundary of a solid torus
{\mathcal{V}}
and contains in its interior the attracting periodic orbit
\gamma^{+}
and the repelling periodic orbits
\gamma_{i}^{-}
.
Let
P:={\mathcal{M}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{V}}
, and denote
Y
the restriction of
X^{\prime\prime}
to
P
.
We then show by analogous arguments that the pair
(P,Y)
is a building block which satisfies the proposition.

Figure 81
.
Double Blow-up and Excision on a
3
-prong singularity

∎

10.2.
Addition of compact leaf

A method is described for “adding” a compact leaf to the boundary lamination of a building block
(P,X)
.
Topologically, this operation has the effect of gluing a solid torus along an annulus on a boundary component of
P
.
We will describe this operation using partial gluing maps of intermediate blocks (Definition
9.1.1
).
We use the results of Section
9
for the correspondence between building blocks and intermediate blocks.

Let
(P,X)
be an orientable filled building block, and
T
be a boundary component of
{\partial P}
which contains a nonempty set of periodic orbits of
X
.
We denote
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
the boundary lamination and
\Lambda_{X}
the maximal invariant set.
Let
B
be an annulus of
T
bounded by two compact leaves
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
of
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
and containing no compact leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}
on its interior.
Equip
P
with the orientation such that a geometric enumeration of the compact leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
satisfy
\gamma_{n-1}=\gamma
and
\gamma_{0}=\gamma^{\prime}
, i.e.
\gamma^{\prime}
is the first leaf, and
\gamma
is to the left of
\gamma^{\prime}
for the dynamical orientation of
\gamma^{\prime}
and the orientation of
T
.
Let
\sigma
be the associated combinatorial type of
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
.

Proposition 10.2.1
.

For any combinatorial type
\sigma^{\prime}
on
{\mathbb{Z}}/(n+1){\mathbb{Z}}
such that the restriction of
\sigma^{\prime}
to
\{0,\dots,n-1\}
coincides with
\sigma
, there exists
(Q,Y)
a oriented filled building block, such that

(1)

(boundary)

\partial P\simeq\partial Q
;

(2)

(Smale’s graph)

the Smale’s graph of
(Q,Y)
is the Smale’s graph of
(P,X)
where we add a vertex
O
and
n
oriented edges
O\rightarrow\Lambda_{i}
if
B\subset P^{\mathrm{in}}
, or
n
oriented edges
\Lambda_{i}\rightarrow O
if
B\subset P^{\mathrm{out}}
, where
O
is a saddle periodic orbit, and
\Lambda_{1},\dots,\Lambda_{n}
are the basic pieces of
\Lambda_{X}
whose invariant manifold intersect the annulus
B
.

(3)

(combinatorial type)

there exists
T^{\prime}
a connected component of
\partial Q
such that:

•

the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
on
T^{\prime}
has combinatorial type
\sigma^{\prime}
, and the
(n+1)
-th compact leaf is the intersection of the invariant manifold of
O
with
\partial Q
;

•

{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
and
{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
extends as topologically equivalent foliations on
\partial Q{\smallsetminus}T^{\prime}
and
\partial Q{\smallsetminus}T
respectively.

(4)

(topology)

there exists a torus
T^{\prime\prime}
embedded in the interior of
Q
, isotopic to a torus quasi-transverse to
Y
, which cuts
Q
into two pieces
P_{0}
and
P_{1}
such that
P_{0}\simeq P
and
P_{1}
is a Seifert fibered space adjacent to
T^{\prime}
and
\partial Q
, whose regular fiber is freely homotopic to the periodic orbits of
Y
contained in
T^{\prime}
.

Figure 82
.
An addition of compact leaf

Item
3
means that the boundary lamination of
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
is essentially the same as the boundary lamination of
(P,X)
, where a compact leaf has been added to the middle of the annulus
B
bounded by
\gamma
and
\gamma^{\prime}
.

Remark 10.2.2
.

If
\sigma^{\prime}
is a combinatorial type which coincides with a combinatorial type
\sigma
on the complementary of an integer in
{\mathbb{Z}}/(n+1){\mathbb{Z}}
, then this integer does not correspond to a marked leaf, by parity of the marked leaves.
This process thus allows us to add compact unmarked leaves to a boundary lamination.

A first step is to build an “elementary” intermediate block (Definition
9.1.1
), containing a single periodic orbit.

Lemma and Definition 10.2.3
.

There exists an intermediate block
(V_{e},X_{e})
, such that :

(1)

(boundary)

\partial V_{e}=V^{\mathrm{in}}_{e}\cup V^{\mathrm{tan}}_{e}\cup V_{e}^{\mathrm{out}}
, where the entrance boundary
V_{e}^{\mathrm{in}}
is a single annulus, the exit boundary
V^{\mathrm{out}}
is a single annulus, and the tangent boundary
V_{e}^{\mathrm{tan}}
is the union of two annuli tangent to the vector field
X_{e}
.

(2)

(maximal invariant set)

the maximal invariant set is a single saddle periodic orbit
O
with negative multipliers,

(3)

(boundary lamination)

the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{X_{e}}
is reduced to two compact leaves, one
\gamma^{s}
on
V^{\mathrm{in}}_{e}
and one
\gamma^{u}
on
V^{\mathrm{out}}_{e}
, corresponding to the intersections of the stable and unstable separatrix of
O
respectively.

(4)

(topology)

V_{e}
is homeomorphic to a solid torus
{\mathbb{D}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
, it is a Seifert fibered space whose regular fiber is freely homotopic to the trace of the stable manifold
\gamma^{s}
, and has a unique singular fiber homotopic to the periodic orbit
O
.

Such a block is unique up to orbit equivalence.
It is called the
elementary intermediate block
.

Figure 83
.
Elementary block
(V_{e},X_{e})
and boundary lamination

Proof.

Consider the suspension of the matrix
A\colon(x,y)\mapsto(-\frac{1}{2}x,2y)
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times{\mathbb{R}}/{\mathbb{Z}}
, and
O
the periodic orbit induced by
(0,0)
.
Let
V_{e}
be a tubular neighborhood of
O
whose boundary consists of two tangent annuli and two transverse annuli, whose trace in the transverse plane
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}\times\{0\}
is described by Figure
83
.
The block
(V_{e},X_{e})
where
X_{e}
is the restriction of the suspension vector field satisfies the lemma.
If
(V,X)
is another intermediate block which satisfies Lemma
10.2.3
, up to push the boundary of
V
along the vector field we can assume that it is contained in a linearizing neighborhood of the unique periodic orbit
O
.
Saddle hyperbolic periodic orbits with negative multipliers are all orbit equivalent on a linearizing neighborhood.
We show with arguments similar to the proof of
9.1.4
that neighborhoods described by Figure
83
are orbit equivalent.
∎

We will need the following lemma to describe partial gluing of intermediate blocks.
Let
(\check{P},\check{X})
and
(\check{Q},\check{Y})
be two intermediate blocks.
Let
\partial\check{P}=\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}\cup\check{P}^{\mathrm{tan}}\cup\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}
denote the decomposition of the boundary into entrance boundary, tangent boundary and exit boundary, and
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
the boundary lamination of
(\check{P},\check{X})
.
We denote
\check{f}_{X}:\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}\to\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}
the crossing map of
\check{X}
.
Similarly,
\partial\check{Q}=\check{Q}^{\mathrm{in}}\cup\check{Q}^{\mathrm{tan}}\cup\check{Q}^{\mathrm{out}}
the boundary decomposition,
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
the boundary lamination of
(\check{Q},\check{Y})
.
Let us denote
\check{f}_{Y}:\check{Q}^{\mathrm{in}}\to\check{Q}^{\mathrm{out}}
the crossing map of
\check{Y}
.

Lemma 10.2.4
.

Let
C^{\mathrm{out}}
be a union of connected components of
\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}
and
C^{\mathrm{in}}
be a union of connected components of
\check{Q}^{\mathrm{in}}
, let
\varphi:C^{\mathrm{out}}\to C^{\mathrm{in}}
be a diffeomorphism which reverses the orientation and such that
\varphi_{*}\check{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
is transverse to
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
on
C^{\mathrm{in}}
.
Then the vector fields
\check{X}
and
\check{Y}
induce a vector field
\check{Z}
of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
on the manifold

\check{N}:=(\check{P}\cup\check{Q})\,/\varphi
, and we have

(1)

(intermediate block)

(\check{N},\check{Z})
is an intermediate block

(2)

(boundary)

\partial\check{N}=(\partial\check{P}{\smallsetminus}C^{\mathrm{out}})\cup_{\varphi}(\partial\check{Q}{\smallsetminus}C^{\mathrm{in}})
, and

•

the entrance boundary is

\check{N}^{\mathrm{in}}=\partial\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}\sqcup(\partial\check{Q}^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}C^{\mathrm{in}})

•

the exit boundary is

\check{N}^{\mathrm{out}}=(\partial\check{P}^{\mathrm{out}}{\smallsetminus}C^{\mathrm{out}})\sqcup\partial\check{Q}^{\mathrm{out}}

•

the tangent boundary is

\check{N}^{\mathrm{tan}}=\check{P}^{\mathrm{tan}}\cup_{\varphi}\check{Q}^{\mathrm{tan}}
.

(3)

(lamination)

If
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}
is the boundary lamination of
\check{N}
then:

\check{\mathcal{L}}_{Z}=\check{\mathcal{L}}_{X}\cup\check{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}\cup(\check{f}_{Y})_{*}(\varphi_{*}{\mathcal{L}}_{X}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}_{Y})\cup(\check{f}_{X}^{-1})_{*}(\varphi^{-1}_{*}{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}_{X})

Proof.

For an intermediate block
(\check{P},\check{X})
, if
c^{\mathrm{in}}
is a curve which borders a connected component of
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
and a connected component of
\check{P}^{\mathrm{tan}}
, then there exist local coordinates
(x,y,\theta)
in the neighborhood of any point of
c^{\mathrm{in}}
such that the vector field
\check{X}
is the vertical vector field
\partial_{y}
, the tangent boundary
\check{Q}^{\mathrm{tan}}
is the vertical plane
\{x=0\}
and the entrance boundary
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
is the horizontal plane
\{y=0\}
.
The same is true in the neighborhood of the points of a curve
c^{\mathrm{out}}
which borders a connected component of
\check{Q}^{\mathrm{in}}
and
\check{Q}^{\mathrm{tan}}
(Figure
84
).
It follows that the quotient space

\check{N}:=\check{P}\cup\check{Q}/\varphi
is a manifold with boundary, smooth outside a finite number of simple closed curves contained in the boundary, and a vector field
\check{Z}
of class
{\mathcal{C}}^{1}
induced by the vector fields
\check{X}
and
\check{Y}
and the pair
(\check{N},\check{Z})
satisfies Item
1
and
2
of Definition
9.1.1
of an intermediate block.
Item
2
of Lemma
10.2.4
is deduced from the construction.
Let us then remark that any orbit of the flow of
\check{Z}
which intersects the projection of
C^{\mathrm{in}}
in
\check{N}
never passes through it again, because each orbit of the flow of
\check{X}
intersects the exit boundary at most once, each orbit of the flow of
\check{Y}
intersects the entrance boundary at most once, and the blocks
(\check{P},\check{X})
and
(\check{Q},\check{Y})
are disjoint.
It follows that we can see
\varphi
as a
partial gluing map without cycle
(see Subsection
7.1
).
We can then adapt the proof of Proposition
7.1.1
for intermediate blocks: if
\varphi
maps the boundary lamination
\check{\mathcal{L}}
of
(\check{P},\check{X})
on
C^{\mathrm{in}}
on a lamination transverse to the boundary lamination
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
of
(\check{Q},\check{Y})
on
C^{\mathrm{in}}
, then the maximal invariant set
\check{\Lambda}_{Y}
of the pair
(\check{N},\check{Z})
is a hyperbolic set and the pair is a intermediate block.
Item
1
is verified.
Item
3
is deduced with considerations analogous to the proof of Lemma
7.1.2
concerning partial gluing without cycle of building blocks.

Figure 84
.
Gluing intermediate blocks

Proof of Proposition
10.2.1
.

We recall that
(P,X)
is a filled orientable block,

a boundary component of

Recall that we have fixed an annulus

of

The corresponding combinatorial type of lamination

\sigma

.
The vector field

is transverse to
B
and say exiting to fix the ideas.
Let
(\check{P},\check{X})
be the intermediate block associated to

by Lemma

Denote
\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}

the boundary lamination (Definition

and

It is a annulus and the boundary lamination

on the manifold

We want to compute the Smale’s graph of

Let

\Lambda_{X}

By Item

By definition of

We deduce:

Claim 10.2.5

The Smale’s graph of

Lemma

Since the lamination
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
is filling on
\check{P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
, then the lamination
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
on
\check{Q}^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{*}
is a lamination which contains a filling lamination, and the first item follows.
The lamination
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
on
C^{\prime\mathrm{out}}=V^{\mathrm{out}}_{e}
coincides with the union
\gamma^{u}\cup(f_{e})_{*}(\varphi_{*}\check{\mathcal{L}}_{X}{\smallsetminus}\gamma^{s})
.
As the lamination
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
is filling, so is the image by
f_{e}\circ\varphi
, so the lamination
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
is filling on
V^{\mathrm{out}}_{e}
.
The lamination
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
on the complementary of
\gamma^{u}
is topologically equivalent to the lamination
\check{\mathcal{L}}
on the complementary of
\varphi^{-1}_{*}\gamma^{s}
.
Recall that we chose
\varphi
so that
\varphi^{-1}_{*}\gamma^{s}
is contained in the interior of the annulus
\check{B}
defined earlier, bounded by two compact leaves of
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
and without compact leaves of
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
on the interior (Figure
85
).
Denoting
\check{B}^{\prime}=f_{e}(\check{B})
, the last item follows.
∎

Finally, let
(Q,Y)
be the building block associated to the intermediate block
(\check{Q},\check{Y})
by Lemma
9.1.8
.
Note that the periodic orbits of
Y
contained in
Q
are the same as the periodic orbits of
X
contained in
P
(via the embedding of
(P,X)
into
(Q,Y)
).
Indeed, it is enough to see on the associated intermediate blocks that the compact leaves which border the annuli
{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{*}
and the compact leaves which border the annuli
{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
in
\partial\check{P}
come from the separatrices of the same periodic orbits by the gluing construction.
We recall that these separatrices correspond to a single periodic orbit in the intermediate block, and this orbit is a periodic orbit contained in the boundary of the associated building block (see Figure
75
and Lemma
9.1.5
).
There is a bijective correspondence between the pairs of compact leaves bordering
{\mathcal{A}}_{*}
in the intermediate block, and the periodic orbits in the boundary of the building block (Remark
9.1.7
).

Item
1
of Proposition
10.2.1
is deduced from the fact that the surgery performed to go from
(P,X)
to
(Q,Y)
neither adds nor removes boundary components.
Topologically, a solid torus is glued together with
P
along a annulus in the component
T
of
\partial P
, resulting in a component
T^{\prime}
homeomorphic to
T
, and the other components are left intact.
Item
2
directly follows from Claim
10.2.5
and Item
1
, Lemma
9.1.8
.

Recall that Lemma
9.1.8
, Item
2
gives a diffeomorphism between
\check{Q}^{\mathrm{in}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{*}
and
\overline{Q}^{\mathrm{in}}
which maps the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{X}
on the lamination
\check{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{in}}_{X}
and a diffeomorphism between
\check{Q}^{\mathrm{out}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}_{*}
and
\overline{Q}^{\mathrm{out}}
which maps the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{X}
on the lamination
\check{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}_{X}
.
The laminations are topologically equivalent on these domains.
The annulus
\check{B}^{\prime}
is therefore isotopic to a annulus
B^{\prime}
in the exit boundary
Q^{\mathrm{out}}
, bounded by two compact leaves of the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
and containing a single compact leaf in its interior.
Let
T^{\prime}
be the connected component of
{\partial P}
which contains
B^{\prime}
.
Choose the first compact leaf of
{\mathcal{L}}_{Y}
on
T^{\prime}
to be the compact leaf which borders
B^{\prime}
and which corresponds to the leaf
\gamma^{\prime}
bordering
B
(by the correspondence given by topological equivalences and isotopies to go from
B
to
B^{\prime}
).
Denote
\sigma_{Y}
the associated combinatorial type.
Then according to Claim
10.2.6
and the topological equivalence of laminations,
\sigma_{Y}
is a combinatorial type on
{\mathbb{Z}}/(n+1){\mathbb{Z}}
which coincides with
\sigma
on
\{0,\dots,n-1\}
, and the
(n+1)
-th compact leaf is the unique intersection of
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(O)
with
\partial Q
.
We don’t know a priori if we have
\sigma_{Y}(n)=(*,\uparrow,*)
or
\sigma_{Y}(n)=(*,\downarrow,*)
, but up to replace the partial gluing map
\varphi:C^{\mathrm{out}}\to V^{\mathrm{in}}_{e}
in the proof by
\tau\circ\varphi
, where
\tau
is a diffeomorphism of
V^{\mathrm{in}}_{e}
which fixes
\gamma^{s}
, reverses its orientation, and preserves the global orientation, then the value of
\sigma_{Y}(n)
coincides with
\sigma^{\prime}(n)
, where
\sigma^{\prime}
is the combinatorial type prescribed in Proposition
10.2.1
.
Item
3
of Proposition
10.2.1
follows.

Let us show Item
4
.
By construction,
\check{P}
and
V_{e}
are embedded in
\check{Q}
, and the boundary
\partial\check{P}
cuts
\check{Q}
into a component homeomorphic to
\check{P}
, hence to
P
, and a component homeomorphic to
V_{e}
, hence to a Seifert fibered space.
Moreover, we know that the regular fiber of
V_{e}
is homotopic to the leaf
\gamma^{s}
of the boundary lamination of
V_{e}
, and
\gamma^{s}
is connected to a curve (freely) homotopic to the compact leaves of the lamination
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
on
\partial\check{P}
.
The block
P
is embedded in
\check{P}
and isotopic to
\check{P}
(Lemma
9.1.4
), and the compact leaves of the lamination of
\check{\mathcal{L}}_{X}
on
\partial\check{P}
are homotopic to the periodic orbits of
\check{\mathcal{L}}
contained in the boundary of
T
according to Item
2
, Lemma
9.1.4
.
Finally,
Q
is isotopic to
\check{Q}
, it follows that we can embed
P
into
Q
. Denote
T^{\prime\prime}
the boundary of
P
via this embedding.
Since
\check{Q}^{\mathrm{in}}=\check{P}^{\mathrm{in}}
and the boundary laminations coincide along the compact leaves on the entrance boundary, it follows that the free homotopy class of the periodic orbits of
X
contained in
T^{\prime\prime}
coincides with the free homotopy class of the periodic orbits of
Y
contained in
T^{\prime}\subset\partial Q
.
By a small isotopy of
T^{\prime\prime}
to make it disjoint from
T^{\prime}
, it cuts in
Q
a component homeomorphic to
P
and a Seifert fibered space whose fiber is homotopic to the periodic orbits of
Y
in
T^{\prime}
.
∎

Remark 10.2.7
.

From the proof we can see that the torus
T^{\prime\prime}
given by Item
4
of Proposition
10.2.1
is isotopic to a torus quasi-transverse to
Y
which contains the same periodic orbits as
T^{\prime}
(but is not isotopic to
T^{\prime}
).

10.3.
Proof of Theorem
F

Proof of Theorem
10.0.5
.

We show the proposition for a collection
\Gamma
reduced to a single orbit, the generalization for a collection being immediate.
Let
\gamma
be a periodic orbit of a transitive Anosov vector field or the unique singularity of a transitive pseudo-Anosov flow on an orientable 3-manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
.
Let
(P,Y)
be the building block obtained by
Double blow-up – Excision
on the orbit
\gamma
by Proposition
10.1.2
.
It is a connected transitive filled block with a single boundary component, and the boundary lamination is coherent and elementary, i.e., it admits no compact leaf other than the periodic orbits, and the oriented periodic orbits are freely homotopic in
\partial P
.
Let
2p
be the number of periodic orbits contained in
\partial P
.
Then we can iterate Proposition
10.2.1
of compact leaf addition for every annulus of
\partial P
bounded by two periodic orbits and without compact leaves inside, and we obtain a transitive filled saddle orientable connected block
(P^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
such that if
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
denotes the boundary lamination, then
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
contains a collection
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}^{\prime}
of
2p
coherently oriented periodic orbits, and on each connected component of
{\partial P}^{\prime}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
it admits a single compact leaf.
We further require that the dynamical orientation of each of these compact leaves is the reversed orientation of the periodic orbits.

Claim 10.3.1
.

There exists a diffeomorphism
\varphi:{\partial P}\to{\partial P}^{\prime}
which maps the oriented periodic orbits of
Y
in
{\partial P}
to the oriented periodic orbits of
Y^{\prime}
in
{\partial P}^{\prime}
and the exit boundary
P^{\mathrm{out}}
to the entrance boundary
P^{\prime\mathrm{in}}
, and such that
\varphi_{*}{\mathcal{L}}
is strongly quasi-transverse to
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
on
{\partial P}^{\prime}
.

Figure 86
.
Strongly quasi-transverse gluing of the laminations
{\mathcal{L}}
(full line) and
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
(dashed line)

We refer to Figure
86
.
The diffeomorphism
\varphi
induces a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map of the filled saddle block
(P\cup P^{\prime},Y\cup Y^{\prime})
.
Up to make a strong isotopy of the triple
(P\cup P^{\prime},Y\cup Y^{\prime},\varphi)
, we can apply the Gluing Theorem
1
and the vector fields
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
induce on the closed orientable manifold
{\mathcal{N}}:=(P\cup P^{\prime})/\varphi
an Anosov vector field
Z
.

Let us show that the graph
G=G(P\cup P^{\prime},Y\cup Y^{\prime},\varphi)
is strongly connected.
By transitivity of
(P,Y)
, the set
\Lambda
is a basic piece of
(P,Y)
, hence a vertex of
G
.
By iterating Item
2
of Proposition
10.2.1
, the Smale’s graph of
(P^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
is formed by a vertex
\Lambda_{0}
(corresponding to the maximal invariant set of a copy of
(P,Y)
) and
2p
vertex
O_{1},\dots,O_{2p}
, and an oriented edge
\Lambda_{0}\rightarrow 0_{i}
for
i
even, and an oriented edge
O_{i}\rightarrow\Lambda_{0}
for
i
odd (up to change the numbering), where each
O_{i}
is a saddle periodic orbit.
We show

Claim 10.3.2
.

The graph
G
is the union of the Smale’s graph of
(P,Y)
and of
(P^{\prime},Y^{\prime})
where we add the following edges:

•

\Lambda\rightarrow\Lambda_{0}
and
\Lambda_{0}\rightarrow\Lambda

•

\Lambda\rightarrow O_{i}
for
i
odd

•

O_{i}\rightarrow\Lambda
for
i
even

Proof.

We refer to Figure
87
.

Figure 87
.
Graph
G(P\cup P^{\prime},Y\cup Y^{\prime},\varphi)
with edges add by the gluing map in dashed line

If
O_{i}
is an isolated saddle periodic orbit of
\Lambda^{\prime}
such that
O_{i}\rightarrow\Lambda_{0}
(i.e. if
i
odd), then its stable manifold intersect
\partial P^{\prime}
in a compact leaf
\gamma^{s}_{i}
.
According to Figure
86
, and because the boundary laminations of both blocks are filling, we know that the image
\varphi(\gamma^{s}_{i})
intersect
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(\Lambda)
.
We deduce that there is an edge
\Lambda\rightarrow O_{i}
for
i
odd in the graph
G
.
The third item is deduced the same way.
Finally, the stable (unstable) manifold of
\Lambda_{0}
intersects each annuli in the entrance (exit) boundary of
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
along non-compact leaves.
Because the lamination is filling in both block, it follows that
\varphi({\mathcal{W}}^{s}(\Lambda_{0}))
intersect
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}(\Lambda)
and
\varphi({\mathcal{W}}^{u}(\Lambda_{0}))
intersect
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}(\Lambda)
.
The first item follows.
∎

It is now easy to see from Claim
10.3.2
that the graph
G
is strongly connected, and the Anosov flow
Z
is transitive according to Proposition
6.4.2
.

The manifolds
P
and
P^{\prime}
are both embedded in
{\mathcal{N}}
, by an embedding which realizes an orbit equivalence between the vector fields
Y
and
Y^{\prime}
and the corresponding restriction of the vector field
Z
.
By iterating Proposition
10.2.1
, we can show that there exists a torus
T
embedded in
P^{\prime}
which cuts in
P^{\prime}
a submanifold homeomorphic to
P
and a submanifold which admits a Seifert fibration whose fiber is homotopic to the periodic orbits contained in
\partial P^{\prime}
.
Indeed, in the second iteration of Proposition
10.2.1
, we glue a Seifert piece (the elementary block
(V_{e},X_{e})
) to a Seifert piece whose regular fiber is homotopic to the periodic orbits (this is the submanifold adjacent to the boundary given by Item
4
of Proposition
10.2.1
applied once), and according to the construction we glue fiber to fiber.
The union forms a unique Seifert piece whose fiber is homotopic to the periodic orbits contained in
\partial P^{\prime}
, and we continue by recurrence.
Moreover the torus
T
is isotopic to a torus quasi-transverse in the vector field
Y^{\prime}
(Remark
10.2.7
).

Let
T_{1}
be the projection of
\partial P
in
{\mathcal{N}}
, and
T_{2}
the projection of the torus
T\subset\operatorname{int}P^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{N}}
.
The torus
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
are disjoint and both isotopic to a torus quasi-transverse to the Anosov field
Z
and therefore incompressible (
[
Bar95
]
,
[
Bru93
]
).
They cut two submanifolds
P_{1}
and
P_{2}
both homeomorphic to
P
, and such that the vector field
Z
on
P_{1}
and on
P_{2}
is orbit equivalent to
Y
on
P
, and a third component
P_{3}
which is a Seifert fibration, bounded by the torus
T_{1}
and
T_{2}
.
The fiber of the fibration of
P_{3}
is homotopic to a periodic orbit of the vector field, in other word
P_{3}
is a periodic Seifert fibered space.
∎

Remark 10.3.3
.

For a generalization to a collection of periodic orbits the proof is the same, but we have to justify that we can choose a gluing map which reverses the orientation.
We construct a building block
(P,X)
by Double-blow up – Excision on a collection of
n
periodic orbits, this block has
n
boundary components.
Let
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
be the block associated by the (iterated) Proposition
10.2.1
where we have added a compact leaf between each periodic orbit of the lamination, with a dynamical orientation reversed to those of the periodic orbits.
We pair naturally the boundary components of
P
and
P^{\prime}
and there is a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map
\varphi:{\partial P}\to{\partial P}^{\prime}
given by Figure
86
which realizes this pairing.
Note that if
P
has an orientation and
P^{\prime}
has the reversed orientation (the one which coincides with the reversed orientation of
P
seen as a submanifold of
P^{\prime}
), then the diffeomorphism
\varphi
reverses the orientation (on each boundary component).
It follows that the glued manifold is orientable.

10.4.
Knot complement in
S^{3}
as atoroidal JSJ piece

A knot is an embedding of the circle
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
to the 3-sphere
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
.
A link is an embedding of
n
copies of the circle
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
to
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
.
We usually confuse a knot (link) with its
isotopy class
in
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
.
We refer to
[
Ada04
]
for a general reference.
Theorem
5
allows us to realize the complementary of some periodic orbits of transitive (pseudo-)Anosov flow as JSJ pieces of transitive Anosov flow.
One can then ask if there exist knots
K
in
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
whose complementary
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}{\smallsetminus}K
is homeomorphic to the complementary of a periodic orbit
\gamma
of a transitive Anosov flow or of the unique singular orbit of a transitive pseudo-Anosov flow.
We are interested in this question for knots whose complementary in
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
is atoroidal.
Such knots are called
hyperbolic
.

The Gordon-Luecke theorem states that the isotopy class of a knot
K
is determined by its complementary in
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
, i.e. if
K
and
K^{\prime}
are two knots such that the complements
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}{\smallsetminus}K
and
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}{\smallsetminus}K^{\prime}
are homeomorphic, then
K
and
K^{\prime}
are isotopic.
Knot theory is a very rich theory for low dimensional topology.
Let us quote for example the Lickorish-Wallace theorem which states that any connected closed orientable manifold of dimension 3 is obtained by a
Dehn surgery
on a link
L
in
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
(
[
Ada04
]
).

Figure eight knot

It is a known fact (
[
Thu97
]
) that the complementary of the figure eight knot
K_{8}
(Figure
88
) is atoroidal, and homeomorphic to the complementary of the periodic orbit
\gamma
of the matrix suspension flow

A:=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\
1&1\end{pmatrix}:{\mathbb{T}}^{2}\to{\mathbb{T}}^{2}

induced by the fixed point
(0,0)
.
The suspension field
X_{A}
is an Anosov vector field.
In
[
FW80
]
, the authors construct an Anosov flow by gluing two copies of a building block homeomorphic to the figure eight knot complement
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}{\smallsetminus}K_{8}
, obtained by blow-up and excision on the periodic orbit
\gamma
of
X_{A}
.
This Anosov flow has the particularity of not being transitive.
Theorem
5
gives the existence of a transitive Anosov flow whose JSJ decomposition is formed by two copies of
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}{\smallsetminus}K_{8}
, adjoined by a periodic Seifert piece
P
.
Note that the periodic Seifert piece can be described completely: it is the gluing of two copies of the elementary intermediate block
(V_{e},X_{e})
(Definition
10.2.3
).

Figure 88
.
The figure eight knot

Hyperbolic fibered knots

Let
K
be a knot in
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
.
We say that a compact orientable surface
S
embedded in
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
is a
Seifert surface
for
K
if
\partial S=K
.
We say that
K
is a
fibered knot
if the complementary
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}{\smallsetminus}K
admits a fibration over the circle with fiber a Seifert surface
S
of
K
.
The
monodromy
of a fibered knot is the monodromy
h:S\to S
of such a fibration, well-defined up to isotopy relative to
\partial S
.
A diffeomorphism
h:S\to S
is said to be
pseudo-Anosov
if there exists a pair of foliations with singularities
({\mathcal{F}}^{s},{\mathcal{F}}^{u})
, transverse to each other, and provided with a transverse measures, which are uniformly contracted and expanded by
h
respectively.
The laminations have a finite number of
p
-prong singularities
with
p\geq 3
(Figure
79
).
The suspension of a pseudo-Anosov diffeomorphism of a closed surface is a pseudo-Anosov flow in the sense of Definition
10.0.2
.
If the monodromy
h
of a fibered knot
K
admits a pseudo-Anosov representative, then the knot
K
is a hyperbolic knot and the complementary of
K
is homeomorphic to the complementary of a periodic orbit
\gamma
of the pseudo-Anosov suspension flow of
\hat{h}:\hat{S}\to\hat{S}
, where
\hat{h}
is the pseudo-Anosov diffeomorphism induced by
h
on the surface
\hat{S}
obtained by blowing down the boundary of
S
(
[
Thu79
]
).
The boundary of
S
induces a fixed point
\hat{p}
of
\hat{h}
, and
\gamma
is the suspension of
\hat{p}
.
If
h
admits no singularities on the interior of
S
, then
\gamma
is the unique singularity of the suspension pseudo-Anosov flow of
h
, and it follows as a corollary of Theorem
5

Corollary 10.4.1
.

If
K
is a fibered knot whose monodromy is pseudo-Anosov and admits no singularity in the interior of the fiber, then the complementary
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}{\smallsetminus}K
is an atoroidal JSJ piece of a transitive Anosov flow.

We are looking for a family of knots which satisfies the hypotheses of Corollary
10.4.1
.

Plumbings of the Seifert surface of the figure eight knot

I. Agol pointed out to me the following construction, allowing to exhibit a family of knots satisfying the hypotheses of Corollary
10.4.1
, which are the knots obtained by
plumbings of the Seifert surface of the figure eight knot
.
Let us explain the ideas briefly.

In
[
GK90
]
, the authors describe a construction by
Hopf plumbing
allowing to compute the invariant foliations of the monodromy of a hyperbolic fibered
2-bridge
knot.
Let
H_{g}
be the left Hopf band, and
H_{d}
the right Hopf band described in Figure
89
.
The left Hopf link
\partial H_{g}
is a fibered knot with monodromy a right Dehn twist
D_{d}
along the core, and the right Hopf link
\partial H_{d}
is a fibered knot with monodromy a left Dehn twist
D_{g}
(Figure
89
).
The
Hopf plumbing
is the surface
S:=H_{1}\#H_{2}
obtained by gluing two unlinked Hopf bands in
{\mathbb{S}}^{3}
together along a rectangular neighborhood of an arc which crosses the band from boundary to boundary (Figure
89
), with the orientation convention given by Figure
89
.
If
D_{i}
is the monodromy of
H_{i}
, then
\partial(H_{1}\#H_{2})
is a fibered knot of fiber
H_{1}\#H_{2}
and monodromy the product of
D_{i}
(
[
Sta78
]
).

Figure 89
.

Consider
S:=H_{g}^{1}\#H_{d}^{1}\#\dots\#H_{g}^{n}\#H_{d}^{n}
a
2n
Hopf plumbing of alternating left band and right band, and denote
K:=\partial S
.

Lemma 10.4.2
.

K
satisfies the assumptions of Corollary
10.4.1
, i.e.
K
is a fibered knot whose monodromy is pseudo-Anosov and admits no singularity on the interior of the fiber.

Proof idea.

The surface
S
is a surface of genus
n
, and the cores of the Hopf bands are the curves drawn in Figure
90
.
By construction, the boundary
K:=\partial S
is a fibered knot of monodromy the product
h=D_{d}^{1}D_{g}^{1}\dots D_{d}^{n}D_{g}^{n}
of
2n
Dehn twists alternating right and left along the core of each of the Hopf strips.
The surface
S
is a second order branching covering of the disk
D
which is a quotient of
S
by the involution around the horizontal axis of Figure
90
, provided with
2n+1
marked points corresponding to the branching points.
The cores of the Hopf bands are arcs connecting the marked points, and the diffeomorphism
h:S\to S
induces a diffeomorphism
g:D\to D
, which is a product of “half”-twists of Dehn along the arcs, alternated on the left and on the right, denoted
\alpha_{g}^{i}
and
\alpha_{d}^{i}
.

Figure 90
.
Monodromy on
S
and covering
S\to D

In
[
GK90
]
, the authors use the method of
invariant train tracks
(
[
PH92
]
) to construct a
g
-invariant train track obtained from the invariant
subtracks

\tau_{g}
and
\tau_{d}
of the half-twists
\alpha_{g}^{i}
and
\alpha_{d}^{i}
.
They are described in Figure
91
.
The invariant train track
\tau
is a recollection of the subtracks
\tau_{g}
and
\tau_{d}
which overlap on monogons (Figure
91
).
The authors show (
[
GK90
, Proposition 4]
) that
g
is isotopic relative to
\partial D
to a pseudo-Anosov homeomorphism of
\hat{g}
of
D
. Moreover if we denote
\lambda^{s}
the stable invariant lamination of
\hat{g}
on
D
, then a
n
-gone of
D{\smallsetminus}\tau
corresponds to a disk with
n
cusps of
D{\smallsetminus}\lambda^{s}
.
We then observe that, in the particular situation of an alternating left and right Hopf plumbing, these connected components are disks with one or two cusps, and it follows that the stable foliation with singularities
f^{s}
of the pseudo-Anosov homeomorphism
\hat{g}
that extends the
\lambda^{s}
lamination admits only singularities of type
1
-prong.

Figure 91
.

Passing to the
2
order covering on
S
, it follows that (up to isotopy relative to
\partial S
), the monodromy
f
of the knot
K
is pseudo-Anosov, and its foliations have no singularities on the interior
S
.
∎

Finally, let us notice that the Seifert surface of the figure eight knot is a plumbing of a right Hopf band and a left Hopf band.
We deduce:

Corollary 10.4.3
.

Let
K=\partial(S_{1}\#S_{2}\#\dots\#S_{n})
be a plumbing of
n
copies of the Seifert surface of the figure eight knot.
Then
K
is a fibered hyperbolic knot and the complementary of
K
is an atoroidal JSJ piece of a transitive Anosov flow.

11.
Gluing pieces of skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov flows

In this section, we show a general result which allows us to glue pieces cut along incompressible tori in a skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov flow on a toroidal 3-manifold.
An Anosov flow on a 3-manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
is said to be
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered
if the leaf space of the lifted stable foliations
\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}^{s}
on the universal cover
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}
is separated (hence homeomorphic to
{\mathbb{R}}
).
It is said to be
skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered
if it is moreover not orbit equivalent to a suspension.
The proposition below allows us to cut building blocks out of a a skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov flow along a collection of incompressible tori.

Proposition 11.0.1

(
[
Bar95
, Theorem A’ and Theorem E]
)
.

Let
Z
be a skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov vector field on a closed orientable 3-manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
, whose stable and unstable foliations are transversely orientable.
Let
{\mathcal{T}}=\{T_{1},\dots,T_{n}\}
be a finite collection of incompressible tori embedded in
{\mathcal{M}}
, pairwise disjoint and pairwise non-homotopic.
Then there exists a collection
{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}=\{T^{\prime}_{1},\dots,T^{\prime}_{n}\}
of pairwise disjoint tori isotopic to
{\mathcal{T}}
and quasi-transverse to
Z
, and this collection is unique up to homotopy along the orbits of the flow.
As a consequence, if we set
P:={\mathcal{M}}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{T}}^{\prime}
, then
(P,Z|_{P})
is a building block.

Proof.

According to
[
Bar95
, Theorem A’]
any incompressible torus
T_{i}
embedded in an
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov flow in an orientable 3-manifold, and whose foliations are transversely oriented is isotopic to a quasi-transverse torus
T^{\prime}_{i}
.
The torus
T^{\prime}_{i}
is unique up to homotopy along the orbits.
According to
[
Bar95
, Theorem E]
, we can choose the tori
T^{\prime}_{1},\dots T^{\prime}_{n}
pairwise disjoint.
If we set
P:={\mathcal{M}}{\smallsetminus}\cup_{i}T^{\prime}_{i}
, it is clear that the pair
(P,X):=(P,Z|_{P})
is a building block.
∎

Definition 11.0.2

(Skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov block)
.

In the setting of Proposition
11.0.1
, we call a
skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov block
any union of connected components of
(P,Z|_{P})
.

We show the following proposition

Theorem 6

(Theorem
H
)
.

Let
(P,X)
and
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
be two skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov blocks and
\varphi\colon{\partial P}\to{\partial P}^{\prime}
a gluing map.
There is a gluing map
\psi
isotopic to
\varphi
among gluing maps such that the vector fields
X
and
X^{\prime}
induce an Anosov vector field
Z
on the closed 3-manifold
{\mathcal{N}}:=P\cup P^{\prime}/\psi
.

Recall that skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov flow blocks are orientable, and we say that a diffeomorphism
\varphi\colon{\partial P}\to{\partial P}^{\prime}
reverses the orientation if there exists an orientation of
P
and
P^{\prime}
such that
\varphi
reverses the canonical orientation induced on the boundary
{\partial P}
and
{\partial P}^{\prime}
.

Remark 11.0.3
.

Any
piece of (finite cover of) geodesic flow
is a skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov block.
This result then generalizes Handel-Thurston or Clay-Pinsky gluing surgeries, and generalizations by T. Barbot and S. Fenley and gets rid of the
positivity
constraint on the isotopy class of the gluing map.
It allows us to use of much more general blocks than geodesic flow pieces.
For example, any Anosov flow obtained by Dehn-Goodman-Fried surgeries
of coherent orientations
on a suspension or a geodesic flow is skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered (
[
Fen94
]
).
It also allows us to glue blocks
(P,X)
and
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
which are not cut in the same Anosov flow.

Note that the statement does not require any assumption on the boundary lamination and the gluing map.
We will see (Lemma
11.2.1
) that the existence of a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map
\varphi\colon{\partial P}\to{\partial P}^{\prime}
is equivalent to a simple criterion of matching boundary components containing the same number of periodic orbits.

11.1.
Boundary lamination

The proof relies on the following key lemma.

Lemma 11.1.1
.

Let
(P,X)
be a skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov block.
Then
(P,X)
is a filled block, and the boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
of
(P,X)
is alternating elementary (Definition
10.1.1
), i.e. the only compact leaves of
{\mathcal{L}}
are the periodic orbits of
X
, and two successive periodic orbits have an orientation given by the flow which is opposite.

Proof.

Let
Z
be a skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov vector field on a 3-manifold
{\mathcal{M}}
which contains the block
(P,X)
.
Let
T_{i}
be a torus quasi-transverse at
Z
embedded in
{\mathcal{M}}
corresponding to a connected component of
{\partial P}
, and let
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
be the collection of periodic orbits of
Z
contained in
T_{i}
.
T. Barbot shows in
[
Bar95
]
that
T_{i}

is a gluing of elementary Birkhoff annuli, i.e. the traces of the stable and unstable foliations
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{u}
of the Anosov vector field on the closure of each of the connected components of
T_{i}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
are foliations of dimension 1 with no compact leaves other than the periodic orbits of the boundary and with no Reeb components.
In other words, the traces of the foliations
{\mathcal{F}}^{s}
and
{\mathcal{F}}^{u}
on
T_{i}
are elementary alternating.
The periodic orbits bordering a connected component of
T_{i}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
are then oppositely oriented by the flow of
X
, in other words they are freely homotopic to the inverse of the other.
The boundary lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
of the block
(P,X)
is a sublamination of the quasi-Morse-Smale foliations induced by the pair
({\mathcal{F}}^{s},{\mathcal{F}}^{u})
on
T_{i}
, it follows that the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
is elementary alternating, and so on any boundary component
T_{i}
.
Let us show that this is a filling lamination.
It is a prefoliation with no compact leaves other than the periodic orbits.
If it is not filling, there exists an annulus
A
in, say,
P^{\mathrm{in}}
bounded by two periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{i},{\mathcal{O}}_{j}
, and disjoint from the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
on its interior (Proposition
1.2.11
).
The periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
thus admits an unstable separatrix
{\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{+}({\mathcal{O}}_{i})
, which is disjoint from the lamination
{\mathcal{W}}^{s}
, and thus from
\Lambda
.
Indeed, an orbit of
\Lambda\cap({\mathcal{W}}^{u}_{+}({\mathcal{O}}_{i}))
in a linearizing neighborhood of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
has its stable manifold intersecting the boundary of
P
on the annulus
A
, which is a contradiction (Figure
92
).
It follows that (the unique) unstable separatrix of
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
in
P
is free.
It intersects
\partial P
along a compact leaf of lamination
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathrm{out}}
, which is not a periodic orbit.
This is a contradiction.
∎

Figure 92
.
A periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}_{i}
which borders a component of
{\partial P}{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{L}}
has a free separatrix

11.2.
Criterion for the existence of a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map

We show the following lemma, which gives a simple necessary and sufficient criterion for the existence of a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map.

Lemma 11.2.1
.

Let
(P,X)
and
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
be two oriented skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered blocks.
Then the following are equivalent:

(1)

There exists a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map
\varphi\colon{\partial P}\to{\partial P}^{\prime}
.

(2)

There is a gluing map
\varphi\colon{\partial P}\to{\partial P}^{\prime}
.

(3)

There is a pairing of boundary components of
P
and
P^{\prime}
containing the same number of periodic orbits.

Recall that
\varphi\colon{\partial P}\to{\partial P}^{\prime}
is a gluing map if
\varphi
maps the oriented periodic orbits of
X
in
{\partial P}
to the oriented periodic orbits of
X^{\prime}
in
{\partial P}^{\prime}
and the exit boundary
P^{\mathrm{out}}
to the entrance boundary
P^{\prime\mathrm{in}}
.
The condition of Item
3
means that
P
and
P^{\prime}
have the same number of boundary components, denoted
T_{1},\dots,T_{n}
and
T_{1}^{\prime},\dots,T_{n}^{\prime}
, and for each
i=1,\dots,n
we require that there are as many periodic orbits of
X
in
T_{i}
as there are periodic orbits of
X^{\prime}
in
T_{i}^{\prime}
.

Figure 93
.
An alternating elementary lamination and a pair of strongly quasi-transverse alternating elementary laminations

Proof.

Denote
P^{\mathrm{in}}
and
P^{\mathrm{out}}
the entrance and exit boundary of
(P,X)
,
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
the periodic orbits of
X
contained in
{\partial P}
, and
{\mathcal{L}}
the boundary lamination of
(P,X)
.
Similarly, denote
P^{\prime\mathrm{in}}
and
P^{\prime\mathrm{out}}
the entrance and exit boundary of
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
,
{\mathcal{O}}^{\prime}_{*}
the periodic orbits of
X^{\prime}
contained in
{\partial P}^{\prime}
, and
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
the boundary lamination of
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
.

It is clear that
\ref{lem: cns, it: sqt}\Rightarrow\penalty\ \ref{lem: cns, it: gluing map}\Rightarrow\penalty\ \ref{lem: cns, it: pair}
.
Conversely, suppose we have a pairing of boundary components
\{(T_{i},T^{\prime}_{i})\}
of
P
and
P^{\prime}
respectively, containing the same number of periodic orbits.
According to Lemma
11.1.1
, the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
on
T_{i}
and
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
on
T_{i}^{\prime}
are elementary alternating, and according to the matching conditions, they have the same number of marked leaves.
Two such laminations can be glued together in a strongly quasi-transverse way (Figure
93
), and we have for each
i=1,\dots,n
the existence of a diffeomorphism
f_{i}\colon T_{i}\to T^{\prime}_{i}
which maps the oriented periodic orbits to the oriented periodic orbits, the exit boundary
P^{\mathrm{out}}
to the entrance boundary
P^{\prime\mathrm{in}}
, and the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
to a strongly quasi-transverse lamination at
{\mathcal{L}}^{\prime}
.
The product
\varphi\colon\partial P\to\partial P^{\prime}
thus defines a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map of building blocks.

∎

11.3.
Proof of Theorem
H

We will need the following general lemma.

Lemma 11.3.1
.

Let
(P,X)
be a building block and
\varphi
be a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map of
(P,X)
.
Let
T
be a boundary component which contains a nonzero number of periodic orbits of
X
.
Then for any Dehn twist
\tau\colon T\to T
along the periodic orbits, there exists a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map isotopic to
\varphi\circ\tau
.

Proof.

We refer to the end of the proof of Proposition
2.1.16
, which shows that we can choose the Dehn twist
\tau
in a well-chosen support so that
\varphi\circ\tau
is still a gluing map strongly quasi-transverse.
Let us briefly recall the idea.
Denote
{\mathcal{L}}
the boundary lamination and
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
the collection of periodic orbits contained in
T
.
It suffices to choose the support of
\tau
in a compact annulus of
T{\smallsetminus}{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
so that the composition is still a gluing map, in other words maps the oriented periodic orbits on the oriented periodic orbits and the exit boundary on the entrance boundary.
We then choose a small neighborhood
{\mathcal{V}}
of a periodic orbit
{\mathcal{O}}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, in which the holonomy of the lamination
{\mathcal{L}}
along a transversal of
{\mathcal{O}}
is contracting on
P^{\mathrm{out}}
and expanding on
P^{\mathrm{in}}
, and the holonomy of the lamination
\varphi^{-1}_{*}{\mathcal{L}}
is expanding on
P^{\mathrm{in}}
and contracting on
P^{\mathrm{out}}
.
Following the sign of the Dehn twist, it suffices to choose the support of
\tau
in a compact annulus contained either in
P^{\mathrm{in}}\cap{\mathcal{V}}
or in
P^{\mathrm{out}}\cap{\mathcal{V}}
, so that
\tau_{*}(\varphi^{-1}_{*}{\mathcal{L}})
is (strongly) transverse to
{\mathcal{L}}
.
∎

Proof of Theorem
6
.

Let
(P,X)
and
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
be two skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov blocks and
\varphi\colon{\partial P}\to{\partial P}^{\prime}
a diffeomorphism which maps the oriented periodic orbits of
X
in
{\partial P}
to the oriented periodic orbits of
X^{\prime}
in
{\partial P}^{\prime}
and the exit boundary
P^{\mathrm{out}}
to the entrance boundary
P^{\prime\mathrm{in}}
.
It follows from Lemma
11.2.1
that there exists a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map
\psi\colon{\partial P}\to{\partial P}^{\prime}
.
The gluing map
\psi
defines the same pairing of boundary components as
\varphi
.
Let
T_{i}
and
T^{\prime}_{i}
be two boundary components of
{\partial P}
and
{\partial P}^{\prime}
matched by
\varphi
and
\psi
.
Since
\varphi({\mathcal{O}}_{*})=\psi({\mathcal{O}}_{*})={\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, it follows that the isotopy class of
\varphi
and
\psi
differ by a Dehn twist
\tau\colon T_{i}\to T_{i}
along the periodic orbits
{\mathcal{O}}_{*}
, in other words such that
\psi\circ\tau
and
\varphi
are isotopic.
According to Lemma
11.3.1
,
\psi\circ\tau
is isotopic to a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map
\psi^{\prime}\colon{\partial P}\to{\partial P}^{\prime}
, which is then isotopic to
\varphi
.

We consider the triple
(P\cup P^{\prime},X\cup X^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
.
The block
(P\cup P^{\prime},X\cup X^{\prime})
is a filled block according to Lemma
11.1.1
, and
\psi^{\prime}
is a strongly quasi-transverse gluing map.
Up to a strong triple isotopy, we can apply the Gluing Theorem
1
and the vector fields
X
and
X^{\prime}
induce on the closed manifold
{\mathcal{M}}:=P\cup P^{\prime}/\psi^{\prime}
a vector field
Z
which is Anosov.
It satisfies Theorem
6
by construction.
It is easy to check with the criterion of Proposition
6.4.2
that if the blocks
(P,X)
and
(P^{\prime},X^{\prime})
are both transitive, the vector field
Z
thus obtained is transitive.
∎

Figure 94
.
Gluing operation of two skewed
{\mathbb{R}}
-covered Anosov blocks
